# Cairnfold design

Cairnfold is a cozy building toy for VR, inspired by Tiny Glade: no goals, no
failure, just a meadow and tools that turn rough strokes into stone walls, paths,
houses and gardens that look hand-made. You draw in the space around you, stand in
what you built at life size, or teleport into the air and float above it like a
diorama.

It is a clean-room game. Every asset is generated by code in this repository or
authored for it, apart from a few CC0 animal calls, sounds and models whose sources AGENTS.md lists; nothing comes from Tiny Glade's files, and nothing here is derived
from inspecting them. The "how the original does it" column below summarises what
its developers have said publicly (sources at the end), so we know what feel we are
aiming at; our implementation is our own.

## Requirements

- Standalone on the Steam Frame, from Steam as an Android depot run by Lepton (see
  "Steam Frame route") or as the arm64 Linux export from a library shortcut, and on
  Quest 2 and later as a sideloaded APK (`scripts/android.sh quest`). Every release
  (`scripts/release.sh`) carries every build.
- One graphics configuration for every headset: a ladder from full quality down
  that each headset walks by its own GPU time, settling on the prettiest step it
  can hold (see "Automatic quality"). No per-device quality tiers, no settings
  menu.
- Hold the headset's refresh rate at all times, whatever is built. A frame that
  misses vsync in VR is felt, not seen; every feature is measured at worst-case
  density before it lands (see "Frame budget").
- Original assets, generated procedurally wherever possible. Human avatar motion
  prefers licensed human-made recordings (`LICENSE-CMU`) to invented poses; a
  preference, not a gate. Animals move by animation made here, procedural or not.

  Directive (verbatim): "if we removed bot-made animations from animals because of our bot-made animation rule, we can add them back. that rule applies to *human* posing. and it please treat it as a suggestion, not a rule. I seem to recall seeing some good wing flits on ducks before."
- One input mapping that works on any OpenXR controller with a stick, trigger,
  grip and two face buttons.
- No text, with one exception: the player's name in the session picker (the
  figures on the friends dial, docs/multiplayer.md "Finding each other"). Nothing else gets text
  under this exception.

  Directive (verbatim): "in the multiplayer menu... I don't expect this to be solved now, but it was difficult to determine which... session to join... just by the avatar. I think eventually we'll need to break my rule about having no text. And we'll need to... I guess for Steam works, we'll need to display the... user profile... next to their avatar and their name"

## Engine and target

**Decision: Godot 4.6 (Mobile renderer, OpenXR), running standalone on the Frame.**

Two options were considered:

| Option | What it costs | Evidence |
| --- | --- | --- |
| A. Standalone on the Frame (Snapdragon-class SoC, Adreno GPU) | A mobile GPU budget: forward/tiled rendering, no screen-space GI, careful overdraw | Measured: see "Frame measurements" below. Godot's official aarch64 Linux build runs on the Frame's SteamOS and drives SteamVR's OpenXR runtime directly |
| B. Rendered on a desktop PC, streamed to the Frame | A desktop GPU budget, but every frame crosses Wi-Fi: encode, transmit, decode, reproject | Desktop-to-Frame streaming sessions measured on our setup show inconsistent delivered frame rates and stalls; that path is being reworked separately. A game that must hold refresh cannot depend on it today |

Standalone wins on the requirement that matters most, a steady refresh rate, and
it removes a PC from the loop. The look is designed for the mobile budget
(below). If streaming becomes reliable, the same Godot project can switch to the
Forward+ renderer on a PC with no code change, which would unlock heavier lighting
as an optional tier.

Godot rather than a custom Rust/Vulkan engine: OpenXR, multiview stereo,
instancing, shadows and fog come built in, the official binary runs on aarch64
Linux, and GDScript reloads without a compile step. Generators are plain data code
(`WallLayout` builds stone transforms, nodes only render them), so a hot generator
can move to a GDExtension later without touching the rest.

### Steam Frame route

**Decision: the Frame gets Cairnfold from Steam as an Android depot,
`scripts/android.sh lepton`, run by the Frame's Lepton container.** It is the only
Frame route Steamworks documents (a depot with its OS set to Android and a top-level
`.apk`); its depot settings name no ARM Linux option. The APK uses the OpenXR vendors
plugin's Khronos flavor, so no Meta SDK material is in it (docs/package-notices.md).
The arm64 Linux export stays for direct installs.

Both builds were measured on the Frame from one revision, one stress tour
(`demo/stress.json`), unworn, at 1728×1728 per eye and 72 Hz. Both ran on the same
Turnip Adreno 750 driver and SteamVR OpenXR 2.18.2, in the order native, Lepton,
native. GPU time is per frame, the median of each phase's seconds, from Godot's
viewport GPU timer in every column.

| | Native arm64, run 1 | Native arm64, run 2 | Lepton APK |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Phases holding 72 Hz, of 39 | 18 | 17 | 21 |
| GPU ms, flora/aerial | 10.83 | 10.82 | 7.43 |
| GPU ms, r15/aerial (walls) | 18.36 | 20.14 | 15.38 |
| GPU ms, hills/aerial | 29.26 | 34.78 | 25.42 |
| Lepton ÷ native GPU time, median over phases | | | 0.84 (vs run 1), 0.77 (vs run 2) |

The APK ran between the two native runs and beat both, so drift over the session
does not explain the gap; the native runs themselves differ by up to 19% on the
heaviest phases. Its cause was not measured. Both builds rendered at the same size, through the same driver and
runtime, with only the fossilize Vulkan layer loaded. Over budget, the APK and
the second native run dropped to whole fractions of the refresh rate (36 or 24 fps),
and the first native run to its GPU-bound rate (31 to 67 fps); either way frames are missed. Launched from a
Steam library entry through the Lepton compatibility tool, the APK opens its XR
session at 72 Hz and holds it on the opening scene: 73 fps, 4.7 ms of GPU time, no late frames.

Inside Lepton, `metrics.json` has no `gpu_ms`, because the in-game DRM reading
finds nothing there. The Lepton figures above are the viewport GPU timer, which
`Quality` steps by and which works there. Phase by phase, it agrees with the
same DRM counters read from the host (median difference 0.8%, at most 7.6%). That host reading matched the in-game one
over a native run: 457.3 s against 456.5 s.

### Frame measurements

Explicit `--metrics PATH` writes a benchmark record to that path: per second, frame-time
percentiles, frames over 1.5× the refresh period, and `gpu_ms`, the GPU time the
game process used in that second (summed `drm-engine-*` time from the kernel's
fdinfo, so it is real on the device whether or not the runtime reports any).
`info` records the render-target size, the render scale and each eye's field of
view. Ordinary play writes no session telemetry unless explicitly opted in (see
"Local play-session history" below).

`info.eye_gaze` records whether the runtime tracks the eyes for the game
(XR_EXT_eye_gaze_interaction: the Steam Frame's SteamVR does; the Quest 2 has no eye
cameras). The rig's `Gaze` node follows the `/user/eyes_ext` tracker. Nothing in play
uses it ("Detail by gaze"); `demo/gaze.json` shows a debug gaze ray and logs the gaze pose twice a
second, which reads untracked while the headset is off the head.

Off the head SteamVR puts the headset in standby and, by default
(`power.pauseCompositorOnStandby`), pauses the game: about 0.3 ms of GPU time a
frame, every frame on vsync, nothing drawn, so such a run measures nothing. With
that setting off, set live through SteamVR's settings interface rather than by a
SteamVR restart, the unworn headset renders the game through its own XR session and
compositor at full cost; `scripts/bench.sh`'s Frame mode measures that way. Its
times are the headset's render path: the runtime's render target, both eyes, the
foveation layer and the compositor. They stand in for a worn session's game GPU
time, short of what only a worn session runs: eye tracking and the passthrough
cameras, whose load on the shared SoC is not in these numbers, and a tracked gaze,
so foveation follows no eye unworn. A run with the headset worn (Godot 4.5.1 Mobile
renderer, Turnip Adreno 750, SteamVR 2.18.2, 1728×1728 per eye, 4× MSAA, one drawn
wall, before the flora stage) rendered at 27.78 ms per frame, every other 72 Hz
refresh, with about 16 ms of GPU time per frame.

A 28-minute worn session building a garden on 8c0a52a (before the 0.9 resolution step,
the governor's line and the worker-thread lays) wrote 1,662 seconds of `metrics.json`.
Worn, the viewport's GPU timer reads 0.15 ms under fdinfo's (median; correlation 0.999),
so the governor's signal holds on the head. The runtime still halved the rate whenever
the GPU time per frame neared the period, and the time followed the primitives drawn:

| Primitives a second's frames drew | Seconds | Viewport GPU per frame (median) | Seconds at half rate |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| under 0.6 M | 202 | 6.0 ms | 0% |
| 0.6–0.9 M | 504 | 8.3 ms | 7% |
| 0.9–1.3 M | 759 | 13.6 ms | 58% |
| 1.3–2 M | 115 | 15.6 ms | 74% |
| 2–2.5 M (the finished garden) | 81 | 23.1 ms | 99% |

Over the session 637 seconds ran at half rate and 987 at 72 Hz; the quality ladder at its
bottom step did not bring the finished garden under the period. Holding the refresh rate
at a finished garden's density is open work. The first of each run of late frames is logged
as it happens (`metrics: late frame`), with the time the frame before it took in
scripts and on the GPU, between the script steps around it.

The runtime's recommended render target is 1728×1728 per eye on a 2160×2160 panel,
and Valve's foveation layer (`VK_LAYER_VALVE_fdm_injection`) lowers shading density
outside the gaze in every frame. The ladder's swapchain rungs set the render target relative
to the runtime's recommendation (see "One ladder for every headset").

`demo/headset.json` unworn with the standby pause off, rendering (about 8 ms of
GPU time per frame), first three phases:

| Phase | Seconds | Frame time p99 | Frames over 1.5× the refresh period |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Meadow idle | 18 | 14.29 ms | 0 after the session's first second |
| Drawing a wall (live rebuild) | 18 | 14.29 ms | 0 |
| Floating 6 m up, air teleport and a capture | 47 | 14.29 ms | 0 |

A 142 ms frame once seen at the air teleport was the script's capture just before
it, which encoded its PNG on the main thread; captures now save on a worker
thread, as the camera's photos do.

A painted stroke grows its plants over the frames after its command, within 1.5 ms of
script a frame. Grown in the frame its command applied in, the flora phase's 4 s grass
stroke (about 850 plants) took 24 ms of main-thread script on the Frame and one late frame
at every release; a full stroke of 4000 would take about 110 ms. Its spots are still laid out
as the command applies: 7.4 ms for a full stroke of 4000 on the Frame, with no late frame. The flora phase (flowers,
grass, two trees, ivy on the wall), unworn with the standby pause off:

| Run | Flora phase: late frames, frame time p99 |
| --- | --- |
| Before (d9f8ec7), two runs | 1 (22 ms), 1 (28 ms) |
| After | 0, 13.89 ms |

A command that takes more than 4 ms to apply is logged (`world: applying path took 11.8 ms`):
the late-frame line's script time leaves out what a demo script's driver runs, and it showed 3 ms
for the frame the stroke cost 24 ms in.

Building lays nothing on the main thread. A wall settling after a command, a roof being
pulled and a wall top changing are laid on a worker thread while the stones standing stay in
view; the garden's copy of a wall in hand takes the stones the player's preview already laid
when they were laid from the same inputs. Each edit now costs 1 to 3 ms of main-thread script
(20 to 30 ms before, headless). The houses phase's edits (a tower, a house, two roofs reshaped,
a wall top changed), each followed by 10 s, with the ladder held at its first step, then at
full 3D resolution:

| Build | Frames over 1.5× the refresh period |
| --- | --- |
| Before (8de66b6) | 72 (tower 1, roofs 37 and 33, top 1) |
| After, four runs | 0 each |

Governed, the late frames left in the houses phase come from GPU time, not the edits. The
tower, the house and their ivy take full quality to 11.5 to 11.8 ms a frame on the governor's
timer for two to three seconds while the house is still being carried. That timer reads about
0.3 ms under the fdinfo time, so this sits at the runtime's 0.88 line (12.2 ms) but not over
the governor's old line at 0.85 (11.8 ms), and the runtime halved the rate before the governor
stepped down. The line is now 0.80 of the period:

| Line | Late frames in the houses phase |
| --- | --- |
| 0.85 (e988801), the phase up to its capture | 37, 3, 98: the runtime at every other refresh for up to 4 s |
| 0.80, the phase up to its capture | 1, 1, 1 |
| 0.80, the whole of `demo/headset.json` | 34, 1 |

Every one of those at 0.80 followed the ladder's step to 0.9 resolution itself, which the
governor took before the runtime's line: switching to 0.9 cost a 25 to 72 ms frame now and
then, and once that frame took the runtime to every other refresh for half a second (the 34).
The Frame now renders at 0.9 from launch, and only the ladder's last step, below every other,
changes the resolution (see "Which knobs matter" and "Holding 72 Hz on the Frame").

`demo/headset.json` on 5a0292a against the same build held at 0.9 from launch, three runs each,
late frames (over 1.5× the refresh period) and GPU time per frame (fdinfo):

| Phase | Stepping down to 0.9 | Held at 0.9 |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Houses | 34, 2, 34; 9.65 ms | 0, 0, 0; 9.6 to 9.7 ms |
| Erase | 0, 0, 0 | 0, 0, 0 |
An earlier pair of runs with the line at 0.82 gave 1 and 75 late frames; they were not split
by cause.
`scripts/bench.sh` with `CAIRNFOLD_SCENE=demo/headset.json` runs this check on the Frame; its
`late` column counts every frame of a phase.

### Foveation on the Frame

On 2026-10-22, build `6df1dc9c`, the Frame's SteamVR OpenXR runtime advertised
`XR_EXT_eye_gaze_interaction` (version 2), `XR_FB_foveation` (1),
`XR_FB_foveation_configuration` (1), `XR_FB_foveation_vulkan` (1),
`XR_META_foveation_eye_tracked` (1) and `XR_META_vulkan_swapchain_create_info` (1).
These came from `xrEnumerateInstanceExtensionProperties` on the headset's
`/opt/steamvr/bin/linuxarm64/libopenxr_loader.so`, with the same list when Valve's
injection layer was disabled. This supersedes the assumption that this runtime
has no FB foveation. The full lists and loader logs are job 9323's artifacts.

The implicit layer manifest
`/usr/share/openxr/1/api_layers/implicit.d/XrApiLayer_VALVE_fdm_injection.json`
names `DISABLE_VULKAN_FDM_INJECTION_LAYER`; setting it to `1` for the game process
makes the loader log that the layer is disabled. Unset, the log confirms loading
`XR_APILAYER_VALVE_fdm_injection`. No global runtime setting was changed for that A/B.

Four fresh processes ran layer on/off/off/on through `frame-unworn`, at the fixed
calibration scale 0.70, with MSAA **4x** in every measured phase. Each repeated the
air and standing views from `demo/antialias.json`, four alternating AB/BA pairs per
view, in the seed-25 meadow. The existing driver's `foveation: 0` selects VRS disabled
and FB level 0; `foveation: 3` selects `VRS_XR` and FB level 3. Thus this compares
those complete states, not VRS alone at a constant FB level. Each entry is the median
of 20 one-second viewport GPU samples (five per phase after discarding its first second):

| Layer / run order | Air, VRS off | Air, XR VRS / level 3 | Standing, VRS off | Standing, XR VRS / level 3 |
| --- | ---: | ---: | ---: | ---: |
| on0 | 3.303 ms | 3.835 ms | 3.232 ms | 3.623 ms |
| off1 | 3.427 ms | 3.881 ms | 3.245 ms | 3.617 ms |
| off2 | 3.407 ms | 3.873 ms | 3.206 ms | 3.620 ms |
| on3 | 3.412 ms | 3.882 ms | 3.202 ms | 3.624 ms |

The [record](../perf/frame/foveation/20261022T211737Z-6df1dc9.json) preserves the
samples. XR VRS / level 3 added 0.454–0.532 ms in the air and 0.372–0.422 ms standing
within these runs. Layer on/off ranges overlap; this experiment establishes no
repeatable saving from disabling the layer. These are whole viewport costs, not
an attribution of the remaining GPU work. Stock clocks, the small meadow and
0.70 scale limit generalization to other loads and full-resolution play.

Godot 4.6's `OpenXRInterface::get_vrs_texture` first returns a valid runtime density
map; only without one does it generate its own texture from eye focus.
`OpenXRFBFoveationExtension::_update_profile_rt` chains the eye-tracked profile when
the runtime reports the required support. An advertised extension or an unworn run
alone does not prove that the gaze follows correctly in the image. The measurements
therefore do not establish two independent, stacked gaze maps.

No rendering default changes on this evidence. Fixed foveation remains load-driven
in the shared quality controller; any eye-tracked path must use capabilities, remain
outside that ladder, and avoid headset-model or build checks. The worn same-view,
gaze-following pair is carried by job 9454, parked on `hand:frame-foveation-pair`;
MSAA stays 4x. The current compositor screenshot restriction to unworn captures
must be respected when arranging its worn evidence method. No screenshot ran during
these timings. All four processes exited 0 and `frame-unworn` restored
`steamvr.vrsettings` before the TV lease was released.

### Quest 2 measurements

The Quest 2 (XR2 Gen 1, Adreno 650) is the weakest headset and sets the one
graphics configuration: 13.9 ms per frame at its 72 Hz. Its runtime prints a line
a second to logcat (`VrApi`): `FPS=` delivered/refresh and `App=`, the game's GPU
time per frame. With the proximity sensor held closed
(`adb shell am broadcast -a com.oculus.vrpowermanager.prox_close`) the headset
renders the game off the head, so those numbers are real; `--script` and the other
game arguments reach it through `launch.args` in its external files folder, one a
line, since Godot drops a launch intent's arguments on an exported activity. Off the head, with no floor known, the tracked head sits at ground level, so
a script lifts the view with `place`'s `lift`. Godot 4.5.1 Mobile renderer, 4×
MSAA, the runtime's default render size, median of 20 seconds after two walls are
drawn.

How the configuration was found, with the eye at ground level looking at the
walls:

| Configuration | GPU per frame | Frames per second |
| --- | --- | --- |
| As first built, the Frame's settings | 32.1 ms | 29 |
| Glow off | 22.6 ms | 42 |
| Grass clump mesh indexed (63 to 35 vertices) | 18.2 ms | 51 |
| The meadow's plants baked into 16 m cells the camera culls | 16.8 ms | 56 |
| Grass drawn to 28 m, shrinking into the ground from 20 m | 13.4 ms | 70 |
| Flower and leaf meshes indexed | 13.1 ms | 71 |
| Grass drawn to 24 m | 12.4 ms | 72 |

Single changes from the first row, for scale: shadows off saved 2.6 ms, fog off
1.4 ms, MSAA off 6.3 ms, render scale 0.8 (with glow off) 1.6 ms; foveation level 3
applied (`Fov=3`) and saved nothing. The GPU is bound by grass vertices and by
glow's extra passes, not by pixels.

The configuration as it stands:

| View | GPU per frame | Frames per second |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Standing (1.65 m) at the walls | 11.8 ms | 72 |
| Floating 8.5 m above them, looking down over the meadow | 15.9 ms | 58 |

Floating misses. What it costs there: the grass 3.2 ms, trees and bushes 2.1 ms,
shadows 2.0 ms, flowers 1.0 ms; one shadow split instead of two, or 30 m of shadow
instead of 45 m, saved nothing.

The animals on the stress scene (`bench.sh OUT --android APK`, the ladder at its top, median of each
phase; frames are over the then 12.5 ms budget everywhere at the top, the bare meadow standing included at
26.5 ms):

| Phase | GPU per frame | Draw calls | Primitives |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `meadow/ground` | 26.5 ms | 114 | 369,126 |
| `sheep/ground` | 41.7 ms | 310 | 1,107,236 |
| `deer/ground` | 31.1 ms | 157 | 944,983 |
| `fox/ground` | 31.1 ms | 162 | 949,479 |

The deer and fox views look over the meadow's edge at the trees, which take the rest of their time.
What the animals themselves cost, on the meadow alone, the same view with them shown and then hidden,
twice each (GPU ms a frame):

| View | Shown | Hidden | Difference |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Deer, first | 32.25 | 32.50 | -0.25 |
| Deer, second | 33.80 | 33.83 | -0.03 |
| Fox, first | 31.50 | 31.33 | +0.17 |
| Fox, second | 33.22 | 32.67 | +0.55 |

They cost 2 to 3 draw calls and 2,000 to 6,000 primitives in view, within the run's half-millisecond
noise, so nothing about them is made cheaper.

The fish's feeding frenzy, from one stress-scene run with the 0.7 swapchain
(`perf/quest-2/stress/20261019T153539Z-16f670e.json`): `fish-frenzy/ground` looks down on the pond at
radius 14 a second after three handfuls land on it, with its fish feeding, and `deer/ground`, the next phase,
draws nearly the same frame with the pond calm:

| Phase | GPU per frame | Frame p50 | Draw calls | Primitives |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `ponds/ground` | 25.37 ms | 28.06 ms | 189 | 789,916 |
| `sheep/ground` | 30.31 ms | 32.83 ms | 212 | 809,936 |
| `fish-frenzy/ground` | 25.38 ms | 27.78 ms | 177 | 758,434 |
| `deer/ground` | 25.18 ms | 27.72 ms | 177 | 758,184 |

The frenzy reads +0.01 ms against `ponds/ground`, -4.93 ms against `sheep/ground` and +0.20 ms against
the calm `deer/ground` view, inside the 0.6 ms spread of the pond phases with nothing added
(`ponds`, `ducks`, `conures` on the ground: 25.37 to 25.98 ms). The record before it
(`20261019T071201Z-6f3dc2d.json`, the full-size swapchain) read the same order: 41.82 ms against 44.81 ms
and 44.87 ms. The frenzy costs nothing measurable, so `Fish.tick` is left as it is.

### Another headset is no stand-in for the Quest 2

A Quest 2 figure comes from a Quest 2. One Quest APK was run on a Quest 2 and on a
Quest 3, both at the Quest 2's fit step (`{"op": "quality", "step": "fit"}`, which
at the time resolved an Android headset to the Quest 2's), through `demo/costs.json` and the stress scene with
every `quality` op set to `fit`. Each prediction below is held out: the phase's family
is left out of the fit, or, for the stress scene, the whole scene is.

| Estimate from the Quest 3 (`App=` GPU ms) | Foliage | Masonry | Water | p90 error, all |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Build cost over the bare meadow, per-category ratio | 2.76 | 1.10 | 0.90 | 1.60 |
| Stress-scene frame, calibrated on the costs scene | 8.24 | 27.63 | 9.74 | 21.83 |
| Frame, a local curve over both scenes | 2.44 | 5.85 | 4.32 | 4.48 |

The room for a garden's builds on the Quest 2 is `Budget.line_ms()` less the meadow's base cost, about 2 ms, so an
estimator would need a p90 error near 0.25 ms, an eighth of that room, to say whether a change fits. None of these
comes close. The ratio is not one number. Foliage costs the Quest 2 1.7 to 10 times what it
costs the Quest 3, masonry mostly 0.7 to 1.7 times, and wildlife 4 to 5 times. It also
grows with load. Light phases run 1.0 to 1.1 times, and the heavy village views run 2.8
times (19.3 ms against 54.6 ms). Two identical Quest 3 runs differ by 0.3 ms median and
1.06 ms p90 on a build's cost, and the Quest 2's slope multiplies that noise. CPU time
does not correlate at all: the slope between the two headsets' build costs is 0.1. The
Frame's own fitted table, the former Frame table (at its own fit step), prices the same units between 1.8 and 10.7
times below the former Quest 2 table (easel against thatch), so it fails the same way.

A Quest 3 run still orders phases that differ by a millisecond or more on the Quest 2
(92 to 95 per cent of such pairs agree), so it can screen a large change. Any number
that is reported as Quest 2 milliseconds, written to the former Quest 2 table, or compared with
the budget is measured on a Quest 2.

## Frame budget

Directive (verbatim): "The-the only reason I could see for adding a resource to this
game... is to represent... frame budget. Cause I don't want the game getting bogged
down when someone makes some big build. I don't want it to be possible to make a
build that... bogs down the frame rate"

Directive (verbatim): "...through the whole course of this game, we'll need a heavy
focus on... making it run fast, because it's in VR. That's important"

So speed is a standing focus: every feature holds the refresh rate at worst-case
density, and no build the player can make may bring the frame rate down.

- `game/src/budget.gd` holds the budget: the refresh rate of the weakest headset,
  and `Budget.line_ms()`, the GPU time a frame may take there: the governor's own line (`Quality.Governor.LINE`,
  0.8 of the period), 11.1 ms at 72 Hz, so the bench holds a phase to the line the running game holds. Nothing else
  states it. Passages and records from before held Quest phases to 0.9 of the period, 12.5 ms; their verdicts
  below are against that then line.
- `demo/stress.json` is the worst case: the bare meadow, then every flora pool full,
  a 27 m wall drawn live, walls 2 m apart (the closest `WallPlan` lets them stand)
  over discs of 10 to 25 m radius at 4.5 m tall, 9 m walls, a village of houses
  two storeys high, every other one turned a quarter, each crossed by a wing, tiled and then thatched, eight ponds, and the most ducks there can be at them (`bench.sh`
  fixes the seed) and the most sheep at four fences between them (seen too from the ground by a fence), the deer and the fox each seen from a person's height, with every butterfly there can be
  over the full flower pool and the most conures there can be among the full pool of planted trees,
  every firefly there can be over the ponds at dusk, and a hill pulled up live under the walls the build budget
  leaves room for beside a path, each seen from the air and standing among them, then both hands pushed into the walls
  beside the player, then two painted easels stood before them and the camera held up to them and a photo taken each
  second, each saving both paintings, then the selfie camera held out at the player, whose own avatar it draws, then
  the other players waving (`avatars/wave`). It plays governed, the ladder walking as it does in play. Its feature
  phases stand on gardens the build budget admits, built bounded as a player builds; its pricing gardens (the wall
  discs, villages and roof families) are built unbounded past the budget, which no player can do, and price what
  such a garden draws.
- `scripts/bench.sh OUT_DIR` plays it and prints, per phase, frame time, draw
  calls, primitives and GPU time; on a Quest it holds every phase to the budget and fails when one goes over. Only
  measuring are `sweep/` phases and those whose label the scene marks `past_budget` while their garden is past the
  build budget (`World.fill` over 1): a mark exempts nothing a player can build, and an unmarked phase is held
  wherever it stands ("The stress scene on the Quest 2"). A new feature adds itself to the stress scene, unmarked, on
  a garden the build budget admits.
  `CAIRNFOLD_PLAYERS=8 CAIRNFOLD_WAVE=1` fills the session with flat-screen
  companions playing the recorded wave throughout the tour, including its final
  `avatars/wave` view.
- Each run records its phases under `perf/HEADSET/SCENE/`, named by the build measured; `scripts/trend.py` prints the
  latest run of each beside the median of the three before and the best, marking a rise of a quarter and 2 ms; on a
  named scene a GPU-time rise fails it, and bench.sh runs it on the scene it recorded.
- Nothing the player does waits on a build or a compile. Every Vulkan export ships its shaders baked (`shader_baker`,
  run by `scripts/export.sh` in an editor with a RenderingDevice): a Quest 2 with its data cleared sat 52 s on its
  first frame, 34 s baked. The baker compiles only shaders it meets in exported materials, and the game makes its
  materials in code, so `game/shaders.tres` holds one of each kind a booted game makes, every `.gdshader` among them
  (`scripts/shaders.sh` rewrites it; `tests/test_shaders.gd` fails when it falls behind): with it a cleared Quest 2
  reaches its first scripted step in 17.6 to 20.0 s rather than 31.2 to 33.2 s. What a cleared headset still compiles is its driver's pipelines, which no export can ship:
  the first easel 11 s, the first house 7 s, once per install; an update keeps them. The style wheel's miniatures are
  built on a thread of their own (`ToolModels`) and the rest of the warm-ups run from one list, `Warmup`.
  `demo/first-use.json` plays each tool's first use, and `CAIRNFOLD_COLD=1 scripts/bench.sh` clears the game's data
  first, its saves kept.

What makes building cheap:

- Each wall's mortar and plaster, which stand behind its stones' faces, is an occluder (`ArrayOccluder3D`), and
  the view and every camera's view cull what those hide. Standing among walls, the ground chunks wholly behind the
  nearest walls are not drawn. On the Frame (stress scene, step 6, 09f8b53 against it with occluders, fdinfo GPU
  ms a frame): `hills/ground` 12.75 to 11.15, `hands/walls` 12.95 to 10.86, `camera/ground` 14.66 to 12.52, the
  views from the air unchanged (7.8). On a Quest 2, standing 25.5 to 24.5 and the camera 29.0 to 27.3 (`App=`),
  frame times no higher, so the CPU's occlusion pass costs no frame. Hiding the walls in those views left 4.8 ms of
  13.05: the walls' stones are what is left of the cost.
- Settled walls are drawn by `Masonry` per 8 m ground chunk: one MultiMesh per
  kind of piece per chunk, each piece in the chunk under it, so a chunk is culled
  and limited by distance by where its pieces stand, however long their wall. A
  wall being drawn or pulled draws by a `Masonry` of its own, in the same chunks with
  the same coarse far stones, until it settles into the glade's.
- A chunk wholly beyond the near distance of the quality step draws its stones
  and roof tiles with `Stones.far_mesh`, the same lumps at the corners alone: 12
  triangles to the full stone's 108 and the full tile's 48. Ivy chunks past ivy's reach are not drawn at all.
- Stones and roof tiles cast no shadow themselves; a box of 8 vertices per stone
  does. Mortar, seen only in the gaps, is a plain box. A stone's faces share the
  vertices along their borders.
- A wall's live preview is laid on a worker thread from a copy of its inputs, so a
  long stroke costs the main thread nothing while it grows.
- A vine lays one three-leaf sprig a step rather than three leaves; sprigs shrink
  away over the last 15 m of ivy's reach.
- Ground moving under walls changes neither their data nor where paths cross them,
  so a stroke only lays their stones again on a worker thread, and a wall settles
  from the preview laid on the ground as it settled. A replaced layout's stones are
  freed by the wall's next worker task: dropping thousands of them took 3 to 5 ms
  a wall. Pulling a hill up under eight of 973 m of walls, headless (CPU only),
  had frames of 22 to 58 ms at the 99th percentile and two of 117 and 133 ms as
  it settled; now no frame of the stroke or its settling passes 7.1 ms.

Two traps in measuring this. A scripted view used to last a second in the headset: the
rig's hover spring pulled it back to its rest height, so every phase "from the air" was
measured from the unworn headset's own pose on the ground; views now hold. And GPU time
read at half rate is not the scene's cost (see "Holding 72 Hz on the Frame" below): rows at
a frame time of 27.8 ms or more compare only roughly.

Where the time went among walls on a 25 m disc, measured by hiding each part in turn
within one run at the ladder's top, before the wall changes, from the unworn headset's own
pose: on the ground, facing a wall a few metres off:

- Stones: their triangles. Every pixel the same, 3 segments a face to 2 saved 4.6 ms and
  to 1 (12 triangles) saved 10.2 ms; sharing the vertices along the faces' borders (56
  instead of 96, the same surface) saved nothing measurable. The cost follows the
  triangles each stone runs, not its vertices or its pixels.
- Ivy: 5 to 9 ms, its far leaves shrunk to nothing but
  still drawn, because a whole wall's ivy sat in the one chunk under the wall's centre.
- The sun's shadows of the walls: about 3.6 ms; the shadow boxes alone 0.7 ms once they
  were boxes of 8 vertices.
- Flora, terrain and the meadow's shadows: the rest, 9 to 14 ms.

So settled pieces now go to the chunk under each, far chunks draw 12-triangle stones,
ivy chunks past ivy's reach are not drawn, and both distances are on the quality ladder
("Automatic quality"). From the air the walls cost about a third less; standing among
them, where the stones are near, about the same.

At the ladder's bottom step, a garden as built in play (`demo/stress.json`'s garden
phase: about 200 m of walls, two houses, forty planted trees, hills), every part hidden in
turn at 72 Hz, from the air: walls 2.5 ms, the sun's shadows 2.0 ms, terrain 1.6 ms,
trees 0.6 ms, ivy 0.2 ms; a 3D resolution of 0.7 instead of 0.9 saved 2.4 ms. Standing:
walls 0.8 ms, shadows 1.3 ms, terrain 0.5 ms.

What is left grows with what is built, so building needs a bound: "Build budget" below.

Measured on a Quest 2 (`CAIRNFOLD_SCENE=demo/costs.json bench.sh OUT --android APK` on
ecef026: in the headset with its proximity sensor held closed, the runtime's `App=` GPU time
per frame, median of each phase, CPU and GPU held at level 4, 525 MHz, the ladder at its
bottom step, no other process rendering). GPU milliseconds a frame against the then 12.5 ms budget;
every phase ran at half rate, 36 fps:

| Phase | From the air | Standing |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Bare meadow, its own flora cleared | 18.7 | 17.5 |
| Meadow | 21.2 | 20.2 |
| Grass pool full | 21.3 | 22.9 |
| Flower pool full | 19.6 | 20.8 |
| Tree pool full | 23.7 | 21.4 |
| Walls, 10 m disc | 22.8 | 28.1 |
| Walls, 15 m disc | 26.6 | 29.3 |
| Walls, 20 m disc | 32.8 | 31.3 |
| Plaster walls, 15 m disc | 22.1 | 25.6 |
| Fences, 15 m disc | 20.3 | 20.1 |
| Eight ponds | 20.8 | 18.0 |
| Village, 12 m disc | 28.1 | 20.5 |
| Lanterns on the 10 m disc's walls | 23.3 | 31.1 |

The meadow alone, from the air (22 m up) and standing, at the ladder's top and bottom
steps (`App=` ms):

| Build | Top, air | Top, standing | Bottom, air | Bottom, standing |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 48029e0 | 27.7 | 22.7 | 21.0 | 18.1 |
| fdb33cb | 41.1 | 27.1 | 28.7 | 23.8 |
| ecef026 | 33.4 | 23.8 | 20.9 | 20.1 |

An earlier table here read the meadow at 14.2 and 12.0 ms on 48029e0 at the top step. That
build still reads so on the Quest 2 (14.1 and 12.7) while the Meta plugin's dynamic
resolution runs: the runtime's `SF=` fell to 0.50 and the render region with it. With it
off, as 737a292 left it (see "Automatic quality"), the same build reads 27.7 and 22.7 at
`SF=1.00`, so the old table measured half the pixels, not a faster scene.

Between 48029e0 and fdb33cb two commits added cost at full resolution: d353166's sparse
far grass to 60 m (9 ms from the air at the top step only, the ladder's to give up) and 14753cc, whose
fragment shader branched into `ground_normal` for live humps and paid it on every ground
fragment (7.5 ms from the air, 3.5 standing, at every step; ecef026 reads that slope per
vertex). The scene renders as one 4x MSAA 1440x1584 surface and its render stage now
dominates (17 ms of it at the bottom step from the air, 1.7 ms binning), so on the Quest 2
it is fragments, not vertices, that hold 60 fps out of reach: the bare meadow alone needs
about a third less GPU time, which the measurement at `SF=0.50` says fewer pixels buy.

The walls' cost is vertex work on the GPU. Frame time follows the GPU time, the
live wall preview holds 72 Hz on the CPU (below), and draw calls stay near 150 at
every size. Dropping the stones' levels of detail (primitives at 973 m from 4.4 M to
7.1 M) cost nothing; a trial build with a plain 12-triangle box per stone (24 vertices
instead of 96) had about the primitives the levels of detail had and roughly halved
what the walls cost over the flora (measured with the headset's shell rendering
beside the game, so those runs' own times read high): what costs is the vertices each
stone instance runs, so the stone mesh is the lever.

The live wall preview, measured with the view turned to the sky so the GPU sits
idle: every second of two 5 s strokes ran 72 frames with none late, the 99th
percentile 14.3 ms as with no stroke at all.

### Holding 72 Hz on the Frame

Through the ladder the stress scene held 72 Hz on the Frame in all but ten of its phases:
9 m walls standing, the village, the path and hill strokes, the hills from the air, the camera
and a garden from the air sat at every other refresh at the ladder's bottom step for the whole
of each phase. Three causes, measured on
the Frame unworn with its standby pause off, the governor walking the ladder and each phase held
20 s:

- The runtime's half rate holds itself. Once at every other refresh, the GPU timer reads
  high by the runtime's own work in between: 9 m walls standing at the bottom step read
  11.2 ms at 72 Hz entered calm and 14.0 ms held at half rate, the same step and the same
  1.9 M primitives. The runtime let a scene back only once it read under about 11 ms (a
  path stroke sat at half rate reading 11.2 to 11.6 ms, and left it once it read 7.9), and the governor's
  line is 11.1 ms, so it sat there without stepping. Turning SteamVR's motion smoothing off
  mid-run took 2 ms off that reading and left the half rate as it was. Now the runtime
  holding the game at every other refresh counts as over budget whatever the timer reads,
  as long as the timer is above 0.6 of the budget, below which the CPU held the frame.
- The governor waited for each step to arrive before judging the next, and flowers
  receding from 60 to 20 m take five seconds: a path stroke walked from step 4 to the bottom
  over 16 s at half rate. Frames over budget now step on while a step is under way, short of a
  step that pops.
- A wall under a moving hill drew itself whole in full stones until it settled: the hill
  stroke doubled the primitives drawn (0.97 to 2.17 M) at the bottom step. It now draws by
  its own chunks, far ones in coarse stones.

What the bottom step still costs, every part hidden in turn after the scene had returned to
72 Hz (GPU ms a frame, fdinfo):

| Phase, bottom step | All | No sun shadows | 3D resolution 0.7 | No walls | No flora |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 9 m walls standing | 11.2 | 10.1 | 9.3 | 8.0 | 9.5 |
| Village standing | 11.4 | 10.3 | 9.4 | 7.9 | 9.4 |
| Village from the air | at half rate | 12.2 | at half rate | 7.8 | at half rate |
| A path stroke | 11.4 | 9.8 | 9.3 | 8.6 | 7.6 |
| Hills from the air | 11.4 | 9.8 | 9.3 | 8.6 | 7.7 |
| Camera held up | 12.0 | 10.3 | 9.9 | 8.8 | 9.8 |
| A garden from the air | at half rate | 10.2 | 9.4 | 9.6 | 7.1 |

Hiding the terrain and holding shadows to 64 m instead of 256 m each saved nothing. So the
ladder gained two steps below the old bottom: the sun's shadows off, then the 3D resolution
at 0.78 of the swapchain's (0.70 on the Frame then). Both show at once across the whole view, so
a step up out of either waits the governor's longest calm (64 s), and one undone is not taken
again that session. Ending the ladder above a step that saved nothing was tried and cut: the
village appeared in the same second the resolution step landed, read as a step that cost 1.6 ms,
and held the governor two steps up for the rest of the run. On the Quest 2 the bottom step's bare
meadow took 16.5 ms of App GPU time from the air and 14.2 standing at full swapchain size, so
the Quest 2 draws a smaller swapchain (below). Without the resolution step the village from the air stayed
at every other refresh at the shadows-off step (14.7 ms). Every step up out of them in a
governed run of the stress scene cost no late frame (the longest 18.8 ms). A garden within the
build budget never reaches them on the Frame: its `Budget.COSTS` are fitted at step 8, the last
with the sun's shadows and the full resolution (`Budget.fit_step`).

A path stroke over the walls lost frames with the GPU well inside its line: the main thread was the cost. On the
Frame the runtime then holds every other refresh for a second or more after a single frame of 20 ms or so, so one
heavy frame costs dozens. Timed on the Frame during a 48 m stroke over the stress scene's 24 walls, each relay of
the stroke cut its preview arches by walking the whole stroke against every wall (105 ms a second); laying the
stroke re-cut every wall from every path, closed and reopened their arches and wore the whole path into the ground
mask in one frame (59 ms); a wall laid again with a new arch drew every changed Masonry chunk in its landing frame
(4 to 7 ms); and the wildlife's snails and fish tested each step against every sample of every wall. Now a stroke
being drawn keeps the samples, cell grid and wall meetings of its settled points and works only on its moving end; an
index of the walls by grid cell names those it can meet; its ground wear and its arches go on alternate frames; the
laid path keeps the arches its ghost showed (the ghost gives way after the command, as a painted stroke's does);
Masonry draws changed chunks within `Budget.MASONRY_FLUSH_US` a frame and the ground wears a laid path within
`Budget.GROUND_WEAR_US`; one arch cue plays for a stroke, not one per wall; swimmers look only in the cells their step
meets; and the metrics' once-a-second GPU reading (up to 8 ms of procfs reads) runs on a thread of its own.

A visited garden past the build budget, as one came in a played co-op session (4.1 to
4.7 times the room, 23,000 stones, 130,000 to 150,000 sprigs of ivy): `demo/visited-host.json`
builds it unbounded on a headless host on the Frame itself, `demo/visited.json` joins it in
the headset (`--host-port` the host's `--port`), lets its ivy grow and stands looking across
it, the governor free, the tool wheel shut and then held open. Unworn, each phase 20 s shut and
15 s open, twice:

| Build | Shut: GPU ms, late, step | Wheel open: GPU ms, late, step |
|---|---|---|
| Before the wheel lost its own light | 11.0, 0, 7; 6.7, 0, 10 | 7.7 (13.8 max), 58, 7 to 10; 7.8, 0, 10 |
| After | 9.2, 1, 9; 10.1, 1, 8 | 9.9, 1, 9; 10.8, 1, 8 |

Before, opening the wheel lost 58 frames and drove the ladder to its bottom, with no sun
shadows and a 0.78 3D scale. After, the wheel costs 0.7 ms and the garden holds 72 Hz at
steps 8 to 9. With the ladder pinned instead, this garden reads 14.0 to 15.5 ms at steps 0
to 6 with the wheel shut, half rate: a garden this far past the budget holds the rate only
because the governor steps down for it.

What the stone walls cost the Frame is their pixels. At step 6, unworn, each kind of masonry piece hidden in turn
over `r25/ground` and `village/ground` (shown, hidden, shown; GPU ms a frame where the runtime held 72 Hz):
the stones about 4 ms, every other kind under 0.4. Halving the stones' triangles moved the time 0.1 to 0.4 ms in
those views, and dropping their three triplanar detail samples 0.1 to 0.3, inside the spread of two runs. Two
changes (3899ff1e) measured above it, paired runs of the same scene, GPU ms a frame:

| Change | `r25/ground` | `r20/ground` | `village/ground` |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Before | 11.44 | 10.02 | 11.40 |
| A rounded box carries its 2-segment lattice as a mesh LOD (`Meshes.lod_error`), scaled by the longest stone | 10.20 | 9.51 | 11.40 |
| Stones and roof tiles read the bounce and lantern volumes once a vertex (`Stones.small_material`) | 9.80 | | 10.51 |

The LOD's key is the coarse lattice's largest stray from the full one in the mesh's units, and a masonry kind's
`lod_bias` scales it by its longest piece (`WallLayout.LONGEST` for stones), so the coarse stones draw only where
they differ by under the renderer's one pixel: 2.57 M primitives became 1.34 M in `r25/ground`. Mortar, plaster,
timber, floors and boulders keep reading the volumes once a fragment: their triangles span the volumes' metre.

The same two halves on a Quest 2, paired and interleaved on one headset (parent, LOD alone, both; then both, LOD
alone, parent; LOD alone is 3899ff1e with stones and tiles back in `Stones.shared_material`, its records named
ba864ff, the others' 8adbc31 and 700f7df): `demo/stress.json` up to `hills/ground` with its quality op at step 6, runtime `App=` GPU ms a frame,
the mean of the two runs, each pair's difference in brackets (`perf/quest-2/walls6/`):

| Phase | Before (3cf05131) | LOD alone | Both (3899ff1e) | LOD | Per vertex |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `r20/ground` | 20.31 | 20.62 | 19.59 | +0.30 (+0.17, +0.44) | -1.02 (-0.65, -1.40) |
| `r25/ground` | 21.91 | 21.63 | 22.16 | -0.28 (-0.31, -0.24) | +0.53 (+1.55, -0.49) |
| `r15-tall/ground` | 19.77 | 20.45 | 21.12 | +0.67 (+0.24, +1.10) | +0.68 (+1.34, +0.02) |
| `village/ground` | 22.69 | 22.24 | 22.34 | -0.45 (-0.27, -0.63) | +0.11 (+0.04, +0.17) |
| `village/aerial` | 19.30 | 19.30 | 20.05 | +0.00 (-0.09, +0.09) | +0.76 (+0.83, +0.68) |
| `plaster/ground` | 23.25 | 22.34 | 22.00 | -0.91 (-1.60, -0.23) | -0.33 (+0.08, -0.74) |
| `hills/ground` | 23.79 | 23.23 | 23.17 | -0.56 (-1.36, +0.24) | -0.05 (+1.10, -1.21) |

Over the fifteen wall phases (30 pairs each) the LOD moved the Quest 2 -0.02 ± 0.11 ms and the per-vertex reads
-0.03 ± 0.15, where one build run twice differs by 0.70 ms (standard deviation): neither costs it, so both stay. The
LOD takes the Quest 2's primitives in `r25/ground` from 2.0 M to 1.2 M without moving its GPU time, as its frame is
fragments ("Steps 9 and 10 on the Quest 2"), and the per-vertex reads that saved the Frame 0.8 to 0.9 ms neither save
nor cost it.

The stress scene, played governed since it holds the gardens a player can build, on the Frame on b4e2a39
(`perf/frame/stress/20261025T161738Z-b4e2a39.json`, unworn through `frame-unworn`): all 140 phases other than the
sweeps within the 11.1 ms line, the dearest `r25/ground` at 9.4 ms, and every one at the 72 Hz period but
`held/house`, whose frame p50 reads 14.4 ms from a 324 ms main-thread stall as the house tool is taken, with the
GPU at 5.2 ms.

### Steps 9 and 10 on the Quest 2

The two bottom steps were built on the Frame, so they were measured on a Quest 2 too, where the
risk ran the other way: its driver pays a separate tonemap pass for any 3D scale off 1.0
("Which knobs matter"), so step 10 might cost it more than it saves and, being sticky, hold it
there. It does not. The stress scene on 88e219b8 (the Quest 2's 0.7 swapchain and 2x MSAA), the
ladder pinned at each step, runtime `App=` GPU ms a frame and `FPS=` frames a second, the
median of each phase's seconds after its first; governed, the step held in its last 15 s:

| Phase | Step 8 | Step 9 (no sun shadows) | Step 10 (0.78 3D scale) | Governed |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `village/aerial` | 55.3, 17 fps | 55.9, 17 fps | 52.9, 18 fps | 10 |
| `plaster-village/aerial` | 35.7, 23 fps | 36.0, 22 fps | 32.4, 24 fps | 10 |
| `r25/aerial` | 47.6, 20 fps | 45.4, 20 fps | 44.7, 21 fps | 10 |
| `r25/ground` | 26.0, 30 fps | 26.4, 29 fps | 23.1, 34 fps | 10 |
| `hills/stroke` | 49.8, 19 fps | 50.2, 18 fps | 46.6, 20 fps | 10 |
| `hills/aerial` | 46.4, 20 fps | 45.0, 20 fps | 42.6, 21 fps | 10 |
| `hills/ground` | 26.4, 30 fps | 26.7, 30 fps | 24.6, 32 fps | 10 |
| `hands/walls` | 26.3, 30 fps | 26.3, 31 fps | 24.7, 33 fps | 10 |
| `camera/ground` | 28.6, 29 fps | 28.2, 28 fps | 25.9, 31 fps | 10 |
| `camera/shutter` | 28.5, 28 fps | 28.4, 28 fps | 25.3, 31 fps | 10 |
| `garden/aerial` | 32.5, 25 fps | 32.8, 24 fps | 30.4, 27 fps | 10 |

Over the 57 phases in which no other process drew, step 10 read 2.9 ms under step 9 at the median
and nowhere more than 0.01 ms over it. Turning the sun's shadows off (step 9) saves the Quest 2
next to nothing, since its frame is fragments: the scaled scene loses more pixels than the
tonemap pass adds. So no rule keeps the Quest 2 off step 10, and the governed run settled there
in every heavy phase. Before the 0.7 swapchain (679f87e, 4x MSAA) it read the same way: step 10
a median 3.4 ms under step 9, and the governed run at step 10 throughout. Its two higher phases
there, gardens and lanterns from the air, were run noise: the governed run read the garden at
64.7 ms at step 10 against 71.5 pinned at step 9. None of these phases hold 72 Hz on the
Quest 2 at any step; the build budget, not the ladder, keeps a garden inside it.

Walls drawn by their own chunks while they change (03ecac0) against the build before it
(04fb074), the stress scene at its top step on the Quest 2 (4x MSAA then): draw calls and primitives
at the phase's peak, and its median GPU ms:

| Phase | Before | Per chunk |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `hills/stroke` | 542 draws, 3.35 M, 127.2 ms | 674 draws, 2.46 M, 91.2 ms |
| `hands/walls` | 320 draws, 2.78 M, 46.0 ms | 257 draws, 2.71 M, 44.1 ms |
| `paths/stroke` | 532 draws, 2.40 M, 87.0 ms | 460 draws, 2.41 M, 86.2 ms |

The hill stroke draws 132 more calls while its walls move and 36 ms less, the coarse far stones
outweighing the calls; a wall in hand draws fewer.

On the Frame, with these steps and the governor as they stand (07745685), a governed run of the
whole stress scene (each phase 20 s, unworn through `frame-unworn`) held 72 Hz in every phase its
last 15 s, the heaviest at step 10: the village from the air 8.7 ms, the r25 walls 8.0 and 8.8,
the hill stroke 8.0, the camera held up 9.6 to 10.3, the garden from the air 8.5. The two phases
standing on a wall walk through the roofs village did not: `standing/along` and `standing/across`
sat at every other refresh all 20 s at step 10, reading 13.8 and 14.8 ms with 1.28 and 1.10 M
primitives, against 8.9 ms and 0.93 M for the same village from the ground. There the scene is
past the ladder's last step, not a step that costs more than it saves.

What those two views drew on 07745685, the ladder pinned at step 10, each part hidden in turn with
the driver's `layer` op (`part/<kind>` hides one kind of masonry piece, `part/<kind>/near` or `/far`
only its whole or its coarse stones), each view entered from the sky. GPU ms a frame (fdinfo);
"half rate" marks a reading taken at every other refresh, which reads high:

| Hidden | Along | Across |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Nothing | 14.3, half rate (11.1 once at 72 Hz) | 14.9, half rate |
| Walls and roofs (`masonry`) | 4.5 | 4.9 |

| Stones drawn coarse | 10.6 | 14.3, half rate |

Stones drawn whole cost most: a chunk draws its stones whole while its centre is within the step's
4 m plus half a chunk's diagonal, so on the walk every chunk within about 11 m, the wall's own and
the houses beside it, drew 108-triangle stones (0.58 M of the 1.26 M primitives along the street).
A bottom step drawing stones whole to 0 m and ivy to 6 m held both views at 72 Hz pinned (along
9.6 ms, across 10.2) and on the Quest 2 at step 10 took them from 22.4 and 22.1 ms to 17.6 and
17.0 (`perf/quest-2/quest-standing` holds the 07745685 run). It did not land: main had meanwhile
reached 72 Hz in both views without it. A governed run of the whole stress scene on dff34d8, every
`quality` op in `demo/stress.json` set to -1 so the governor walks every phase
(`perf/frame/stress-governed`), held 72 Hz in all 140 phases; at the bottom step (16 on that ladder)
`standing/along` read 6.0 ms with 0.73 M primitives and `standing/across` 6.1 ms with 0.68 M, the
roofs village from the ground 4.6 ms. That ladder's bottom rungs draw the Quest 2's last way of
drawing (0.7 swapchain, 2x MSAA, no lake) as well as 07745685's step 10; which of the commits
between the two builds made the difference was not isolated.

Governed, the walk reads one of two figures run to run on one build, by the step the phases before it
leave the governor on. Two stress runs of the same build, back to back (`perf/frame/stress`), drew
the same 0.70 to 0.74 M primitives and 152 to 166 draws with the GPU clocked at 903 MHz throughout:
at step 16 (3D scale 0.78) `standing/along` read 6.1 ms and `standing/across` 6.0, at step 15 (the
3D scene at the swapchain's resolution, shadows still off) 8.7 and 8.4. Both hold 72 Hz. A scene of
the roofs village and the walk three times over landed on step 15 or 16 at each pass and read the
same two figures. The earlier records, 8.2 to 8.7 ms on some builds and 5.9 to 6.4 on others with
the same primitives, carried no step and match these two. Bench records now carry each phase's step
and GPU clock (`gpu_mhz`, from the driver's cycle count), and `scripts/trend.py` compares a phase only
with runs on its step.

### The stress scene on the Quest 2

What the game promises is the refresh rate for every garden a player can build. The governor walks the ladder for
that, and the build budget keeps a garden within what the ladder's bottom step holds (`Budget.fit_step`). So the bench
holds a phase to the line where the build budget admits its garden, with the governor free. Pinning the start step
(6), as the stress scene did, held every garden to a step the governor leaves as soon as the frame runs over. The
scene's pricing gardens are past the build budget: the wall discs, villages and roof families run from 1.99 to 8.78
times it (`World.fill`). On the Quest 2 no step of the ladder brings those within the line, so their phases, each
label marked `past_budget`, measure what they cost and set no verdict. The features (hands, camera, selfie, other
players, path materials, the wipe) are not pricing: they stand on gardens built bounded, at most the budget, and are
held. At the 3.99 ms capacity a hill under 26 m of walls beside a 12 m path, with the camera's two easels, fills
0.93 of the budget (the 973 m it stood under before read 8.37); a village built until the budget turns a house down,
0.96; the materials garden (cobbles, flagstones, sand, dirt and three 8 m beds), 0.93, where three cobble paths and
six 14 m beds had read 1.87 (desktop `units` lines; the fill is the garden's, the same on every headset). The other
players (`avatars/wave`, `avatars/chains`) stand on the hill garden too.

Each phase holds a view 6 s, so its median takes the frames after the governor has answered what the phase brought.
The held tools (`held/*`, fourteen of one in view) held 3 s each, and fourteen books arriving at step 4 read 11.5 ms
for the second before the governor stepped to 6 (8.5 ms), half of what that phase's median counted. Pinned at step 4
the same books read 10.2 to 10.5 ms (`App=`), within the line; baking their covers first or keeping them out of the
shadow pass moved that by 0.2 ms at most.

On a Quest 2 of the build before the refit, the features on these gardens read, governed: `hands/walls` 7.05 ms,
`camera/ground` 10.10, `camera/shutter` 9.20, `camera/recording` 10.19, `camera/selfie` 9.14, `camera/two` 9.16,
`avatars/wave` 8.48, `wipe/village` 5.79, `materials/aerial` 6.69 and `materials/ground` 7.71, all within the line;
on the hills, the timber village and the paths' old garden they had read 13.6 to 17.0, 14.1 and 12.3.

Measured on a Quest 2, the runtime's `App=` GPU ms a frame, the median of each phase's seconds after its first. The
pinned run is main's last before the change (`perf/quest-2/stress/20261024T221834Z-dff34d8.json`); the governed run
is the scene with the change, the step the median of the phase's:

| Phase | Pinned at step 6 | Governed, step |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `meadow/aerial` | 11.26 | 9.81, 10 |
| `flora/aerial` | 14.55 | 8.31, 13 |
| `flora/ground` | 13.31 | 10.70, 16 |
| `ponds/aerial` | 15.85 | 6.37, 16 |
| `ducks/ground` | 13.72 | 9.61, 16 |
| `fox/ground` | 16.14 | 7.13, 15 |
| `daylight/aerial` | 16.30 | 7.94, 15 |
| `fireflies/aerial` | 16.35 | 7.66, 15 |

Pinned at step 6, 25 of the 81 phases within the build budget were over the line. Governed, none was, the dearest
`flora/ground` at the bottom step. A run of an earlier build pinned at the ladder's top step (full swapchain, 4x MSAA,
no foveation) read the bare meadow at 24.7 ms standing and 35.0 from the air, against 7.9 and 9.8 governed: that is
the top step's cost, not the scene's.

What a village past the budget costs on the Quest 2 when it can be seen (`demo/village-layers.json`, step 6, records
in `perf/quest-2/village-layers/`): the stress scene's own `village/aerial` view looks through the full tree pool's
crowns, so it read 19.9 ms. The same village from the same eye with no trees between reads 158 and 161 ms. Hiding the
far roof tiles takes it to 83 and 89 ms; hiding all masonry, to 12.0 and 12.1. The timber village read 71 to 131 ms
at every step tried, top to bottom. The GPU spends 78% of that time shading vertices, 117 M a second, so about 19 M
a frame for 1.46 M primitives drawn: its bins re-run the vertices of every piece in them. That is the fixed-foveation
map, not the pieces ("Fixed foveation on the Quest 2"): with the map off the same village reads 25 to 33 ms and each
bin runs only the vertices that fall in it.

The deer and the fox come and go on their own timetable, so the scene brings a band of each in (`roamers`) before
each view of them; a view of an absent animal fails the script.

### Moving and acting at the build budget

Report (verbatim): "That \"after a few seconds settle\" seems to be carrying weight. Framerate during movement and
during actions is the actual important value for players."

`demo/motion.json` builds a garden to the whole build budget the way play does (six trees, a pond, a 3.5 m house
carried in, flowers to the limit: fill 0.999), then walks into it, turns the head, snap turns, teleports on the ground
and into the air, tries a house it has no room for, grabs and sizes the house, paints grass at the limit, deletes the
house, paints grass, digs a pond, puts up a house and carries a conure, every phase measured from its first frame.
`demo/load.json` opens the same garden from its save and stands in it. On a Quest 2 (`bench.sh OUT --android APK`,
CPU and GPU at level 4), frames over 1.5 refresh periods and the 99th percentile frame, before (b735494d) and after:

| Phase | Late frames | p99 ms | Longest frame ms | Main thread ms (median) |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Carrying the session's first house in | 6 → 2 | 1757 → 24.8 | 1757 → 30 | 9.3 → 6.5 |
| Painting flowers to the limit | 0 → 0 | 15.3 → 15.2 | 18 → 18 | 6.9 → 6.5 |
| Walking into the garden | 36 → 12 | 26.5 → 28.5 | 52 → 29 | 7.9 → 7.6 |
| Turning the head | 2 → 0 | 20.2 → 15.4 | 182 → 18 | 9.5 → 9.0 |
| Snap turns | 0 → 0 | 15.8 → 15.8 | 16 → 16 | 8.3 → 7.8 |
| Teleports, ground and air | 0 → 0 | 15.4 → 15.5 | 16 → 17 | 10.4 → 8.4 |
| A house with no room | 0 → 3 | 19.1 → 40.0 | 21 → 55 | 9.1 → 10.8 |
| Grabbing and turning the house | 1 → 0 | 18.1 → 17.4 | 22 → 19 | 10.1 → 11.3 |
| Sizing it with two hands | 0 → 0 | 18.2 → 18.1 | 19 → 18 | 12.1 → 12.3 |
| Grass at the limit | 0 → 0 | 15.3 → 15.0 | 16 → 15 | 11.3 → 11.5 |
| Deleting the house | 1 → 2 | 20.6 → 19.0 | 36 → 37 | 40.7 → 12.2 |
| Painting grass | 2 → 1 | 23.6 → 16.0 | 34 → 33 | 8.9 → 9.0 |
| Digging a pond | 4 → 7 | 24.3 → 24.8 | 25 → 25 | 27.4 → 19.7 |
| Putting up a house | 0 → 0 | 15.2 → 14.8 | 16 → 15 | 8.6 → 8.1 |
| Carrying a conure | 0 → 0 | 14.9 → 15.4 | 15 → 20 | 9.1 → 8.8 |
| Opening the garden, first 5 s | 38 → 32 | 80 → 32 | 15145 → 471 | |

Rebased onto a later main (7b9d7cd9, the full day's sky) and run again against it, paired: the walk 40 → 19 late
frames, turning the head 3 → 0 (longest 157 → 17 ms), the first house 6 → 2 (longest 232 → 31 ms), the rest as above.

What each overrun was, and what changed:

- The session's first roof made four shader materials (plaster, thatch, the torching under the tiles, the roof's
  wood), and a wall's first fittings three more, on first use: a 0.25 to 1.5 s frame as the first house went up
  (`scripts/hitches.py` on a simpleperf recording: `ShaderMaterial::set_shader` down to `vkCreateShaderModule`). They
  are now made as the game loads.
- The governor stepped down in time but the steps arrived over seconds: grass, flowers and ivy receded toward a
  lower step at 8 m/s, so a walk from the garden's edge into it ran at 13 to 18 ms of GPU time for four seconds with
  the governor already at step 8. A step down now takes its reaches at once; only a step up grows them out.
- The Quest 2 misses refreshes from about 0.84 of the period, short of the governor's "over budget" (1.05): those
  frames stepped it down only through the one-second average, a step a second. A refresh missed with the GPU over
  the governor's line now steps down two at once, and a step settles three frames instead of six.
- The first step to the 0.78 3D scale compiled 112 to 130 pipelines for its separate pass, a 110 to 180 ms frame
  mid-play. The first two frames of a session now draw the last step, among loading's own late frames; reaching it
  later in the session then compiles nothing (pinned: 112 pipelines and a 112 ms frame before, no late frame after).
  What is built after those frames still compiles its own pipelines the first time a step draws it: the house and
  flowers this scene builds cost 10 to 25 pipelines, a 28 to 40 ms frame, where a garden opened from its save does
  not.

The main thread's share, found per frame with a debug build on the Quest 2 sending each frame's GDScript profile to
the remote debugger (`--remote-debug` written into the APK's `_cl_`, `adb reverse`), and fixed. Late frames, p99 and
longest frame in ms, two runs of main (d64f3924) before and two after, the garden saved as play saves it:

| Phase | Before | After |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Deleting the house | 2, 21.7, 35.5 · 3, 21.2, 35.8 | 0, 16.5, 20.2 · 1, 20.8, 21.4 |
| Painting grass | 2, 24.8, 34.2 · 1, 16.4, 34.9 | 1, 16.2, 28.4 · 1, 16.4, 28.2 |
| Digging a pond | 5, 27.0, 29.0 · 6, 25.3, 25.8 | 2, 23.1, 25.1 · 3, 23.7, 27.7 |
| Walking into the garden | 17, 45.4, 56.5 · 14, 35.8, 61.5 | 20, 44.5, 115.9 · 12, 42.6, 51.0 |
| A house with no room | 0, 15.1, 15.2 · 1, 16.7, 26.4 | 0, 15.1, 15.2 · 0, 15.6, 16.2 |

- A wall freed while its ivy was still being laid on a worker thread waited for it: 25 ms of a deleting frame. A
  freed wall's tasks now finish on their own and are collected as they do (`Wall.reap`); only quitting waits.
- Digging forced every stroke still growing to grow at once, so its plants stood on the old ground: 68 of an 80 ms
  frame after a grass stroke. A stroke's spots now keep only where they are across the ground and take its height as
  each plant grows.
- The grass cover (FloraCover) found each changed texel's plants by a radius search of their grid squares, every
  quarter second while a stroke grew: 10 to 25 ms frames. It now reads each square under the changed texels once.
- A grown stroke hid its ghost a plant at a time, 17 ms for 1900 plants, though the ghosts go together once the last
  stroke has grown; that case now clears them in one call.

What is left, measured: letting go of a grass stroke works out all its spots and which fall on bare ground in that
frame (`Flora.grow`, 17 of the frame's 37 ms in the debug build), one 28 ms frame; a dig fills its pond on the main
thread when the fill looks small (`World.AHEAD`), and pouring it in applies the fill and repaints the grass cover, two
or three frames of 23 to 28 ms; working the fill out ahead of every dig instead let the next dig find the ground not
yet dug and refuse its pond. Deleting is at the line: its frame is 20 to 21 ms, of which the bench driver stepping
its script (`Driver._run`) is 6 in the debug build. The house with no room was late in 1 of 9 runs, 26 ms, which no
profiled run caught.

The walk's late frames are the governor's, logged now as it moves (`quality: step 6 to 8, the GPU at 22.0 ms of
13.9`): the walk's first frame compiles 4 or 5 pipelines while drawing (28 ms; the metrics line now tells those
from the ones compiled on worker threads); a step down not yet far enough misses refreshes until the next (step 8
held 13.3 ms of GPU for half a second); and the step to the 0.78 3D scale reallocates the scene's buffers in one
50 to 58 ms frame. Dropping straight to the last step that pops nothing on a frame a quarter over budget measured
the same 20 late frames, so it was taken back out.

Opening the garden from its save: its first frame is the load itself, 490 to 510 ms; the next two seconds run 30 to
40 late frames of 22 to 45 ms, the first two rendered frames at the last step reallocating the 3D buffers and
compiling 10 to 20 pipelines while drawing, the rest frames at 10 to 13 ms of GPU while the governor sits out its
warm-up and the main thread finishes laying. Reading the picture book's pages back from the GPU without waiting
(`texture_get_data_async`) delivered the same 103 frames in the first three seconds, and a 1 s warm-up the same 36 to
40 late frames, so neither was kept.

## Build budget

Directive (verbatim): "I'd like an indicator somewhere. Uh, I think probably on the- on
the little hands, on the little controllers, that displays a bar, and that bar should
represent, how much of the frame budget, you've consumed by placing objects. I want to
make it not possible to place so many objects that the game- starts stuttering, and I
wanna make it obvious to the user that there is a limit there"

Directive (verbatim): "I'd like it to be fixed... a fixed amount and not change depending on what hardware is running"

- What a garden holds counts in units (`Budget.UNITS`): square metres of stone wall,
  plaster wall, fence and path face, and of a tiled or flat roof and a thatched one laid along their slopes out to their eaves; fittings that add a mesh (lanterns, flags,
  chimneys, dormers, each dormer's boarded faces counted as stone wall and its slopes as tiled roof besides); painted grass blades and flowers; trees and bushes standing; square metres of pond water, all of it charged at most what a view full of water adds; square metres of painted stone ground, all of it charged at most what a view full of stone adds (see "Ground materials"). The
  meadow's own trees and bushes count as planted ones do, so a fresh garden starts 32% full and clearing them frees
  their share; its own grass and flowers are part of the base and count nothing (below). A tree counts one unit at
  every scale. `World` keeps the count as every command applies, so deleting
  frees exactly what placing took, and undo, wipes and a garden loaded or joined count
  alike on every machine.
- `Budget.COSTS` is one fixed table in GPU microseconds per unit, and
  `Budget.CAPACITY_MS` is one fixed 3.99 ms-equivalent capacity. Fill is
  the cost of the garden divided by that capacity, on Frame, Quest, desktop and tests.
  Neither the running quality step, refresh rate nor the visitors present changes it.
  The host and visitor apply the same check; removing cost still works in older gardens
  already over capacity, and undo uses the same limit.
- Masonry (stone, plaster and fence faces, tiled roofs and thatch) is charged as the frame pays for it: `Masonry`
  draws a stone whole only while it stands within the fit step's `stone_near_m` of the eye, and 12-triangle
  stones past it (see "Near masonry a piece at a time"). So each masonry unit costs a far rate on all of it, fitted from the air, where every chunk draws far
  meshes, and a near excess (`wall_m2_near` and the like) on what stands in the chunks one eye can have near at once
  (`Budget.near`): `World` bins each build's masonry into Masonry's 8 m chunks along its line (`Budget.spread`), and
  the near units are what the 3 by 3 chunks around the dearest one hold (`Budget.near_span`; a chunk can hold a piece
  drawn near only while its centre is within 4 m and a chunk's radius, 7.2 m, of the eye, which never reaches past
  the chunks beside the eye's own). The excess is fitted standing among the walls, over what their far rate already charges. Timber stays
  counted per member at its dense-village price (`TIMBER_US`). Two interleaved Quest 2 runs on 2eb107d
  ([record](../perf/quest-2/costs/20261024T035706Z-2eb107d.json),
  [record](../perf/quest-2/costs/20261024T041730Z-2eb107d.json)), the larger of each, µs a m², far + near: stone wall
  4.29 + 6.23 (each from the 10 m disc), plaster 1.11 + 4.28, fence 2.27 + 3.38, tiled roof 9.44 + 0 (9.49 since a8dc2bc, below), thatch
  10.18 + 0. The near excess was refitted since a piece takes its near mesh by its own distance (see "Near masonry
  a piece at a time"). Lanterns and thatch are measured on the builds before them left standing (the 10 m disc of walls, the
  village re-thatched) and fitted against those from the same view, so they are charged only what they add:
  `fitting` 15.19 µs. A hamlet of five default stone houses 14 to 25 m apart (`hamlet`, its walls 264 m² and roofs
  200 m²) held 72 Hz from the air and standing and measured 3.42 ms from the air, its dearest view; it is charged
  3.60 ms, where the per-square-metre table before (wall 8.84, roof 22.09, both from their densest views) charged it
  6.75 ms, 144% of the capacity. A default stone house alone (53 m² of wall, 40 of roof) takes 20.0% of the capacity
  where it took 28.9%; each more one out of its near chunks takes 12.9%. `demo/full.json` filled to 100.0% on this
  table held 72 Hz from the air, standing, among the walls and by the house
  ([record](../perf/quest-2/full/20261024T042238Z-6833585.json): 9.62, 7.83, 9.30 and 9.17 ms).
- The fixed table takes the larger measured price for each unit from the Quest 2 and
  Frame calibrations below: the Quest 2 prices throughout, each the larger of two interleaved costs runs
  ([record](../perf/quest-2/costs/20261022T103832Z-6c986f4.json),
  [record](../perf/quest-2/costs/20261022T110509Z-6c986f4.json)), except the flora "Cheaper flora" made cheaper,
  refitted on that build (trees and bushes from its costs fit), and grass and flowers, refitted on 3eb6468, at the
  dearest view of a 1.8 m disc packed as full as still holds the refresh rate, in the costs scene beside the eye or the
  pondweed scene from the bed and the shore. Two costs runs of a8dc2bc fitted on `App=`
  ([record](../perf/quest-2/costs/20261026T070527Z-a8dc2bc.json),
  [record](../perf/quest-2/costs/20261026T073236Z-a8dc2bc.json)) priced three units over the table, which took the
  larger: `water` 2,510 µs (2,380 in the other), the lake 2.51 ms over the meadow from the shore, over the Frame's 1.91
  from the air, which set it until then; `easel` 890 (860); `roof_m2` 9.488 (8.129). Every other unit fit under its
  price. Refitted on 0774568 (below), every Frame price but `water` fell under the Quest 2's: `fitting`, the
  Frame's 15.609 µs until then, fit to 3.852 in that run, under the Quest 2's, so the Frame no longer sets it. The prices
  themselves live only in `Budget.COSTS`; this document cites the runs they came from. Each price a record carries (`prices`, added by `scripts/paired.py --price`) is held by
  `tests/test_prices.py`: no unit is charged below the latest one recorded for it. Capacity is the Quest 2's room, every device priced on its table: the 72 Hz calibration
  line of 11.111111111111111 ms less the Quest 2's 7.12 ms base, rounded down to 3.99 ms. The base is what
  `scripts/costs.py` defines, on the runtime's `App=` time (see "Bench repeatability on the Quest 2"): the dearest view
  of the meadow without its own trees and bushes, plus wildlife, plus the compositor's timewarp, from two costs runs of
  a8dc2bc ([record](../perf/quest-2/costs/20261026T070527Z-a8dc2bc.json),
  [record](../perf/quest-2/costs/20261026T073236Z-a8dc2bc.json)) and two runs of `demo/meadow-flora.json` interleaved
  with them on one Quest 2 ([record](../perf/quest-2/meadow-flora/20261026T071237Z-a8dc2bc.json),
  [record](../perf/quest-2/meadow-flora/20261026T073943Z-a8dc2bc.json), read by `scripts/layers.py`). The meadow, the
  larger of each view, less the smaller of what the flora runs read its trees and bushes to cost there: from the air
  5.90 less 0.37, standing 5.61 less 0.30, from the shore 5.57 less 0.22; the bare meadow's own views (`base/`, the
  same meadow with them cleared) read at most 5.55 from the air, the dearest, so 5.55; plus the larger run's 0.39 of
  wildlife (ponds) and 1.18 of timewarp. costs.py's own base, the meadow with its trees and bushes, was 7.40 and
  7.42 in the two runs. The room was 4.68 ms until then, fitted on the governor's viewport timer, whose base (6.42 on
  6a5692a) carried the timewarp only in the spells it fell inside a frame's span. The meadow's 46 trees and 60 bushes are charged
  1.51 ms at the table's prices, at least the 0.37 the room gained for them.
  The Frame's room below (1.77 ms,
  its own plants in the base, the ladder held at step 8) is no input: Directive (verbatim): "I'd like everything
  priced on the quest2 table."
  The meadow's grass and flowers stay in the base: priced as painted plants, at their densest beside the eye, its
  22,000 clumps and 3,200 flowers would charge several times the whole capacity, where clearing them with its trees and bushes saves 1.14 to 1.15 ms
  from the air, 1.83 to 1.88 standing and 0.62 to 0.67 from the shore (the a8dc2bc flora records). These are fixed design numbers, independent of future governor tuning.
  The Quest 2 is the weakest supported target; its full-garden runs below held 72 fps
  once the ladder settled. This choice admits no more cost than either measured table.
  The measurements bound the tested scenes, not every possible arrangement; the initial
  quality-settling frames remain as documented below.
- `demo/fill.json` checks the whole table against the frame: four gardens built to 50%, 75% and the full room as a
  player is refused at it (`{"op": "budget", "fill": F}` stops builds at that share), a fresh meadow built out as a
  hamlet, walls and planting, walls alone, houses alone and a mix, each seen from the air, standing at the meadow's
  centre and standing among its builds, at `Budget.fit_step()`; at the full room it frees the governor and hides each
  layer in turn. Two runs on one Quest 2 ([record](../perf/quest-2/fill/20261030T024507Z-6d75ada.json)), `App=` ms,
  the dearest view of each, against the table's prediction (its 5.94 ms base, wildlife included, plus fill times
  the capacity):

  | Garden at its full room | Fill | Predicted | Measured, dearest view |
  | --- | --- | --- | --- |
  | Meadow built out | 1.000 | 9.93 | 6.43, from the air |
  | Walls | 0.957 | 9.76 | 7.04, among them |
  | Houses (four) | 0.875 | 9.43 | 9.02, among them ([record](../perf/quest-2/fill-profile/20261030T033104Z-6d75ada.json)) |
  | Mix | 1.000 | 9.93 | 6.86, among them |

  The table holds where it must: standing among a full room of houses reads within 0.4 ms of its prediction, the
  wildlife allowance (no ponds here) left out, so the room cannot grow without that view going over. Spread and mixed
  gardens leave 2.5 to 3.5 ms, since each unit is charged its dearest view and no one view meets them all at it; the
  governor spends that on the ladder (step 5 in every full garden, 72 Hz held). Among the houses at their full room,
  hiding the masonry saves 4.2 ms of 9.0 (96,000 primitives in 30 draws), 2x MSAA 1.5, grass 0.9, the terrain 0.3;
  among the walls, masonry 2.8 of 7.5, grass 1.0, MSAA 0.9, the terrain 0.7; shadows, post and ceilings read within
  run noise of nothing. A garden holds more by making those cheaper, not by charging them less.
- While a hand's tool shows a placement, a bar behind the held tool shows this same garden
  fill, moss green warming to amber near full, with a paler stretch for what the placement
  adds; it swells and pulses red, and the sight turns red, when the placement would not fit.
  Otherwise the bar is hidden. Refused actions knock twice; painting stops at capacity and
  knocks once.
- `demo/costs.json`, played by `CAIRNFOLD_SCENE=demo/costs.json scripts/bench.sh OUT ...`
  and fitted by `scripts/costs.py OUT`, which prints a candidate table for review, measures each kind of build alone over the bare
  meadow at `Budget.fit_step()` (the bottom of the quality ladder), from the air and standing. Each phase of walls, a
  village or paths is laid by a `sized` step: while either view reads over the governor's line,
  the meadow is bared and the builds laid again over the share of their area that would bring
  it under (walls farther apart, the village on a smaller disc, paths shorter), so one script
  is measured at the refresh rate on every headset. It opens on 30 s of the bare meadow unmeasured: a
  Quest's shell draws a tip beside a freshly launched game for its first 30 s, at the cost of the
  game's GPU time. Grass and flowers are measured twice: their pool spread over a 20 m disc, and packed into a 1.8 m disc
  seen standing over it (`sweep/grass-dense`, `sweep/flowers-dense`, the `near` view), where each plant covers the most:
  9,000 clumps of grass, the most that still hold the refresh rate there at the fit step, and the whole 4,000 flowers.
  Packed past any budget a garden holds, they only measure. A unit's
  cost is the highest any view gives; a view whose frames fell below the refresh rate is left out, its GPU time not the scene's.
  Hills measured within noise of the bare meadow, so shaped ground counts nothing; painted stone ground does
  not on the Quest 2 (see "Ground materials", Cost). Lantern light is baked (`Lamps`); a lantern counts by its meshes.
- A roof counts the area along its slopes: its plan out to the eaves times `RoofLayout.stretch`, the slope's length
  over its run for a gable, the cone's surface over its disc for a tower, 1 for a flat deck. Tiles and straw courses
  are laid a fixed distance apart along the slope, so they grow with that area, not the plan. The Frame's `roof_m2`
  and `thatch_m2` are fitted by slope. The Quest 2's were fitted per square metre of plan, on the costs scene's
  village alone, whose houses are all one shape (4.9 by 3.08 m, rise 1.29 m, straight slopes; stretch 1.22022), and
  divided by that stretch, which is the cost the same run gives counted by slope, until it is measured again.
- `demo/stress.json`, `demo/ladder.json`, `demo/tour.json` and `demo/costs.json` build past
  the budget on purpose (`{"op": "budget", "bounded": false}`), to measure what lies
  beyond it.

On the Steam Frame (`CAIRNFOLD_SCENE=demo/costs.json bench.sh OUT SSH_HOST BUNDLE UNWORN...`, unworn, at
`Budget.fit_step()`; [record](../perf/frame/costs/20261022T185026Z-0774568.json)), viewport GPU milliseconds a frame,
from the air and standing, every phase at 72 Hz, over the bare meadow's 5.05 and 4.54 ms (5.11 beside the eye, 4.81 at
dusk). `scripts/costs.py` fits each unit below; the Frame's base and room are no input to `Budget`. Its prices before
this run, on b717f4b before the specular cut, are in [that record](../perf/frame/costs/20261020T213034Z-b717f4b.json):

| Phase | Units | From the air | Standing | This run's fit | b717f4b |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |

| Flower pool full | 4,000 flowers | 5.38 | 4.99 | 0.300 µs a flower, packed beside the eye (6.31) | 0.215 |

| Bush pool full | 256 bushes | 5.31 | 4.65 | 0.994 µs a bush | 1.60 |
