| Walls, 15 m disc | 1,562 m² | 7.39 | 8.25 | | |
| Walls, 20 m disc | 2,837 m² | 9.31 | 8.80 | | |
| Plaster walls, 15 m disc | 1,562 m² | 5.78 | 6.85 | 1.48 µs a m² | 2.01 |
| Fences, 15 m disc | 416 m² | 5.32 | 5.12 | 1.37 µs a m² | 3.17 |
| Ponds | 71 m² | 4.95 | 4.27 | nothing over the bare meadow | 19.5 a pond |
| Lake, the meadow's bowl filled | | 7.72 | 3.52 | 1,908 µs, all of a garden's water | |
| Village | 215 m² of wall, 158 m² of roof | 6.39 | 5.74 | 2.35 µs a m² of roof | 6.65 |
| Village thatched | 215 m² of wall, 158 m² of thatch | 6.20 | 5.53 | 1.14 µs a m² of thatch | 3.48 |
| Lanterns | 90 on 714 m² of wall | 6.36 | 8.10 | 3.85 µs a lantern | 15.6 |
| Easels | 4 | 5.17 | 5.19 | 162 µs an easel | 532 |

The lake's view from the air is the meadow's 5.81 ms plus 1.91; standing in it, it drowns the plants around the eye
and reads 1.40 ms under the meadow. The walls, village and paths are laid by `sized` steps, so their units are what
the run laid; on this build none was thinned. Walls cost most per square metre standing among the first ones: each
wall near the eye is drawn in full, while those further off draw 12-triangle stones. So the fit takes the 10 m disc
standing.

The meadow's own scenery is the base, measured whole, not counted at the pool-full prices:
its plants stand spread across the whole meadow, where a pool-full phase packs as many
into a 20 m disc. Counted, its 3,200 flowers, 46 trees and 60 bushes charge the Frame 1.73 ms
against the 1.62 measured, and the Quest 2, once refitted at 0.68 µs a flower and 70.7 µs a tree
(81105e0), 5.42 ms against the 2.09 measured: 141.5% of the room before anything was built,
so every build was turned down on a Quest.

A garden filled to the budget through the tools (`demo/full.json`: six trees, a flower
stroke, a path, a pond, a house, walls until they were turned down, then a flower stroke that
paints until the budget stops it), played with the ladder free. The script lays most of the
garden within a second, so the ladder starts from the top and walks down over the next
seconds; the frames late there are that walk, which a garden built by hand never makes.
Once the ladder settles (step 10 on both):

| Headset, share of the budget | From the air | Standing | Among the walls | By the house |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Quest 2, 99.9 to 100% (four runs) | 72 fps, 9.7 to 9.8 ms App | 72 fps, 7.6 ms | 72 fps, 8.0 to 8.1 ms | 72 fps, 7.9 to 8.0 ms |
| Steam Frame, 97.7% (the next wall turned down) | 72 to 73 fps, 7.5 ms viewport | 72 to 73 fps, 5.9 ms | 72 to 73 fps, 5.6 ms | 72 to 73 fps, 6.0 ms |

Both are held to the 11.1 ms line, the Quest 2 in App time and the Frame in viewport time; at the
limit the Quest 2 keeps 1.4 ms and the Frame 3.6 ms. In the first two or three seconds from the
air, while the ladder walked down, the Quest 2 ran 52 to 70 fps and the Frame, from the top
step, half rate for about ten seconds.

### Bench repeatability on the Quest 2

One build benched twice read the meadow up to 2 ms apart, steady within each run, and once stepped 1.5 ms mid-run
(`perf/quest-2/meadow-layers/20261021T203327Z-ab7159c.json`, `…/20261021T222648Z-ab7159c.json`,
`…/20261021T221029Z-e6f5b3b.json`). Two causes, each measured:

- **The hands.** Scenes that posed no hand left them where the unworn headset's idle controllers last stood. The dear
  runs drew about 20 more draw calls for a few hundred primitives, and their captures show the shut book in the left
  hand and the right hand's beam in view, which the cheap run's do not; the mid-run step came the second logcat had
  both controllers go `CONNECTED_INACTIVE tracking: NONE`. `demo/hands-repro.json` alternates the hands held as a
  player holds them while building with the hands out of view, three times from each view: held reads 0.29 to 0.37 ms
  dearer from the air and 0.45 to 0.69 standing, every time
  ([record](../perf/quest-2/hands-repro/20261025T113811Z-b6adba0.json)). In a headset the driver now holds both hands
  so from a script's start (`Driver.HELD`, the stress scene's pose), until a step poses them.
- **The governor's timer.** The viewport GPU timer reads about 1 ms more (the compositor's timewarp, VrApi `TW=` 1.15
  to 1.21 ms) in spells of seconds when that work lands inside the frame's span, with draws, primitives and VrApi's own
  `App=` unchanged; a 7 s phase can fall wholly in or out of one. `scripts/costs.py` fits on `App=` (bench.sh's
  record), and its base adds the run's timewarp, so the room holds in those spells too.

Three costs runs of one build with both: the base 7.44, 7.64 and 7.51 ms, where the viewport timer gave 6.98, 7.63 and
6.90 ([record](../perf/quest-2/costs/20261025T120532Z-b6adba0.json),
[record](../perf/quest-2/costs/20261025T123216Z-b6adba0.json),
[record](../perf/quest-2/costs/20261025T130115Z-b6adba0.json)). The rest of the spread is `App=` itself: about
0.25 ms more in seconds the runtime preempts the game four times a frame (VrApi `Preempt=` near 280) over twice (near 145).

### What the bare meadow costs the Quest 2

`CAIRNFOLD_SCENE=demo/meadow-layers.json bench.sh OUT --android APK` hides each part of the meadow in turn, twice, each
stretch against the part shown beside it, from the air and standing, at the fit step; `scripts/layers.py OUT` reads it.
Milliseconds a frame in the governor's signal, on main before the cut below
([record](../perf/quest-2/meadow-layers/20261021T203327Z-ab7159c.json)), and the share of each kept at a 0.5 swapchain
(a render target multiplier of 0.5, 51% of the pixels; [record](../perf/quest-2/meadow-layers/20261021T204840Z-ab7159c.json)):

| Hidden | From the air | Standing | Kept at 0.5, air and standing |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| The meadow shown | 10.26 | 8.33 | 0.65, 0.68 |
| Ground (terrain mesh) | 3.99 | 2.07 | 0.68, 0.69 |
| Trees and bushes | 3.22 | 1.63 | 0.89, 0.97 |
| Grass blades | 0.46 | 2.05 | 0.83, 0.79 |
| Flowers | 0.59 | 0.98 | 0.68, 0.88 |
| Sky | 0.10 | 0.18 | |
| Mountains | 0.06 | 0.07 | |
| Sun's disc, water, hands, grading | 0.0 to 0.05 | 0.0 to 0.05 | |
| Everything at once | 9.06 | 7.40 | |
| The sun's light, over all of it | 3.25 | 1.70 | 0.78, 1.12 |
| 2x MSAA, over all of it | 2.41 | 1.79 | 0.61, 0.80 |
| Ambient sky light | 0.35 | 0.64 | |
| Fog | 0.31 | 0.57 | |

With everything hidden 1.2 and 1.1 ms remain: the clear, the compositor's share and the wildlife. The parts' costs add
to 0.6 and 0.2 ms less than the whole, which is what they share. The sun, MSAA, ambient light and fog are not parts:
each is paid inside the ground's, trees' and plants' pixels. The foothills were gone before this run (c157993b).

The frame is two thirds pixels: a 0.5 swapchain keeps 65 to 68% of its time where pure fill would keep 51%. The ground
and the flowers are fill; the trees are not (0.89, 0.97), and neither is the sun's light standing. "Which knobs matter"
found the render region saved nothing because the region does not shrink the swapchain's passes; the swapchain itself
does.

Leaves, bark, stone, plaster, thatch and roof timber shade without specular: all at roughness 0.92 or rougher, where
the sun's highlight is a faint sheen side-by-side renders do not show. On one Quest 2, the same scene played
main, cut, main (median of three 15 s rounds a view): 10.33, 9.24, 10.38 ms from the air and 8.69, 8.09, 8.76 standing.

The same build and headset read 10.26 and 8.33 ms in the first run and 11.90 and 10.38 two hours later
([record](../perf/quest-2/meadow-layers/20261021T222648Z-ab7159c.json)), the clocks held at CPU 4 / GPU 4 throughout
and the headset 37.2 °C then 39.0 °C. So only interleaved comparisons resolve a millisecond on a Quest 2, and a
`base_ms` fitted in one run is good to about 2 ms; the cut build's own layer run
([record](../perf/quest-2/meadow-layers/20261021T221029Z-e6f5b3b.json)) steps by 1.5 ms between its stretches the same
way.

Refitted on that build ([record](../perf/quest-2/costs/20261021T214216Z-e6f5b3b.json)): `base_ms` 9.38, room 1.73 ms.
Main before it fitted 10.78 and 0.33 ms ([record](../perf/quest-2/costs/20261021T210901Z-ab7159c.json)), against the
9.10 and 2.01 ms the table carried from 20261020. Walls cost 23.8 µs a m² from the 231 m² disc standing, and the
standing cost of walls saturates: +5.51, +5.34 and +4.76 ms for 231, 450 and 584 m², against +1.67, +3.74 and
+4.64 ms from the air. The village's 43 m² of wall and 32 m² of roof measured +0.36 to +0.59 ms and are priced 1.62 ms;
the lanterns' phase reads under its walls' price, so `fitting` fits to nothing.
The lake drowns the plants the cut made cheaper, so its view reads as before (9.56 ms App time) over a meadow 0.7 ms
cheaper: a view full of water cost 1.99 ms, more than the room then, until the water itself was made cheaper ("Water").

### Anti-aliasing on the Quest 2

Anti-aliasing stays on every headset. `demo/antialias.json` (driver step `antialias`) holds each state for 5 s, three
times in alternating order, from the air and standing, at the fit step
([record](../perf/quest-2/antialias/20261022T041131Z-5b9fdbe.json)); viewport GPU ms a frame:

| State | From the air | Standing |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 2x MSAA (as shipped before) | 8.51 | 7.22 |
| No MSAA | 7.10 (−1.42) | 6.12 (−1.10) |
| 4x MSAA | 11.17 (+2.65) | 8.74 (+1.52) |
| 2x MSAA, fixed foveation 1 | 8.24 (−0.27) | 7.09 (−0.13) |
| 2x MSAA, fixed foveation 2 | 8.07 (−0.44) | 6.95 (−0.27) |
| 2x MSAA, fixed foveation 3 | 7.30 (−1.22) | 6.55 (−0.68) |
| FXAA, no MSAA | 10.70 (+2.17) | 10.02 (+2.79) |
| SMAA, no MSAA | 7.31 (−1.19) | 11.01 (+3.79) |

Where 2x MSAA's cost goes. The Adreno draws the scene in tiles of on-chip memory, and 2x samples double each pixel's
bytes there: the render-stage trace (`ovrgpuprofiler -t`) shows the scene pass going from 12 tiles of 288×256 to 25 of
192×176 (4x: 45), and the extra time is in the tiles' Render stage, which runs every draw once per tile it touches
(aerial: Render 4.07 → 5.49 ms, StoreColor 0.13 → 0.36, Binning 1.25 → 1.08). It is not bandwidth: the bus read about
26 MB a frame either way and sat under 20% busy. It is not extra shading: drawn unshaded, 2x still costs 2.0 ms both
ways. And it follows no one part of the meadow: with the ground, grass, flowers or trees hidden in turn, 2x costs about
the same as with all of them drawn (the part's cost with and without MSAA differs by −0.41 to +0.43 ms, summing to
+0.14 from the air and −0.47 standing). Edges are few: 2x changes 3.4% of the pixels from the air and 4.8% standing,
most of them grass blades.

Fixed foveation (`XR_FB_foveation`) works through the Mobile renderer only with the view's VRS mode at `VRS_XR`
(Godot 4.6 `render_forward_mobile.cpp`, `get_color_fbs`: the runtime's density map joins the scene pass only then); the
row in "Which knobs matter" set the level without it, so its map was never applied. With it, level 3 draws 15 of the 25
tiles, merging the outer ones, which is where it saves. Its outer edges are coarser, close to no MSAA at the rim, and
the middle keeps 2x. Fixed foveation is now one of the ladder's knobs on every runtime that supports it, level 3
from the start rung down and 0 above it (see "One ladder for every headset"). Its saving
only lowers what a frame costs, so `Budget.COSTS` were not refitted: every charge still bounds its unit, and a fit from
the one foveated `demo/costs.json` run, which the system shell drew beside, scattered both ways (roofs 41 µs a m²
against the 18.7 then charged, walls 11 against 23.8), wider than any change foveation could make.

That saving was measured over the bare meadow. Over built walls seen from the air, the density map is the dearest
thing the Quest 2 draws. Bisected on the device, the stress scene's village/aerial went from 29 ms (36f3888f) to 291
(9ad5a2ba), r25/aerial from 27 to 191. On one build at step 5, with the village held from the air, viewport GPU ms
a frame ([records](../perf/quest-2/foveation/README.md)):

| State | village/aerial |
| --- | --- |
| VRS mode XR, foveation 3 (main) | 249 |
| VRS mode never set (foveation 3 asked of the runtime, no map applied) | 27 |
| VRS mode XR with foveation 1 / 2 / 3, interleaved | 263 / 249 / 171 |
| VRS mode off, foveation 0, interleaved | 32 |

The cost belongs to the mode, not the level. It is not the sun's shadows: 119 ms with them at step 14, 117 without at
step 15. It is not shading: 164 ms drawn unshaded, against 184. It is not MSAA: 149 ms without, 186 with 2x, 314 with
4x. Ground views of the same village are unaffected, at 25 ms either way.

### Fixed foveation on the Quest 2

The order in which a session first draws the map does not clear it. Where the map is dear is what the view holds.
The timber village from the air at step 6, every phase in one session on one Quest 2, viewport GPU ms a frame
([records](../perf/quest-2/foveation-map/README.md)):

| View | Map on | Map off |
| --- | --- | --- |
| The village alone | 100 to 110 | 25 to 33 |
| The same village with 64 tall trees in view | 16.5 to 17 | |
| With those trees there but hidden | 96 | |
| With 64 trees of a third the size in view | 88 | |
| With the 64 tall trees out of view | 101 | |
| The village with its masonry hidden | 6.8 | |

So the stress scene's village/aerial read 19.6 ms with the map at step 6 only because the flora pools' trees, filled
earlier in the scene and kept by `clear`, stood in view ("The stress scene on the Quest 2"). The render-stage trace (`ovrgpuprofiler -t -s 21,39`) shows
what the map does when it is dear. Without it, each of the 42 tiles of 192×176 shades the vertices that fall in it,
1 to 19 thousand. With it, each of the 19 tiles it draws shades 1.46 M vertices, every vertex of the walls in view,
and takes about 10 ms. Otherwise the tiles shade the same pixels. With the tall trees in view the map's tiles go back
to 15 to 37 thousand vertices each. What in the view makes the driver go one way or the other is not known. A larger
swapchain makes it worse, since there are more tiles to run the walls in: 110 ms at the 0.7 swapchain, 272 at 0.85 and
405 at 1.0.

What the map runs in every tile is instanced masonry. A build sets each kind of piece in one MultiMesh per chunk; a far
chunk baked into one mesh instead (`Masonry.bake`), each vertex carrying its piece's frame and custom data
(`piece.gdshaderinc`), draws the same stones with the map on at a fraction of the cost. One Quest 2, one session,
the stress scene's roofs and timber villages from the air at the bottom step, viewport GPU ms a frame
([records](../perf/quest-2/foveation-map/README.md), `demo/foveation-instancing.json`):

| Build | Roofs, map on | Roofs, map off | Timber, map on | Timber, map off |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Main, far chunks instanced | 97 | 20 | 98 | 22 |
| Far boxes cut from 12 triangles to 4, still instanced | 65 | 18 | 57 | 18 |
| Far chunks baked (this) | 28 | 18 | 19 | 19 |

A third of the triangles saved a third of the cost; dropping the instancing saved nearly all of it. Twice the
distance, a quarter of the pixels, read the same (67 against 63 ms), so it is not the pixels. The tall trees' cure fits:
their crowns hide the walls before the binning pass sorts them into tiles. Near chunks, whose pieces change as walls are
built, stay instanced, and a far chunk draws from its instances while its bake runs on a worker after any change.

So whether the map is drawn is measured, not assumed (`Quality.Map`). While the frame on a foveated rung reads over
the governor's line for half a second, the map is taken off for a trial. The trial reads the median of at least 12
frames once four have let the timer catch up. The map stays off only if the frame reads under 0.8 of what it read with
the map, and a trial that saves nothing waits 8 s before the next. Once the frame has been under the line for 2 s, the
map goes back on for a trial. It stays on unless that cost a fifth of the frame or more, and after a failed trial the
calm it waits for doubles, up to 64 s. Where the eyes are tracked there is no fixed map, so nothing changes on the
Steam Frame. Played governed, the timber village from the air read 92 ms on main, the map always on, and 22 ms with
`Quality.Map`. The same village with the tall trees in view held 72 Hz with the map on, 8.5 ms. A trial of the map off
over that view saved nothing, so the map went back on.

None of the meadow's plants cut out by alpha (`grass`, `flower`, `leaf` and `plant` shaders write no `ALPHA` and do
not discard), so alpha-to-coverage has nothing to act on. FXAA and SMAA blur textures and cost more standing; temporal
AA is out in VR.

What walls cost the Quest 2 (`CAIRNFOLD_SCENE=demo/costs.json bench.sh OUT --android APK`,
the ladder at its bottom step): GPU milliseconds a frame over the bare meadow of the same run,
whose own time read 19 to 27 ms here, so only the differences count. Before is 16 m masonry
chunks, after is 8 m; the bar of that time charged every headset the Frame's then 6.07 µs a m²:

| Walls 2 m apart, 4.5 m tall | Units | Bar then charged | Standing, before | Standing, after | From the air, before | From the air, after |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 10 m disc | 714 m² | 4.33 | 9.69 | 6.59 | 3.32 | 3.76 |
| 15 m disc | 1,562 m² | 9.48 | 15.15 | 9.67 | 6.42 | 7.16 |
| 20 m disc | 2,837 m² | 17.23 | 16.51 | 9.34 | 12.00 | 12.37 |

On main with the ground shading fix (684440f), both chunk sizes in one build and one run,
twice over (A B A B), over a bare meadow of 18.2 to 19.6 ms:

| Walls 2 m apart, 4.5 m tall | Standing, 16 m | Standing, 8 m | From the air, 16 m | From the air, 8 m |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 10 m disc | 14.6, 12.8 | 12.7, 13.3 | 4.7, 4.5 | 5.0, 4.9 |
| 15 m disc | 17.9, 18.6 | 14.7, 13.6 | 8.4, 8.3 | 9.3, 9.1 |

Standing among the 15 m disc's walls 8 m chunks save about 4 ms; from the air their extra
draw calls (198 to 234) cost about 0.6 ms.

The same build and scene on the Quest 3, whose bare meadow held 72 Hz at 8.6 to 9.3 ms
([record](../perf/quest-3/wall-cost-short/20261018T203359Z-31703e7.json)):

| Walls 2 m apart, 4.5 m tall | Standing, 16 m | Standing, 8 m | From the air, 16 m | From the air, 8 m |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 10 m disc | 4.1, 4.2 | 4.0, 4.3 | 1.1, 1.1 | 1.4, 1.3 |
| 15 m disc | 7.4, 7.3 | 5.0, 4.0 | 2.1, 2.0 | 2.5, 2.5 |

There 8 m chunks save 2.3 to 3.4 ms standing among the 15 m disc's walls and nothing among
the 10 m disc's; from the air they cost 0.2 to 0.5 ms.

**Decision: pieces cut by distance (stones, timber, ivy) stand in 8 m chunks, and so do their
near and far meshes; past the near distance a whole 16 m chunk draws the far meshes of the 8 m
ones under it in their place; everything drawn at every distance (mortar, tiles, coats, shadow
boxes) stands in 16 m chunks.** `Masonry` makes each 16 m far instance the `visibility_parent`
of its 8 m chunks' near and far instances, so the renderer draws either the one or the others,
never both and never neither; within an 8 m chunk the near and far instances each draw the pieces on their own
side of the near distance. Standing among walls a garden pays 8 m chunks' full stones;
from the air, 16 m chunks' draw calls. The extra draw calls of 8 m chunks were what cost from
the air: their primitives matched 16 m chunks' to under 1%.

Measured on the Quest 3, the ladder at its bottom step, every variant in one build and one
run, twice over (A B C, C B A), GPU milliseconds over each pass's own bare meadow (9.8 to
11.1 ms). First 8 m chunks for pieces cut by distance only, their far meshes too
([record](../perf/quest-3/chunks/20261019T044920Z-b8ce193.json)):

| Walls 2 m apart, 4.5 m tall | Standing, 16 m | Standing, 8 m | Standing, 8 m cut pieces | From the air, 16 m | From the air, 8 m | From the air, 8 m cut pieces |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 15 m disc | 7.8, 7.8 | 5.3, 5.1 | 5.3, 5.2 | 4.1, 4.0 | 4.6, 4.5 | 4.3, 4.3 |
| 20 m disc | 10.2, 10.3 | 6.5, 6.4 | 6.5, 6.3 | 7.7, 7.6 | 8.5, 8.3 | 8.2, 7.9 |

Then the far meshes by 16 m chunks, as decided
([record](../perf/quest-3/chunks2/20261019T050450Z-359736d.json); draw calls from the air over
the 20 m disc 202 to 204 against 16 m chunks' 202 to 203 and 8 m chunks' 262 to 264):

| Walls 2 m apart, 4.5 m tall | Standing, 16 m | Standing, 8 m | Standing, decided | From the air, 16 m | From the air, 8 m | From the air, decided |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 15 m disc | 7.8, 7.5 | 5.9, 5.5 | 5.3, 5.1 | 3.9, 4.8 | 4.3, 5.0 | 4.2, 4.2 |
| 20 m disc | 10.0, 10.2 | 6.5, 6.3 | 7.3, 6.2 | 8.1, 7.6 | 8.6, 8.3 | 7.8, 7.9 |

Among the 20 m disc's walls, the densest measured, standing costs 3 to 4 ms less than with
16 m chunks, as much as with 8 m chunks, and from the air no more than with 16 m chunks.

The same build and run on the Quest 2, whose bare meadow ran 22 to 29 ms, at half rate, so only
the differences count ([record](../perf/quest-2/chunks/20261022T112953Z-359736d.json)):

| Walls 2 m apart, 4.5 m tall | Standing, 16 m | Standing, 8 m | Standing, decided | From the air, 16 m | From the air, 8 m | From the air, decided |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 15 m disc | 17.3, 16.0 | 13.1, 11.1 | 13.9, 12.6 | 11.5, 11.4 | 11.4, 11.6 | 11.6, 11.6 |
| 20 m disc | 22.7, 20.4 | 17.1, 13.6 | 16.8, 14.8 | 20.4, 20.0 | 20.8, 20.4 | 20.4, 20.1 |

There too the decided chunks stand among the 20 m disc's walls about 6 ms cheaper than 16 m
chunks, within the runs' spread of 8 m chunks, and cost the same from the air as 16 m chunks
(draw calls 179 to 181 against 178 to 179, and 8 m chunks' 239 to 240).

Standing, a wall's cost no longer grows with the garden past the chunks around the eye, so
a linear price per m² undercharges the first walls and overcharges a wide garden. Over the
15 m disc standing, each kind of piece hidden in turn (paired runs, 8 m chunks): ivy 5.3 to
6.5 ms, full stones 1.7 to 2.5 ms, mortar about 1 ms, the stones' shadow boxes 0.1 ms.

Ivy there costs by the triangle it rasterises, not by its area, instances or MSAA. Over the
same scene without ivy, each variant paired in one run (medians of three rounds): ivy 6.8 to
8.0 ms; its vertices alone, every leaf collapsed to a point, 0.7 to 1.7 ms, and as much again
with one triangle a sprig, so instances cost nothing; leaves at 0.4 their size still 7.0 ms;
ivy without MSAA still 5.9 ms; unlit ivy 3.7 ms less; sprigs past its reach dropped 1 ms less;
vertex lighting, Lambert diffuse and no specular nothing. A leaf drawn as two triangles, one
per lobe folded along the midrib, instead of four around a raised vein: ivy 6.8 to 4.3 ms
and 8.0 to 5.4 ms, the leaves' outline and fold unchanged ([before, after](ivy-triangles.jpg)).
Earlier, sharing each lobe's vertices saved 0.4 ms, dropping its backlight nothing, and
drawing one side 1.4 ms at the cost of the leaves vanishing from behind. From the air the stones cost about 8 ms over few primitives,
and one detail-texture read instead of three saved nothing.

On the Quest 2, the run of 20261020 on 81105e0 that refitted the table then (`bench.sh OUT --android APK`, the bottom
step, 0.7 swapchain, 2x MSAA; no record of it is in perf/, and every price since is refitted), viewport
GPU milliseconds a frame over the bare meadow's 6.92 and 6.71, every view at 72 frames a second;
each `sized` phase was laid over the share of its area shown, the first that read under the line or the
fourth tried. With the wildlife the base, the meadow's own plants standing, is 9.10 ms and the room 2.0 ms:

| Walls, 10 m disc | 0.12 | 260 m² | 9.80 | 11.67 | 19.1 µs a m² |

Walls cost little more spread wide than close: standing in a field of them the nearest rows
fill the view however far apart they stand, so the sparsest field gives the dearest square
metre. Roof tiles past the ladder's near distance are plain boxes, as far stones are: drawn whole, five tiled houses
took 14.0 ms from the air against 9.3 with them plain and 9.4 with no tiles at all (bare 6.3), and a house's roof
fitted 92.6 µs a m². Thatch costs more than tiles from the air: five thatched houses read 11.4 ms.

Four easels, each with a stroke on its canvas, are the `easels` phase of `demo/costs.json`. On the Frame a later run
fitted them alone (`perf/frame/costs/`), its bare meadow 7.62 ms from the air and 5.67 standing: the easels read 8.05
and 7.91 ms, 0.62 ms an easel.

### Near masonry a piece at a time

At the build budget's full room, standing among four default stone houses (`demo/fill-profile.json`, fill 0.875),
the masonry took 4.4 ms of a 9.0 ms Quest 2 frame. Each part hidden in turn over that view and over the walls'
(`demo/masonry-prof.json`, [record](../perf/quest-2/masonry-prof/20261030T161232Z-a065cf4.json); `App=` ms, the
view with everything shown read 8.6 to 9.2 among the houses and 7.5 to 7.6 among the walls):

| Hidden | Among the houses | Among the walls |
| --- | --- | --- |
| All masonry | 4.64 | 4.70 |
| All masonry and MSAA | 4.34 | 4.53 |
| Roof tiles drawn whole | 6.92 | 7.55 |
| Stones drawn whole | 7.76 | 6.33 |
| Stones drawn coarse | 9.02 | 7.17 |
| Ivy | 8.04 | 6.31 |
| Mortar | 8.76 | 7.24 |
| Floors | 8.81 | 7.48 |
| Everything else (tiles coarse, shadow boxes, plaster coats, timber) | 9.00 to 9.03 | 7.48 to 7.60 |

What the whole tiles and stones cost is their small triangles shaded, not their vertices: over the same view in one
build (all shown 8.96 to 9.23), drawing each kind's whole pieces as their 12-triangle far box instead read 7.36 for
tiles and 7.60 for stones, as much as hiding them (7.23, 7.59); an unlit fragment 7.90 and 7.87; the stone
shader's own fragment work alone (the light kept) 8.29 and 8.09; skipping the light volumes each vertex reads,
nothing (9.08, 8.98).

Masonry drew a chunk's pieces whole once the chunk's centre stood within the near distance and its radius of the eye,
so at the fit step every piece within 11 m could be drawn whole where `stone_near_m` is 4 m: the house in front of
the eye, 7 m off, was. **Decision: each piece takes its near or far mesh by its own distance from the eye.** A chunk
that may hold a near piece draws both its instances, and each collapses the pieces on the other side of
`stone_near_m` to a point (`piece_range`, piece.gdshaderinc). A baked far chunk, drawn only past the near distance,
leaves the test out: read on every vertex of its mesh, it cost the view of a hamlet from the air 0.3 ms
(`demo/masonry-aerial.json`, settled views, `App=` ms; before 55764949, the test everywhere 72a28cd, baked chunks
without it e9db85b):

| From the air | Before | Test on every vertex | Baked chunks without it |

| Village | 8.56, 8.62, 8.68 | 9.09, 9.07 | 8.52, 8.59 |
| Hamlet | 9.06, 8.99, 8.94 | 9.30, 9.25 | 9.00, 9.05 |

## Automatic quality

Directive (verbatim):
> I expect since [Cairnfold] can measure frame times it could dynamically choose graphics settings to keep players safer from stutter. Limitations/quirks I think:
> - delta in the setting would need to not *cause* a frame pause
> - not sure which settings are worth making dynamic
> - multiple settings to tweak but only one signal
>
> My motivation for these thoughts are not wanting myself or my players to need to think about or tweak settings, but still see things as pretty as possible but still experience buttery framerate.

**Decision: the one graphics configuration is a ladder of steps, walked by the GPU time of
every frame, the same on every headset.** `Quality` (`game/src/quality.gd`) first decides how the
view is drawn: the sky's reflections, then fixed foveation's rim, then 4x MSAA against 2x, then the
swapchain at 0.85 and 0.7 of the runtime's size a side. Below that it first draws the meadow's sparse far grass to 60,
48, 36 or 30 m before holding it at 24 m, then settled stones in full only to 8 m instead of 16 m
and ivy to 25 m instead of 35 m; below those, flowers to 20 m instead of 60 m, then foliage from
coarser blobs, and stones in full to 4 m and ivy to 12 m; last, where nothing else held the
refresh rate, the sun's shadows off and then the 3D scale at 0.78 of the swapchain's
(see "Holding 72 Hz on the Frame"). A governor reads each
frame's GPU time and steps down the ladder at once when frames run over the refresh period, a refresh
is missed with the GPU over its line, or their average runs over the line, and back up only after a calm spell with room for what the step
saved. The runtime holding the game at every other refresh counts as over budget, and frames
over budget step on while a step is still arriving. The frames after a stall on the main thread (a build laid at
once) draw what it built, not the scene, so the governor starts its averages again rather than judge them. Every headset starts at the cheapest way of
drawing the view with every reach whole (`Quality.START`) and settles on the prettiest step it
holds at its own refresh rate. There are no per-headset presets, nothing reads which headset or
build it runs on, and there is no settings menu.

### One ladder for every headset

Directive (verbatim): "I usually prefer all hardware use the same dynamic quality systems, Branching on IS_QUEST2 is
an antipattern, but fixed foveation and eye tracked foveation are likely legitimately different user experiences. Good
eye-tracked fovation is free ux-wise. I'm thinking eye-tracked and fixed should be treated differently."

**Decision: how the view is drawn is the ladder's top six rungs (`Quality.DRAWN`), not a table per headset.**
Each rung drops what loses least for what it saves, as measured on the Quest 2 at the ladder's bottom ("Which knobs
matter" and "Anti-aliasing on the Quest 2"):

| Step | Swapchain | MSAA | Sky reflections | Fixed foveation | Estimated cost over the next step |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 0 | 1.0 | 4x | on | 0 | 0.25 of the frame |
| 1 | 1.0 | 4x | off | 0 | 0.17 |
| 2 | 1.0 | 4x | off | 3 | 0.31 |
| 3 | 1.0 | 2x | off | 3 | 0.38 |
| 4 | 0.85 | 2x | off | 3 | 0.47 |
| 5, the start | 0.7 | 2x | off | 3 | |

Steps 6 to 15 are the reach, shadow and 3D scale cuts above, drawn as step 5. Step numbers in measurements recorded
before this ladder count from the start: their step N is step N + 5 now. Records made while the ladder had a seventh
rung, the plain in place of the lake (from 5edf8594 until it went), read one higher from their step 6 down; the demo
scripts' pins were moved with each change. Step 5 is what the Quest 2 drew
for its whole session before (its tuned table) with the lake added, so a Quest 2 starts where it was tuned and the
build budget, fitted at the bottom step (`Budget.fit_step`), prices that frame. A headset with room climbs out of it:

- The estimate. A step up across a rung not yet measured in the scene is expected to cost its share of the current
  frame (the last column, from the Quest 2's measurements; the swapchain's is its pixels': first estimated from the
  0.7, 0.8 and 1.0 rows of "Which knobs matter" at 0.27, a governed stress run on the Quest 2 climbed from 0.7 to
  0.85 at 7.3 ms and read 10.7, and stepped back within seven seconds), and is taken only when the average plus that stays under the line. A measured step
  takes its measured cost, as every step does.
- Pops. The steps into 2x MSAA and each smaller swapchain reallocate what the view is drawn into, as the bottom
  steps' shadows and 3D scale do (`Quality.popping`). Above the start the governor crosses them at the calm of any
  step until the scene first asks for less; once a step down has crossed one, a step back up across it waits the
  longest calm (64 s), and one undone within ten seconds is not taken again that session.
- Priming. Among loading's late frames, before the governor starts, the view is drawn for two frames each at the
  bottom step and at every rung whose swapchain, MSAA or reflections differ, so their pipelines are compiled then
  and not mid-play. Changing the render target size multiplier mid-session recreates the swapchain, which the
  Quest 2's runtime takes in its stride (the log's `quality: step` lines and the compositor's swapchain lines).
- MSAA is never off: no rung has fewer than 2x samples, and a test holds every step to it.
- Fixed foveation (`XR_FB_foveation`) is a knob only where the runtime supports it and the eyes are not tracked
  (`is_foveation_supported`, `is_eye_gaze_interaction_supported`); the view's VRS mode is XR only then. Elsewhere
  its rung changes nothing, so the governor passes it by (`Quality.idle`). Foveation that follows tracked eyes, as
  Valve's runtime layer does on the Steam Frame, costs the player nothing to look at, so it stays on whole and is no
  knob of the ladder.

Measured on a Quest 2 against the tuned table it replaced (main before, `d4a9c75`), each one run of the stress
scene ([records](../perf/quest-2/quality-ladder/)); late frames, phases split by whether the tuned build's median
viewport GPU time sat under the governor's line:

| Stress scene | Phases under the line | Phases over it | Whole run | First 30 s |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| As scripted, the quality pinned | 155 → 138 | 10,600 → 10,752 | 17,882 → 18,682 | 119 → 47 |
| Every pin released, governed throughout | 345 → 244 | 7,121 → 7,093 | 8,984 → 8,872 | 122 → 44 |

Two runs of one build on the same headset earlier read 182 and 437 late frames under the line and 12,008 and
11,788 over it, so every change but the start's, which priming moved, is within a run's spread. Governed, the Quest 2
spent 152 s of the run at step 5, the lake standing, 75 s at step 6 and the rest at the ladder's bottom, where the
tuned build sat at its step 0 (step 6 now) for 256 s; it never held a rung above 5, so its fixed foveation stayed at
level 3 and the rim is as sharp as before. With the swapchain first estimated at 0.27 of the frame it climbed to 0.85
and stepped back within seven seconds, one late frame a second meanwhile, which set that estimate to the pixels'.

The lake (see "Look on a mobile budget") stands round the meadow on every headset at every step; the plain it
replaced is gone. It was a rung for a while on a reading of 1.1 ms ([with](../perf/quest-2/edge/20261022T154215Z-5ef92f8.json),
[without](../perf/quest-2/edge/20261022T154823Z-d4a9c75.json)), but those two runs measured different trees (the
quality ladder's work in progress against main, 1,148 primitives apart where the lake is 256). The same tree with
and without it read 0.00 ms standing and +0.14 ms from the air (`perf/quest-2/edge/20261023T03*`). It stays in every build
while it stays that cheap; a reading that puts it near a millisecond again reopens it.

The 3D scale is exactly 1.0 on every step but the last. The Frame's Turnip driver tonemaps more cheaply at any scale
off 1.0 (below), so the Frame no longer gets its 0.9 for the session from a check of its platform; it draws the
swapchain rungs like any other headset.

### Which knobs matter

Measured on the Quest 2 (72 Hz, GPU at its top clock) with each candidate held on its own,
from 22 m up over the bare meadow, where the full-quality game misses refresh. GPU time is
the runtime's `App=` time per frame, the median of 7 seconds:

| Knob, held at | GPU per frame | Frames per second |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Full quality | 14.0 ms | 67 |
| Grass drawn to 13 m instead of 24 m | 12.2 ms | 72 |
| Render resolution 0.5 of each eye's width and height (the OpenXR render region) | 14.0 ms | 67 |
| Render resolution 0.7 | 13.9 ms | 67 |
| Fixed foveation level 3 (`XR_FB_foveation`, never applied: see "Anti-aliasing on the Quest 2") | 13.9 ms | 67 |
| Sun shadows to 20 m instead of 45 m | 13.8 ms | 68 |
| Mesh LOD threshold 4 px instead of 1 | 14.0 ms | 67 |

Standing in the meadow, or floating 8.5 m above it, every knob measured within the
half-millisecond noise. The GPU is bound by vertices, not pixels (see "Flora on the Quest 2"
below for why the render region does not help), so grass reach is the only one of these that
buys time; the others would cost looks for nothing and are not on the ladder. A knob joins it
only with a measured saving. Grass reach changes at no frame cost:
`Flora.grass_reach` hides whole 8 m grass cells beyond it and sets one shader uniform over
whose second half the surviving clumps sink into the ground one at a time, and it moves toward
each new step at 8 m a second, so the meadow thins rather than ends. Ruled out for any later knob
because they reallocate or recompile mid-play: shadow atlas size and toggling glow
(pipeline variants). MSAA level, the render-target multiplier (Godot frees and recreates the
swapchain when it changes), the sun's shadows and `Viewport.scaling_3d_scale` (see below for
the frame it cost on the Frame) are on the ladder only as pops, primed at load ("One ladder for
every headset").

On the Steam Frame the pixels are the cost. The houses phase of `demo/headset.json` (a tower
and a house with two roofs 3 to 10 m from a floating eye) took the game's GPU time from 8.3 ms
a frame to about 12.3 ms of the 13.89 ms period, and the runtime then drops the game to every
other refresh, where it reads 15.5 ms. Held at each knob, unworn with the standby pause off,
GPU time per frame (fdinfo), median of each phase:

| Knob, held at | Before the houses | Houses |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Full quality | 8.4 ms | 15.5 ms, 36 fps |
| Stones and tiles unshaded | 8.2 ms | 10.7 ms |
| Stones and tiles receiving no sun shadow | 8.2 ms | 11.0 ms |
| Stones and roof tiles as 12-triangle boxes | 8.2 ms | 11.3 ms |
| 3D resolution 0.9 (`Viewport.scaling_3d_scale`) | 8.0 ms | 9.5 ms |
| 3D resolution 0.8 | 7.0 ms | 8.5 ms |
| 3D resolution 0.8, grass to 13 m | 6.4 ms | 8.0 ms |
| 3D resolution 0.8, the ladder's bottom step | 5.7 ms | 7.3 ms |

A tenth off the 3D resolution saves more there than every reach step together, at a loss of
sharpness that is hard to see, so the Frame rendered the 3D scene at 0.9 for the whole session,
upscaled into the same swapchain, until the ladder took the swapchain's size instead ("One ladder
for every headset"). It was the ladder's first step
until the step itself proved the cost: changing the scale reallocates the scene's buffers, and
stepping down to 0.9 mid-play cost a 25 to 72 ms frame now and then, which in the houses phase
of `demo/headset.json` took the runtime to every other refresh for a second in two of three
runs (31 late frames each). Switching every 3 s in place gave a late frame at three of six
switches. Held at 0.9 from launch, the phase had none. The OpenXR render region, which shrinks
the rendered rectangle inside the full-size eye texture without reallocating, saved nothing on
the Frame: the houses took 10.7 ms a frame against 9.6 ms at the 0.9 scale, and the later
phases fell to every other refresh. The Quest 2 shrinks the swapchain instead (below).

Part of what a tenth off saves on the Frame is the render path, not the pixels. At a scale of
exactly 1.0 Godot tonemaps in a subpass of the 3D pass; at any other scale, in a pass of its
own. On the Frame's Turnip driver the subpass is the dearer one: at 1.0001, which renders the
same 1728 pixels per eye the separate way, the houses phase of `demo/headset.json` took 10.4
to 10.6 ms a frame on the governor's timer against 11.5 to 11.8 ms at 1.0 (three runs each,
0 late frames at 1.0001), and compositor captures showed the meadow in both eyes. The Quest
2's driver goes the other way: the stress scene at 1.0001 took a median 2.75 ms more App time
a phase than at 1.0, every one of its 34 phases between 1.4 and 3.2 ms more. So a full-resolution
scale on the Frame was 1.0001, never 1.0, and the Quest 2 stayed at exactly 1.0. Which
render path a driver prefers is not something the game asks about, so every step but the last
now draws at exactly 1.0.

The Meta plugin's separate dynamic-resolution controller is disabled: its explicit render
region can exceed the framebuffer once the 3D scale shrinks it, making both eyes
black while timing reports an apparent improvement. The benchmark rejects this framebuffer
error. Resolution changes must be checked against rendered eye images as well as timings.

The Quest 2 settles on a swapchain 0.7 of its own size a side (the ladder's start, OpenXR's
render target size multiplier): 1008x1108 an eye instead of 1440x1584. Its scene is bound by fragments, and a smaller
swapchain is drawn in the same single pass, where a 3D scale off 1.0 adds the tonemap pass this
driver pays for; the compositor stretches it to the display. Measured on a Quest 2 with the
bare meadow at the ladder's bottom step, `App=` ms a frame and the frame time's median:

| Swapchain | From the air | Standing | Frame p50, from the air |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 1.0 | 16.5 | 14.2 | 21.2 ms (every other refresh) |
| 0.8 | 13.3 | 10.6 | 15.6 ms |
| 0.75 | 12.2 | 10.0 | 14.3 ms, 71 late frames |
| 0.7 | 11.2 | 9.3 | 13.9 ms, 12 late frames |
| 1.0 with a 3D scale of 0.8 | 13.6 | 11.2 | 17.0 ms |

0.7 is the largest that holds 72 Hz. At the top step the bare meadow takes 19.4 ms from the air
(28.1 at full size) and every flora pool full 40.4 at the top and 17.8 at the bottom, so the
governor still walks the ladder. The render region is not used, so the eyes cannot go black as
they did under the plugin's controller; the game's view captures at each eye's pixel count show
grass blades softer at 0.7 and the scene otherwise unchanged.

At the start rung every headset also draws 2x MSAA instead of 4x and takes no reflected light from
the sky (`Quality.DRAWN`), and on the Quest 2 the ground reads the light builds give back once a vertex, the
probes lying a grid cell apart as its vertices do. At the ladder's bottom the bare meadow went
from 12.3 ms from the air and 10.5 standing to 6.9 and 6.7 in the governor's signal, so a garden
has 3.8 ms of room under the 11.1 ms line (the then-current Quest 2 table) where it had none. Found by hiding each part of the bare
meadow in turn, each against the unchanged meadow measured between (`bench.sh --android`, step
10): the ground's mesh took 8.7 ms from the air and 4.1 standing, the meadow's plants 1.0 and
3.7, the sun's light 1.1 and 2.0, the mountains, the sun's disc, the hands and the book nothing
measurable. Settings switched while running, from the air and standing:

| Change | From the air | Standing |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 4x MSAA to 2x | -2.5 ms | -2.5 ms |
| MSAA off | -3.5 ms | -2.2 ms |
| No reflected sky light | -0.7 ms | -1.9 ms |
| Ambient light a flat colour instead of the sky's | -0.2 ms | -0.4 to -1.4 ms |
| Fog off | -0.2 ms | -0.2 ms |
| With 2x MSAA and no reflections: the ground a flat colour | -1.5 ms | -0.8 ms |
| ... the ground's paint and pond reads skipped | -0.6 ms | -0.3 ms |
| ... its bounce and lantern reads skipped | -0.35 ms | -0.2 ms |
| ... its detail noise skipped | 0 | 0 |

Nearly every surface is rough, so the sky's reflections only lifted them a little toward its blue:
the view captures show the meadow a shade more saturated and edges a little harder at 2x, and
otherwise the same ([before, after](quest-2-session.jpg)). Ambient light stays the sky's and MSAA
stays on, where the look would change more for the time.

Each step, measured the same way (`demo/ladder.json`), with the flora changes in "Flora on
the Quest 2" below:

| Step | Grass, flowers, blobs | Bare meadow from the air | Every flora pool full, from the air | Every flora pool full, standing |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 0 | 24 m, 60 m, fine | 12.8 ms, 71 fps | 18.5 ms, 51 fps | 17.9 ms, 52 fps |
| 1 | 20 m | 11.9 ms, 72 fps | 17.6 ms, 53 fps | 17.3 ms, 54 fps |
| 2 | 16 m | 11.8 ms, 72 fps | 16.9 ms, 55 fps | 16.7 ms, 56 fps |
| 3 | 13 m | 12.0 ms, 72 fps | 15.5 ms, 60 fps | 15.5 ms, 60 fps |
| 4 | 10 m | 11.8 ms, 72 fps | 15.2 ms, 61 fps | 14.9 ms, 62 fps |
| 5 | 10 m, 20 m | 12.0 ms, 72 fps | 14.1 ms, 65 fps | 14.5 ms, 64 fps |
| 6 | 10 m, 20 m, coarse | 12.2 ms, 72 fps | 12.0 ms, 72 fps | 12.8 ms, 72 fps |

Before them the ladder was steps 0 to 4 alone.

From the air the grass beyond 16 m is a few pixels a blade, so the settled steps are hard to
tell apart from the top. On the Quest 2 dense walls were beyond what the ladder reached: at
973 m of walls it took 42 ms a frame and no knob moved it by more than noise.

On the Steam Frame, the stress scene played governed holds 72 Hz in every phase: see
"Holding 72 Hz on the Frame" for what it took and the table.

The sky's light is read from one radiance map per roughness, not a texture array of them
(`rendering/reflections/sky_reflections/texture_array_reflections=false`, Godot's own default on
Android, so the Quest draws the same). Godot 4.6 moved sky radiance from cube maps to octahedral
maps, and the array form reads two layers per sample with a level of detail from screen-space
derivatives, for the ambient light, the reflections and the fog's aerial perspective. The arm64
Linux export is not a mobile platform to Godot, so it took the array: on the Frame (`frame-unworn`,
the meadow and full flora pools, the GPU time a frame) 4.5.1 drew 10.0 ms, 4.6 13.0 ms, and 4.6
with one map 8.7 ms, the view captures the same.

### Detail by gaze

Directive (verbatim): "I am curious about foviated LOD."

Gaze-driven detail is not built: what it can buy on the Frame is under a millisecond.
Gaze reaches the game on the Frame, natively and under Lepton (`info.eye_gaze` true in
both), and not on the Quest 2 (false). Measured on the Frame with the stress scene,
unworn, the ladder held at full quality and the "gaze" held at the view's centre, each
column one run; two runs of the unchanged build agree to within 0.1 ms on most phases
(median difference 0.00 ms):

| Outside 30° of the gaze | GPU ms a frame saved, median over phases | Least, most |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Nothing instanced drawn: grass, flowers, ivy, trees, bushes, stones, the meadow's cells outside the cone not submitted | 2.0 | 1.0, 7.5 |
| Grass, flowers and ivy not drawn, meadow cells outside the cone not submitted | 1.4 | 0.5, 4.3 |
| Grass thinned to its survivors and flowers and ivy shrunk away, fading in from 30° to 45° | 0.3 | 0.0, 1.6 |

Only the first row clears a millisecond everywhere, and it takes the walls and trees
beside the player out of their peripheral view, which shows. The last row is the one
a player could not see, and on the heaviest phases (walls on a 25 m disc, the village,
the hills from the air) it saves 0.0 to 0.3 ms. Peripheral shading is already the
runtime's foveation layer's; what remains is vertices that are in view whichever way
the eye turns.

### Distant grass and flowers

Past their meshes' reach flowers draw as one camera-facing triangle each, in their
petal colour, and grass as the ground's own colour. `Flora.Pool` derives the far
flowers from the same placement, colour and custom attributes as the near ones;
painting, recycling, mowing, undo and ghost clearing write both, and the far layer is
one batch per pool. `FloraCover` keeps, for each ground vertex, a clump standing in the
square around it, as the factor its tint and shade make of the ground's grass colour;
`terrain.gdshader` reads it once a vertex and multiplies it into the meadow colours
the vertex hands its fragments, as far in as the clumps there have sunk away by
`grass.gdshader`'s LOD band and survivors. Mown, hidden and recycled clumps reach it
within a quarter second; a reset or a filled disc repaints it whole. Full-detail
photos omit both and draw the original meshes.

Measured on the Quest 2 against the bare meadow from the air (6.91 ms with no far
layer, the same run toggling it off and on, or five samples of a phase a build):

| Far grass | Bare meadow | Full grass pool |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Triangle per clump, lit | 8.89 ms | 13.87 ms, misses 72 Hz |
| Triangle per clump, unshaded | 8.06 ms | 13.15 ms |
| Clump, rank and colour painted per fragment | 9.32 ms | 14.16 ms |
| Colour factor read per fragment | 7.25 ms | 12.06 ms, misses 72 Hz |
| Colour factor read per vertex | 7.05 ms | 11.57 ms |

22,000 meadow clump triangles cost 1.9 ms and the 3,200 flower triangles 0.1; with
the far grass shader's output pinned to a point its vertices alone cost 0.5 ms. The
ground's fragments bound the Quest 2, so anything they do costs: the factor read once
a fragment cost 0.2 ms bare and 0.4 under the full pool. A vertex is a metre apart, so
the distant meadow takes each clump's tint a metre across rather than in its own
patch. Writing the grass's sink as a shared include function cost the full pool a
further 0.25 ms (11.57 against 11.29 ms with every far layer off), so `grass.gdshader`
computes it inline.

`demo/flora-cost.json` interleaves the far layer off and on in the same scene;
`flora_far` is a measurement command, not a player setting. `demo/flora-lod.json`
fills the painted grass and flower pools and views the field from far away, at
grass height and nearby.

### Flora on the Quest 2

Every flora pool full, from the air and standing among it (`demo/tour.json`'s flora
phases), missed 72 Hz at every grass step: 16.6 and 15.5 ms at the bottom. Measured on the
device before changing anything, by `App=` time with each part of the scene hidden in turn
and by the Quest's own GPU profiler (`ovrgpuprofiler`):

- The GPU is the limit (`GPU%=1.00` in the runtime's line, the CPU under 15% busy).
- One frame, read with the profiler's render-stage trace: the shadow map's passes take
  1.6 ms; the scene renders in one 4× MSAA 1440×1584 surface split into 135 bins of
  96×176 pixels, taking 4.1 ms to bin (every submitted vertex runs once there, whatever
  the shader later does with it), 9.3 ms to render the bins and 1.5 ms to store them. The
  OpenXR render region leaves that surface and its bins as they are, which is why render
  resolution moved nothing.
- Per frame, at the bottom grass step, hiding each part saved: painted grass 1.9 ms from
  the air and 1.8 ms standing, the flowers (painted and the meadow's) 2.6 and 1.5 ms, the
  planted trees 2.6 and 1.8 ms, every tree's shadow 1.5 and 1.0 ms.
- Halving the grass vertex shader's arithmetic saved nothing; lighting it per vertex saved
  1 ms at full quality and nothing at the bottom; drawing it unlit saved 4.3 ms. Draw calls
  are not the cost: 32 or 48 m cells instead of 16 m halved them and saved nothing.

So the cost is vertices (binning) and the raster of thin slivers, not shading arithmetic
or draws. What now runs at every step, each measured against the build without it on the
same device in the same session:

| Change | From the air | Standing |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Far grass drawn as 3 widened blades of 7 (row 12 of the grass table below) | 0.6 ms (bare meadow 1.1 ms) | 1.3 ms |
| Foliage blobs from a twice-split icosphere (162 vertices) instead of a 20 × 10 latitude-longitude sphere (252) | 1.2 ms | 0.9 ms |

The ladder's two new steps, measured at the bottom grass step, where the rest is spent:

| Step | From the air | Standing |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Flowers to 20 m instead of 60 m | 1.4 ms | 0.2 ms |
| Then blobs from a once-split icosphere (42 vertices) | 1.9 ms | 1.1 ms |

Flowers come first: beyond 20 m they are a few pixels, while coarse blobs show on every
tree. A flower's reach works as grass reach does: the meadow's flower cells beyond it are
not drawn and flowers shrink to their roots over its last 4 m; painted flowers and a
stroke's ghost shrink alike. Painted plants draw in patches, one per pool and cell square, each boxed
around the plants it holds, so the camera culls a patch off screen and the reach takes one wholly past
it out of view: a 2,000-clump patch behind or beside the eye from 4 m out, or past the reach, cost the
Quest 2 0.09 to 0.11 µs a clump and now measures within noise of nothing, and an 800-flower patch 0.3 to
0.5 µs a flower anywhere and now within noise past 12 m or out of view
([before](../perf/quest-2/flora-distance/20261024T104945Z-5148129.json),
[after](../perf/quest-2/flora-distance/20261024T165227Z-d3fbae0.json)). With every painted pool full
over the stress scene's 20 m disc, flora/aerial fell from 14.6-15.3 to 13.6 ms and flora/ground from
12.3 to 11.8. Both reaches move at 8 m a second. The blob detail changes by
swapping the mesh the foliage's MultiMeshes draw, kept for both details from the start:
switching it back and forth six times over 12 s, from the air, left the slowest frame of
each second at 14.8 to 16.7 ms, as in the seconds around the switches: switching causes no stall.

Measured and left out: drawing both blob details in one mesh and dropping one per vertex by distance
(0.7 ms, where the twice-split icosphere alone saves 1.2: dropped vertices are still binned);
lighting flowers or leaves per vertex, shrinking flower stalks beyond 8 m, and sun shadows
to 25 m (each within noise); grass lit per vertex (near blades lose the rounded shading
of row 3 of the grass table). MSAA, which sets the bin size, stays at 4×:
fewer samples would buy time at the cost of edges.

Measuring this, one trap. The headset's system shell keeps
rendering beside the game while a Store or Library panel is open, or, unworn, while the
Guardian's dialog is up, taking GPU time the game's own `App=` line does not show.
`bench.sh --android` closes the panels, restarts the shell and holds the Guardian paused
before each run, and fails if another process renders during a measured phase.

### Cheaper flora

Directive (verbatim): "It is worth trying to optimize flowers and grass even more, even if it makes them look a little
different. Also, it's worth I'd like to try to optimize trees and bushes some more."

Measured on a Quest 2 at the fit step, the meadow from the air and standing (`demo/flora-layers.json`, read by
`scripts/layers.py`), each build run twice and the builds interleaved
([records](../perf/quest-2/flora-layers/)). Milliseconds a frame; "hidden" columns are what hiding that layer alone
saves:

| Build | View | Meadow shown | Trees and bushes hidden | Grass hidden | Flowers hidden | Everything hidden |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Before | from the air | 7.20 | 2.15 | 0.37 | 0.50 | 6.07 |
| Before | standing | 6.59 | 0.76 | 2.39 | 0.82 | 5.52 |
| One MultiMesh a tree pool | from the air | 6.59 | 1.21 | 0.44 | 0.49 | 5.46 |
| One MultiMesh a tree pool | standing | 6.32 | 0.47 | 2.31 | 1.05 | 5.27 |
| Then grass and flowers | from the air | 6.40 | 1.69 | 0.37 | 0.47 | 5.30 |
| Then grass and flowers | standing | 5.79 | 1.28 | 1.86 | 1.28 | 4.74 |
| Then bounce a vertex (main) | from the air | 6.28 | 1.46 | 0.50 | 0.58 | 5.13 |
| Then bounce a vertex (main) | standing | 5.57 | 1.00 | 1.72 | 1.08 | 4.49 |

The meadow shown is the number to read: 0.92 ms cheaper from the air and 1.02 standing. A single layer's saving
depends on what else is drawn, so hiding one does not split the whole: the trees' share standing rose when only the
grass changed.

- **One MultiMesh a tree pool** (image unchanged). The meadow's trunks, crowns and bushes each bake into one
  MultiMesh, not one per 16 m square (`Flora.SOLID_POOL_CELL`): 51 fewer draws from the air, 21 standing. The Quest 2
  pays 7 to 19 µs a draw whatever it holds: hiding the 46 trunks (19 draws, 1,600 triangles) saved 0.36 ms from the
  air, and the 60 bushes (24 draws, 4,600 triangles) 0.36. One MultiMesh a pool saved 0.35 and 0.37 ms from the air
  and 0.20 and 0.33 standing in two runs; 32 m squares saved 0.30 and 0.11.
- **Grass**: a clump draws five blades 1.4 times as wide instead of seven (`Flora.CLUMP_BLADES`, `CLUMP_WIDE`), the
  far clump's three kept blades as wide as before. Near grass reads as fewer, broader blades.
- **Flowers**: four petals instead of six (`Flora.PETALS`), and land flowers hand off to their far patches from 12 m
  instead of 18 (`Flora.FLOWER_NEAR`). Heads read as four-pointed stars; the band from 12 to 18 m shows colour patches
  a little sooner.
- **Bounce a vertex** (image nearly unchanged): plants read bounce and lantern light once a vertex from the ground they
  stand on (`given_over`), as grass does, not once a fragment: 0.17 ms from the air and 0.27 standing in two
  interleaved pairs.

Before (left) and after (right), from the air, standing, and standing looking a little down: [cheaper-flora.jpg](cheaper-flora.jpg).

Measured and left as options (savings from one interleaved run, from the air and standing): blobs from a bare
icosahedron (0.29, 0.13; visibly faceted crowns); four blades a clump instead of seven unwidened (0.14, 0.37; visibly
thinner grass). Swapping a material's shader at run time, even for an identical copy, cost 0.25 to 1.3 ms on the Quest 2
for as long as it lasted, so shader changes are measured only as separate builds.

Prices refitted on the after build ([costs](../perf/quest-2/costs/20261022T104636Z-eda9e72.json),
[pondweed](../perf/quest-2/pondweed/)): a tree 26.628 µs and a bush 4.712, both from the costs fit, against the 28.491 and
5.01 fitted on the build before them.

Grass and flowers are priced on 3eb6468, from both scenes' packed discs in two interleaved pairs of runs on one Quest 2 (costs
[1](../perf/quest-2/costs/20261027T003828Z-3eb6468.json), pondweed
[1](../perf/quest-2/pondweed/20261027T004226Z-3eb6468.json), costs
[2](../perf/quest-2/costs/20261027T010252Z-3eb6468.json), pondweed
[2](../perf/quest-2/pondweed/20261027T010638Z-3eb6468.json)), each over an empty view of the same spot, App time:

| Packed 1.8 m disc | View | Empty | Full | Per plant, runs 1 / 2 |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Grass, 9,000 clumps (costs) | standing over it | 5.37 / 5.34 ms | 9.83 / 9.92 ms | 0.496 / 0.509 µs |
| Grass, 9,000 clumps (pondweed) | standing over it | 5.62 / 5.63 ms | 10.94 / 10.68 ms | 0.591 / 0.561 µs |
| Grass, 9,000 clumps (pondweed) | from the bed's height | 5.40 / 5.42 ms | 9.68 / 9.77 ms | 0.476 / 0.483 µs |
| Flowers, 4,000 (costs) | standing over it | 5.37 / 5.34 ms | 10.60 / 10.72 ms | 1.307 / 1.345 µs |
| Flowers, 4,000 (pondweed) | from 3.2 m off | 5.95 / 5.96 ms | 12.34 / 12.69 ms | 1.597 / 1.682 µs |
| Irises, 4,000 (pondweed) | from the shore | 6.16 / 6.16 ms | 10.90 / 11.43 ms | 1.185 / 1.317 µs |

Each plant unit is charged its dearest view: a grass clump 0.591 µs and a flower 1.682 (`Budget.COSTS`). Every view
here keeps the frame rate (median frames 13.9 to 14.8 ms), so the costs fit takes its packed discs too. The whole 12,000-clump pool packed into the
disc does not: there the frame rate halves and the GPU time read more than doubles, which is what the 1.7 to 2.5 µs a
clump charged before measured. Packed beside the eye on this build
([record](../perf/quest-2/grass-count/20261027T000528Z-3eb6468.json),
[record](../perf/quest-2/grass-count/20261027T000819Z-3eb6468.json)), over 4.84 to 5.17 ms empty:

| Clumps | 2,000 | 3,000 | 4,000 | 6,000 | 7,000 | 8,000 | 10,000 | 12,000 |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| GPU ms | 6.31 | 6.90 | 7.27 | 8.24 | 9.42 | 9.48 | 10.68 | 26.75, at 36 Hz |

`scripts/paired.py --price` refuses a phase whose median frame took over 1.5 refresh periods, as the fit leaves such
views out. Trees and bushes keep their charges.

### Crowns

A crown is lit and shaded as one soft mass, at no measured cost
([before, then each step in turn](crowns.jpg)):

- Each foliage blob keeps its sphere's normals, so light wraps the blob and its lumps show
  only in its outline (`Meshes.blob`).
- Normals then lean out from the trunk (`CROWN_ROUND` in `plant.gdshaderinc`), so the faces
  between a crown's blobs light as its outside does.
- A blob's underside takes a cool tint (`CROWN_SHADE`), so the shade under a crown reads cool.
- Each blob sways as one in the wind every plant shares, by its height above the ground
  (`CROWN_GIVE`), so the crown sways rather than ripples.
- The blobs cast no shadow. Each standing tree or bush casts its shade through one
  once-split icosphere fitted to the box around its blobs (`Flora.crown_shade`), drawn only
  into the shadow map. The ground keeps the crown's shade ([before, after](crown-shade.jpg)),
  blobs no longer shade one another in patches, and the shade stays the same shape when the
  ladder changes blob detail.
- That blob casts from a lump's depth (`Meshes.BLOB_LUMP`) behind its sunlit surface, along
  the sun's rays. Its lumps are not the drawn blobs' lumps, and cast where they stand they laid
  hatching and stair-steps a shadow-map texel wide across the crown's lit side, changing as the
  head moved. Moved along the rays, the shade on the ground keeps its shape. On the Steam Frame
  (fixed head, wind stilled, shadows-off control) this removed the stair-steps and about 40% of
  the shadow-made hatching on a side-lit crown. GPU time a frame: 7.69 ms both before and after
  close under a crown, 6.79 then 6.87 ms looking over the meadow.
- The sun's shadows take one hardware-filtered sample (`soft_shadow_filter_quality` 0), as
  Godot's Android default already gave the Quests. The soft filter rotated its samples per
  pixel, and the crown's caster lies within the filter's reach of the crown over a wide band,
  so that band took the rotation as a fixed diagonal grain one to two pixels wide. Hiding the
  caster left the crown exactly as with shadows off; backlight off, normal bias and a deeper
  caster did not remove the grain short of losing the crown's own shade, more blur strengthened
  it, and one sample removed it. On the Steam Frame (side-lit crown, both eyes, shadows-off control) the grain the
  shadows add at the finest scale fell from 0.19 and 0.14 to 0.05 and 0.03, all of what is
  left on the shade's own edge; off that edge the crown matches the control.
  GPU time a frame on the Frame (`demo/crowns-bench.json`, two runs each) fell from 9.76, 8.43
  and 9.56 ms to 9.35, 8.04 and 9.16 ms; on the Quest 2, which already filtered so, it held at
  7.65, 6.69 and 8.61 ms.

GPU time a frame on the Quest 2 (`demo/crowns-bench.json`: the meadow and the tree pool
filled to 20 m, ladder pinned; two passes of each build in one session, mean of the two),
each build adding one step to the last:

| Build | Bottom step, from the air | Bottom step, standing | Top step, standing |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Before | 41.9 ms | 29.0 ms | 33.2 ms |
| Sphere normals and sway | 41.2 | 28.7 | 32.8 |
| Cool undersides | 41.1 | 28.7 | 33.0 |
| Normals out from the trunk | 41.5 | 28.9 | 32.8 |
| One shadow caster a crown | 41.2 | 28.6 | 32.1 |

Runs of one build differed by up to 0.5 ms, so the shading steps cost nothing measurable.
The shade casters cut what the shadow pass draws: 117 draws instead of 150 and 291k
primitives instead of 350k from the air at the bottom step, 467k instead of 703k standing
at the top, about 1 ms there.

### One signal, many knobs

The signal is the GPU time of the frame the viewport just rendered
(`RenderingServer.viewport_get_measured_render_time_gpu`, GPU timestamps around the
viewport's passes): the same on every headset and blind to CPU hitches, which no graphics
knob would cure. On the Quest 2 it reads about 1 ms above the runtime's `App=` time, and
frames hold 72 Hz until it reaches the whole refresh period. On the Steam Frame it reads about
0.3 ms under the fdinfo time, and the runtime drops the game to every other refresh once that
nears 0.88 of the period, staying there for seconds after the frames have room again, so the
line is 0.80 of the period, under 0.88 by that gap and the average's lag behind a build. The
rate is read from the runtime every frame, since it reports the shell's rate for the first
frames of a session. Any later knob takes its place on the same ladder, cheapest loss per
millisecond first, so one signal still maps to one position.

The governor (`Quality.Governor`) is Valve's adaptive quality with slower recovery:

- Down fast: two consecutive frames over 105% of the period step down one; frames a
  quarter over step down two. Six frames then pass unjudged while the timer catches up,
  and none is judged until grass and flowers have reached their new step.
- Down before frames run late: the frame time averaged over one second over the line steps
  down one. The average starts again after each step, and the first second after it only
  measures what the step saved.
- Up slowly: two seconds with the frame time, averaged over one second, under the line by
  what the step up costs step up one, so a step up does not take the frame time back over
  the line it left. A step's cost is the change in that average across a step between the
  two levels, either way; a step out of every other refresh reads no saving, so a
  measurement only raises it. A step not yet measured is taken to cost 0.04 of the period,
  half the gap to the Frame's cliff: taken as nothing, the full garden from the air stepped
  up from 10.8 ms over the cliff and sat at every other refresh for six seconds. Single
  frames vary by more than a step saves, so they neither make nor break a calm spell. After
  64 seconds under the line the cost is forgotten, unmeasured again: the scene has changed
  since.
- No oscillation: a step down within ten seconds of the step up that reached it doubles the
  calm needed to step up into that step again, up to 64 seconds, for the session.
- The first five seconds of rendered frames are not judged: loading's frames are not the
  scene's cost.

Valve's The Lab drops two levels on a single frame over 90% of the refresh period and climbs
one after three frames under 70%, but its levels only change resolution, which does not
show frame to frame. A step of grass reach does show, so climbing waits for seconds rather
than frames.

`--quality N` and the driver's `quality` op hold the ladder at step N, for measuring. Each
second of `metrics.json` records the lowest step the ladder took (`step`), the viewport's
GPU time per frame (`viewport_gpu_ms`) and the refresh period (`budget_ms`).

### On the Quest 2

`demo/tour.json` walks the meadow from the ground and the air, then every flora pool full
and walls 2 m apart over a 10 m disc. Played governed on the Quest 2 (runtime `FPS=` and
`App=` per second, the GPU at its top clock), with the ladder of grass reach alone and with
the ladder and flora as they now stand:

| Phase | Grass reach alone | Now |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Meadow, standing | 72 fps, 12.0 ms (step 0) | 72 fps, 11.9 ms (step 0) |
| Meadow, from the air | 72 fps, 12.8 ms at step 3 | 72 fps, 12.7 ms at step 0 |
| Every flora pool full, from the air | 56 fps, 16.6 ms at step 4 | 72 fps, 12.1 ms at step 6 |
| Every flora pool full, standing | 60 fps, 15.5 ms at step 4 | 72 fps, 12.6 ms at step 6 |
| Walls over a 10 m disc, from the air | 38 fps, 24.9 ms at step 4 | 48 fps, 19.5 ms at step 6 |

Over the whole tour 2,536 frames missed before and 1,257 now. Held at each step (the table
above), full flora holds 72 Hz only at the bottom, so on the Quest 2 it settles there.

The play area on the Quest 2 resolves to LOCAL space when no boundary is set, as when the
headset lies unworn after a restart; `metrics.json` records it (`info.play_area`) with the
head's height each second (`head_m`).

Worn on the Steam Frame the viewport's GPU timer reads 0.15 ms under the kernel's fdinfo
time for the game process (see "Frame measurements").

Sources: Alex Vlachos, "Advanced VR Rendering Performance", GDC 2016
(https://gdcvault.com/play/1023522/Advanced-VR-) and the adaptive quality code it describes
in Valve's The Lab Renderer (https://github.com/ValveSoftware/the_lab_renderer,
`ValveCamera.cs`); Unity's `XRSettings.renderViewportScale`, which renders into part of the
eye texture so resolution can change without reallocating it
(https://docs.unity3d.com/ScriptReference/XR.XRSettings-eyeTextureResolutionScale.html);
Meta's dynamic resolution, which scales the rendered viewport between a minimum and maximum
by GPU utilisation (https://developers.meta.com/horizon/documentation/unity/dynamic-resolution-unity/).

## Look on a mobile budget

The original's look rests on soft global illumination, warm sun, painterly
colour and dense, touchable detail. On a mobile GPU in stereo at the headset's refresh rate we get it by:

- **Light:** one directional light with soft two-split shadows: the warm sun by day, the cool moon by
  night, handed over in the dark of twilight so the swap is unseen; sky ambient
  from a hand-tuned sky shader (zenith-to-horizon gradient, sun and moon glow, stars at night, painted noise
  clouds); a probe volume of the light the builds give back (see "Bounce light"
  below) on top of baked per-instance occlusion (lower courses darker); wrap-lambert
  diffuse for soft terminators; foliage backlight for the glowing-leaf look.
- **Atmosphere:** depth fog with aerial perspective and sun scatter, tinted towards
  the sky, so distant hills fade and the near meadow stays saturated.
- **Horizon:** the ground's own mesh runs a bank `Terrain.BANK` out from every point of the meadow's edge, read
  from the heights as they stand so it follows the ground as it is reshaped, down to `Terrain.BANK_FOOT`; from that
  foot, vertex for vertex, a lake bed runs out under the water to two rings of stone-faced mountains, 300 to 2100 m
  out (`Mountains`): one indexed mesh of about 8k triangles in one
  draw, its top rows cut finer (`Mountains.DETAIL`) so the skyline breaks into crags, its normals smoothed across every fold gentler than `Mountains.CREASE` so the slopes
  shade softly while ridges and crags keep their edge, unshaded, lit and hazed in its own
  shader from the hour's light, casting no shadow.
  They are real geometry far enough off that both eyes and every step through the garden
  see them as distant. The ridges dip where the sun crosses them low, so it rises and sets
  through a pass. The eyes' far clip (`Rig.FAR`) and the sun's disc stand beyond them.
  A lake stands round the meadow on every headset: water stands at `Mountains.LAKE`
  over the bank's foot out past the first ridge, and the ranges' feet sink
  `Mountains.SUNK` under it, so the water floods their lower slopes and meets them along a
  contour. The water is one opaque, unshaded surface of 256 triangles (`lake.gdshader`) that
  mirrors the ranges without drawing them twice: each fragment follows its reflected ray to
  each range's circle and compares its climb with the ridge there, read from a 512-texel
  ring of ridge heights (`Mountains.ridge_image`). `demo/edge.json` measured it against the
  plain it replaced from the garden and from the air: on a Quest 2 (`perf/quest-2/edge/20261023T03*`, two
  rounds of each build, GPU ms a frame) it adds 256 primitives and one draw, and costs 0.00 ms
  standing (10.66 both) and +0.14 ms from 22 m up (11.02 to 11.16).
- **Sun shadows:** one stabilized orthogonal map reaches 256 m, covering the garden
  without a fade inside it. Godot's Mobile renderer selects cascades and fades
  shadows by view depth: a fixed distant tree used to lose its shade when looked at
  directly, then regain it at the periphery. That map's texels are 21 cm, too coarse
  for a rock to shade itself or the ground, so the engine
  (`scripts/engine.patch`) selects cascades and fades by distance from the
  eye instead, and there a second, sharp cascade covers the first 10 m. Turning the
  head moves nothing across a boundary; walking moves the boundary with the eye.
  An engine without that setting keeps the single map. See
  [the rendered yaw check](shadows.md).
- **Floating viewpoints:** fog follows the eye's height above the ground
  (`Look.fog_at_height`). It begins and ends farther out from above, so the garden
  is not greyed by air a standing eye would not look through. Shadows keep the same
  map settings while the head moves or turns.
- **Eye adaptation:** exposure follows the sky the eye sees, read on the CPU from the probe volume
  (`Bounce.sky_at`, `Look.adapt`): under a roof it rises toward 1.8 times the garden's over about 0.7 s,
  so a house's inside comes up out of the dark once entered and its doorway glows, and it falls back
  on the way out. One texel read a frame and no GPU pass. The eye adapts to the night too (`Look.night_adapted`, a
  per-build option, `Look.NIGHT_ADAPT`): under a sky darker than 0.12 luminance the exposure rises with the dark, up
  to 4 times, so in the moonless hours before dawn (00:24 to 05:18) the builds still read while the sky, stars and
  meadow stay night-dark; with the moon or the sun up it changes nothing. It is set with the hour, nothing a frame.
  On the desktop renderer at 02:24 the view of a house and a wall averages 0.2 of 255 without it, 4.0 with it.
- **Grading:** AgX tonemapping, mild saturation and contrast lift. No glow: its
  extra passes cost the Quest 2 9 ms of its 13.9 (see "Quest 2 measurements").
- **Detail:** everything repeated is a MultiMesh: stones, grass clumps, flowers,
  foliage. Stones are rounded, lumpy boxes with per-stone colour, so walls read as
  individual pieces. Grass is geometry (no alpha test) to stay cheap on tiled GPUs;
  see "Grass" below.

### Bounce light

A low-resolution probe volume stands in for global illumination (`Bounce`). The
play area, 160 m square, has one probe per metre in 12 layers a metre apart that
follow the ground, shared with shaders as a 160×160×12 RGBA8 3D texture
(`bounce_volume`, `bounce.gdshaderinc`). Alpha is how much sky a probe sees past
the builds around it. It scales ambient light only (`AO`), so in shade the feet of
walls, inner corners and the inside of a house darken over about a metre, while
sunlit ground keeps its sun. RGB is sunlight thrown back by the sunlit stone faces
a probe sees, added as emission: the surface's own colour times the sun's colour
and strength (`bounce_light`). A shaded face across from a sunlit one warms up.

The builds are read as a height field: per one-metre column, the bottom and top
of the stones and roof tiles over it and their mean colour, taken from the pieces
each build places (`Masonry`, kinds marked `occludes`). Walls, towers and houses
stand straight up, so a probe's view becomes, in each of 8 directions walked 6
columns out, the bands of elevation the columns hide. A build standing on another
can overhang it, as a tower on a wall does; the air under the overhang keeps its
sky rather than reading as solid stone down to the ground, which had left the
shaded foot of such a wall black. A face counts as sunlit when a walk of 12 columns
toward the sun passes the builds in between at the face's mid-height, over or
under them. A column hanging over a probe, a roof over a floor, is seen as its
whole box, so a roof's columns close into one, and the probe's own column counts
where it hangs over it: inside a house the probes see almost no sky except through
its doorways, and the floor by a door sees more than the back of the room. What
hangs shows a probe only its shaded underside. When a build settles,
changes or goes, when the sun moves, or when the ground is reshaped, the probes
that could see the change are solved again on a worker thread, and the main
thread only uploads the finished layers. Reading each column's bottom as well as its top
costs a solve about an eighth: the same height field solved by the top-only reading and by the
span reading, in turn on one device, took 117 and 131 ms on the Steam Frame and 260 and 295 ms
on the Quest 2, the bottom tested at every column a probe passes, on every layer. On the desktop the densest stress scene
(973 m of walls, about 40,000 stones) solves in under 0.7 s. While the sun is dragged the bounce follows a solve behind
it. Walls still being drawn or pulled are left out until they settle.

The same footprint, roof and openings used by `Lamps.Room` also bound the sky and
bounced sunlight in covered rooms. Each opening contributes its projected area
with inverse-square falloff into the room; sealed rooms contribute none. Boundary
probes include half a cell around the shell so linear filtering does not bring the
outside probe straight onto its inner face. Over the roof they reach a full
layer further: a gable or ceiling face reads half a metre out between layers a
metre apart, so any probe less than a layer over the roof would bring the sky
onto it. A roof's covering (tiles, thatch) reads the volume over that band instead,
so its outer face keeps the sky. Joined rooms share their openings;
a joined roofless courtyard keeps the height-field solution. This runs on the
bake worker and adds no texture or shader sample.

A roof's covering (tiles, thatch) casts its shadow from boxes pulled half a metre
toward the light (`roof_shadow.gdshader`), and its own faces take their shadows
from as far out, so it shades itself as before. Without the pull the sun's shadow
depth bias, sized against acne on the tiles, let light through onto the gable and
wall tops lying within the bias of the roof over them; a lower bias instead
speckles the tiles at a low sun. A face less than half a metre over the roof
toward the light, such as a chimney's foot, takes the roof's shadow.

Probes up among a roof's tiles cannot tell its inside from its outside, so plaster
reads lower: the lime torching under a pitched roof's tiles takes the probe on the
floor below it, and a plaster gable's face the air a metre before it, no higher
than 1.5 m over the ground. The torching is one face thick and faces down, so it
casts shadows from both faces; cast from its front only, the sun would come
through the gaps between the tiles.

![Inside a house with an arched door at four hours, morning to dusk (rows): before and after these, looking along the walls (first two columns) and up at the roof (last two)](interior.jpg)

Its cost on the Quest 2, from `scripts/bench.sh --android` on the stress scene with
and without this pass (the ladder held at the top, 23 phases from the meadow
through the ducks): a median of 1.3 ms more GPU time a frame. The open meadow
costs 0.2 ms more standing and 1.0 ms more from the air, so shadows stretched for
the air are a suspect, as is the stone shader reading the ground's height again
in every vertex; the epic tracks finding and cutting it.

Every lit surface built or grown on the ground reads it: stone, roof tiles and
plaster, the ground, grass, flowers and trees. Each finds its texel in its vertex
shader and samples once per fragment; the ground and grass take the lowest layer
without reading the ground's height again. Tested headless in
`tests/test_bounce.gd`; `demo/interior.json` renders the inside of a house with
an arched door at four hours, and `demo/lighting.json` renders a tower, walls with an arch
and a house from the ground, from 9 m up and from 25 m up, in late afternoon
light.

![The mood target, then the garden without and with the probe volume and the height-following shadows and fog: from the ground (top), 9 m up (middle) and 25 m up (bottom)](lighting.jpg)

The mood target (left) is a generated painting of the light wanted, made for
this comparison. Without the probe volume and the height-following shadows and
fog (middle) and with them (right; ivy grows differently each run): the inside
of the house through its arch and the corner where wall meets tower go dark, the
wall's foot darkens, and from 25 m up the garden is no longer greyed by fog while
the hills beyond still fade. Still short of the target: its warmer, golden
late-afternoon light and blue distant haze belong to the time-of-day colours
(`Daylight`), not to this pass.

### Sun inside rooms

Inside a house or tower the sun and moon arrive only through its openings (`Interior`, `interior.gdshaderinc`). The
shell's shadow boxes leave seams a shadow-map texel wide at every joint, where the walls meet the roof and the floor,
and the ladder's lowest steps draw no sun shadows at all, which lit a whole room; tuning the bias only moves the seams.
So the shell's shaders (stone, its floors, woodwork and ceilings, plaster, torching and thatch) ask, per point, whether
