the way to the light leaves its room through a door, window, arch or dormer window, and scale the direct light by the
answer whatever the shadow map holds; the shadow map still shapes the patch that does come in.

The rooms are `Lamps.Room`'s, sent as two textures: `room_field`, over the play area every quarter metre, how far
inside its walls' centre line a point lies, the floor and ceiling there and its group's row, remade when the rooms
change; and `room_data`, a row per group of the openings facing the light (`Interior.crossing`), remade when the light
moves too. Each opening is stored as the light crosses it from inside: across and up from the middle of its sill as two
affine functions of the point, narrowed by how far the ray runs through the wall's depth so a deep reveal narrows a low
sun's patch, its rounded head, and the plane a point must stand inside. The centre line divides in from out, so a
wall's inner face is in its room and its outer face is not, however its stones are lumped. Each vertex reads the field
once; a fragment whose triangle lies wholly outside reads nothing, one wholly inside walks only the facing openings,
and only a triangle across the centre line reads the field again. Godot scales the sun by
`mix(1, AO, AO_LIGHT_AFFECT)` and ambient light by `AO`: where the sun reaches at least as far as the sky does
(everywhere outside a room) both stay exact; inside, where it reaches less, `AO` is the sun's share and the rest of the
sky's light Bounce gives the point comes back as emission in the sky's colours. Window glass casts no shadow, so the sun through a window lands on the floor.

![Inside a stone, a plaster and a timber house and a tower (column pairs, before and after) at four hours, morning to evening (rows), with the sun's shadows off as the ladder's lowest steps draw them; brightened three times](interior-sun-off.jpg)

![The same with the sun's shadows on](interior-sun-on.jpg)

`demo/interior-sun.json` renders these houses at the four hours, and `demo/interior-cost.json` measures the rooms on a
headset against the same views unmasked (the driver's `interior` layer). Bright lines still left along a timber house's
wall top and a gable's rake are the sunlit eaves outside, seen through gaps in the shell, not light on the inside.
`tests/test_interior.gd` checks the packing and an archway's patch, and `tests/gpu_interior_sun.gd` a sealed room with
the sun's shadows on and off.

Its cost on the Quest 2 (`demo/interior-cost.json`, the rooms against the same views unmasked, three pairs each,
GPU ms a frame, [record](../perf/quest-2/interior-cost/20261030T014740Z-4703714.json)): looking round a stone house
from inside, 9.71 against 8.40, +1.31; the village from outside, 8.40 against 7.96, +0.44. Earlier forms cost more: a
custom `light()` reading the rooms' shapes per fragment +2.5 and +0.9, and the shapes tested per vertex from the
openings' texture +1.5 for that test alone.

### Lantern light

Directive (verbatim): "I am inside of a house right now, and it's dark. So I added lights. But those lights did not illuminate the ground inside of the house, as I would have expected"

Lantern light is baked into a second volume on the bounce probes (`Lamps`, `lamp_volume`): per probe, the
light of every lantern within 9 m in its colour and strength, fading with distance as Godot fades an omni light. Shaders add it with the
bounce (`given_back` in `bounce.gdshaderinc`), so it costs one more read of a small texture where they already
read one, and nothing more however many lanterns hang. A worker solves it again when a lantern is hung or taken
or the ground moves. A house or tower is a room to it: the air inside its walls' inner faces and under its roof.
A lantern in a room lights its connected rooms. An exterior lantern reaches a room through its openings,
with directional spill fading over 3 m; solid walls block it. Each window and doorway shines outward and down with the light the room has at it, fading over 3 m
and brightest straight out from a point half a metre before its face, so it lights the ground below more than the
wall around it and never the eaves; a doorway lets out a fifth. Surfaces read the volume a metre out (`lamp_over`), so a face of the shell
reads the air on its own side, and a pane of glass also shows the light a metre behind it.

A lit window's pane and outward spill share `Lamps.WINDOW_GLOW`. The pane is opaque glass, not a view
into the room: it emits the light sampled behind it, so a lit house remains recognizable beside an outdoor
lantern's flame. The multiplier changes neither the room's light nor a doorway's spill, and adds no light,
mesh or texture read. `demo/window-glow.json` compares the unlit and lit room, nearby outdoor lanterns,
and the house from across the meadow. It brightens no house without an interior lantern.
The paired Quest 2 run in `perf/window-glow/quest-2.json` measures a median GPU difference of +0.09 ms
across nine views (11–13 runtime samples per phase per build), ranging from -0.78 to +1.02 ms.
Both builds exceed the frame budget in this scene; these paired differences include unisolated variation,
and establish neither zero cost nor a fix for that existing budget excess.

Holding the light in rooms cost the Frame nothing measurable (stress scene, GPU ms a frame, before and after):
9.43 and 9.46 with lanterns hung standing, 19.18 and 19.20 from the air, 9.39 and 9.40 in the same garden
standing before its lanterns are hung.
On the Quest 2 (one run of each build, the runtime's GPU ms a frame): 43.12 and 43.73 with lanterns hung standing,
76.26 and 79.33 from the air; the same garden before its lanterns are hung, 43.23 and 43.89 standing, 76.05 and
79.25 from the air. The unlit garden moved as much as the lit one, so none of that shift is the rooms' light.

Godot's own omni lights were the first try and are gone. Three of them, moved to the lanterns nearest the head,
reached only 4.5 m, so a lantern hung 3 m up a tall house's wall left its floor dark; given 9 m, they cost the
Quest 2 milliseconds of GPU time a frame for every surface they touched. Measured on the Quest 2 (`bench.sh`,
the stress scene's garden walls with 9 lanterns hung, then 123, against none, GPU ms a frame, quality step 0):

| Light | Ground, none | Ground, lit | Aerial, none | Aerial, lit |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 3 omni lights, 4.5 m | 52.9 | 71.1 | 70.4 | 84.6 |
| 3 omni lights, 9 m | 48.8 | 74.5 | 65.9 | 93.2 |
| Baked (`Lamps`), 9 lanterns | 50.0 | 50.2 | 67.5 | 67.3 |
| Baked (`Lamps`), 123 lanterns | 49.0 | 49.6 | 67.7 | 68.8 |

Lanterns hung with their light switched off measured 48.8 and 48.9 standing, 66.4 and 66.7 from the air: the
rest of each delta above is the light. The stress scene's `lanterns` phases hang them along every wall.

On the Frame (same scene, 94 lanterns, GPU ms a frame) the volume read per pixel cost the meadow 3 ms standing
(10.7 to 14.2) while lanterns hung with the volume left empty cost 0.6; grass now reads its light once a
vertex, and with it the meadow drew 9.6 standing with or without lanterns, 17.7 and 17.9 from the air.

Report (verbatim): "I just took a picture of some ivy at night that is not lit properly. Despite there being a lantern right next to it, the ivy is dark."

Report (verbatim): "Check to make sure that wildlife is properly lit too. This conure looks kind of dark, despite being in the light of a lantern."

A shader that never reads the volume takes no lantern light at all. Ivy, the animals, flags and canvases did
not, so at night they stood black beside lit stone. They now read it once a vertex (`given_at`), as grass does: a
leaf, an animal, a flag or a canvas is too small for the light to vary across it. `tests/test_shaders.py` holds every shaded spatial
shader to reading it; water, which mirrors the sky, is the one exception. `demo/ivy-lantern.json` hangs lanterns
on a wall at night and looks at its ivy, at a wall out of their reach, and at the conure, ducks and sheep fed
beside them.

Ivy pays most for it, being thousands of sprigs of a dozen and more vertices on every wall: `given_at` reads the
ground twice and both volumes at each vertex. A sprig instead reads them once at its root, facing out of the wall,
over the ground height under its wall that its bake carries in the colour's alpha (`given_over`), and a sprig
shrunk away past its reach reads nothing. Measured on the Quest 2 (`bench.sh`, the stress scene, GPU ms a frame,
quality step 0), against the same build without any of these reads; "ivy lit by a constant" keeps ivy's fragment
work and reads nothing, so what it adds is the cost of using the light rather than of reading it:

| Phase | No reads (2 runs) | Read once a vertex (2 runs) | Ivy lit by a constant | Ivy read once a sprig |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| r10/ground | 38.3 | 40.3 | 37.7 | 38.7 |
| r15/ground | 40.3 | 44.1 | 42.1 | 41.9 |
| r20/ground | 43.8 | 48.2 | 46.0 | 45.9 |
| r25/ground | 49.6 | 51.6 | 48.8 | 52.7 |
| r15-tall/ground | 47.1 | 50.2 | 45.5 | 48.1 |
| plaster/ground | 41.5 | 45.7 | 41.4 | 42.6 |
| hands/walls | 45.6 | 47.1 | 44.3 | 44.7 |
| Mean of these | 43.7 | 46.7 | 43.7 | 44.9 |
| meadow/ground | 28.6 | 28.7 | 28.8 | 28.2 |

Reading at the root alone, still through `given_at`, left the walls where reading once a vertex had them (r15 44.7,
r20 48.9, plaster 45.0): a vertex's four reads cost the same whether or not its neighbours share the texels, and it
is the two ground reads that go. The animals, flags and canvases read once a vertex still; no phase that holds them
rose beyond the 2.5 ms the same build varies by between runs. No phase was within budget before the change, so none
went over by it.

### Grass

What games known for their grass do, ranked by how much each adds to the look
for what it costs. Costs are for tiled Adreno GPUs in stereo, the Quest 2's the
tightest: grass there is mostly vertex work (0.77 M indexed vertices for the
meadow's own grass, up to 0.42 M more painted, before culling), so per-vertex
arithmetic is the currency and anything that
breaks early depth (`discard`, alpha test) is ruled out. Each grass value is
defined once: tunables as uniforms in `grass.gdshader` and, for the wind every plant shares,
`wind.gdshaderinc`; placement in
`FloraLayout`.

| # | Technique | What it adds | Cost on the Frame | Here |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | Root-to-tip gradient with root occlusion (Ghost of Tsushima) | Depth: blades read as a lit canopy over a shaded base instead of flat green | A mix per fragment | Replaced: a blade's gradient starts at the ground's own tone (`canopy`), not the dark root, because the meadow is thin: through gaps between clumps each dark base read as a dark tuft on the lighter ground. Blades now rise out of the ground and brighten toward their tips |
| 2 | Clumping (Ghost of Tsushima: Voronoi clumps share height, facing, lean and tint) | Tufts and bare gaps instead of an even carpet | None per frame: computed on the CPU when a plant is placed (`FloraLayout.clump`) | Done |
| 3 | Rounded normals (Ghost of Tsushima: edge normals tilt outward) | A flat blade shades as if curved across its width | None: baked into the clump mesh | Done |
| 4 | Opaque geometry blades, no alpha cards | Stable edges under head motion; alpha-tested cards shimmer in a headset, and standalone-headset developers report alpha to coverage as too expensive for grass | Vertices instead of overdraw; keeps the tiler's early depth | Kept |
| 5 | Wind as a travelling wave (Ghost of Tsushima, Breath of the Wild) | Gusts visible as bands rolling across the meadow, tips brightening as they bend | A few sines per vertex, no texture fetch | Done (`wind.gdshaderinc`): flowers sway in the same field, a perched butterfly with its flower |
| 6 | Per-clump colour variation | Warm and cool tufts break up large areas | A mix and a multiply per fragment from per-instance data | Done (`warm_clump`, `cool_clump`) |
| 7 | Backlit translucency | Blades glow when you look toward the sun | One lighting term per fragment | Retuned: stronger, tinted by the blade (`backlight`) |
| 8 | Distance convergence (inferred from Tsushima's clump-normal blend for specular aliasing) | Far blades fade toward their mid-tone with normals toward up, so sub-pixel slivers do not flicker as the head moves | One distance per vertex | Done (`far_begin`, `far_end`: tips fully at their mid-tone from 20 m) |
| 9 | Grass parts around the hands | The meadow responds to touch: reaching into it pushes blades aside and down | Two distance tests per vertex; two uniforms set per frame | Done (`hand_radius`) |
| 10 | Per-blade Bezier curves with more segments | Smooth arcs instead of a straight blade | Vertex count grows with segments | Dropped: a blade is one triangle (row 16) |
| 11 | View-space thickening (Tsushima: widen blades seen edge-on) | No vanishing slivers; less thin-geometry aliasing | A few vertex operations, but needs each blade's facing per vertex | Not yet |
| 12 | Distance LOD: fewer, wider, sparser clumps far away | The meadow reads to its far edge at less cost than a 24 m full-detail reach | From 8 m out, over 4 m (`Flora.GRASS_LOD_BEGIN`, `GRASS_LOD_BAND`), a clump keeps 3 of its 7 blades, widened to cover the same ground, and three clumps in four sink into the ground, each at its own distance by its rank (`FloraLayout.thin_rank`); the survivors spread twice as wide and stand a quarter taller to hold the cover. The meadow's grass is baked into 8 m cells, each also holding its survivors alone in a mesh of only their kept blades: a cell wholly past 12 m draws that instead, so far clumps bin 9 vertices rather than 21 and the sunk three quarters bin none. Nothing pops: by the distance a cell switches, what it stops drawing has already sunk away | Done |
| 13 | Draw reach and ground | Survivors sink away one at a time from half of `Flora.grass_reach` (60 m at full quality, less down the ladder) out to it, so the meadow thins into the ground instead of ending at a ring of growing blades. The ground takes its colour from the blades' own palette (`meadow.gdshaderinc`, set from `Meadow.GRASS`) at the tone far blades converge on, and its lush and dry patches from the same field as the blades' (`FloraLayout.dryness`, baked into the terrain mesh), so where the blades thin the ground carries on the meadow they were | Cells wholly out of reach are not drawn | Done |
| 14 | Colour patches in a few flat tones | The meadow reads as broad lush, mid and straw patches with soft edges rather than one smooth wash, on the blades and the ground alike | Two saturated ramps on the dryness the blades and ground already share (`patched` in `meadow.gdshaderinc`): per vertex on blades, per fragment on the ground; computing it per vertex on the ground blurred the patches away | Done |
| 15 | Accent tufts | One clump in twenty, the warmest-tinted, stands 1.6 times as tall with fresher green tips, breaking up the carpet | A step and a mix per vertex from per-instance data already there | Done |
| 16 | One triangle a blade | A blade is a straight tapering spike, leaning and bending whole in the wind, instead of a three-triangle blade bent at its middle | A clump is 7 triangles and 21 vertices instead of 21 and 35. `demo/grass-cost.json` (blades hidden and shown in turn, pinned at full quality, 15 one-second samples each way) put the meadow's blades at 3.60, 3.95 and 3.40 ms on the Quest 2 from a standing, a downward and a kneeling eye, and at 2.69, 3.46 and 2.62 ms after | Done |

Directive (verbatim): "I don't... love how the grass pops into being. Depending on how far I am from it... I mean, I do love the nice frame rates, but... I wish I could see distant grass and distant shadows"

Rows 12 and 13 answer it. GPU time per frame (`bench.sh`, median of each phase) for the meadow and
every flora pool full, from 22 m up and standing, before (the 24 m full-density reach shrinking
over its last 8 m, in 16 m cells) and after, at the same ladder positions:

| Phase | Quest 2 before | Quest 2 after | Frame before | Frame after |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Meadow from the air, 24 m (top step before) | 29.9 ms | 26.1 ms | 8.4 ms | 8.1 ms |
| Meadow standing, 24 m | 26.0 ms | 22.1 ms | 5.9 ms | 5.4 ms |
| Flora from the air, 24 m | 42.1 ms | 37.0 ms | 10.0 ms | 9.8 ms |
| Flora standing, 24 m | 40.7 ms | 34.3 ms | 9.3 ms | 8.7 ms |
| Meadow from the air, bottom step | 22.1 ms | 21.0 ms | 6.5 ms | 6.7 ms |
| Flora from the air, bottom step | 27.7 ms | 26.7 ms | 7.5 ms | 7.7 ms |
| Flora standing, bottom step | 29.9 ms | 30.1 ms | 6.4 ms | 6.5 ms |
| Meadow from the air, 60 m (the new top step) | | 32.6 ms | | 8.8 ms |
| Flora from the air, 60 m | | 46.2 ms | | 10.5 ms |

The Frame held 72 Hz in every phase of both. Far reach costs the Quest 2 from the air, where the
whole meadow is in view: 28.8 ms at 24 m to 40.2 ms at 60 m with 4 m cells, for 6% more
primitives. Some of that is draws (8 m cells took the 60 m view to 36.7 ms, 16 m cells saved no
more) and the rest the raster of far slivers; the survivors' widening cost nothing measurable.
So reach above 24 m is the top of the ladder, taken only with room in the frame.

Rows 14 and 15 cost the ground a little per fragment, paid back by reading its steepness per vertex
(`terrain.gdshader`: the height grid's texels are its vertices, so nothing changes on screen). On
the Quest 2 (`App=` ms, the bare meadow, each round main's shaders and these swapped in one run,
five rounds), against main: at the ladder's bottom step standing 20.67, +0.03 ms (standard error
0.08), from the air 26.75, -0.01 ms (0.17); at its top step standing 28.15, +0.16 ms (0.10), from
the air 47.68, +0.14 ms (0.10). Without that steepness move the bottom step standing was +0.14 ms
(0.05). On the Steam Frame (`frame-unworn`, the kernel's GPU time a frame, the same
pairing): bottom step standing 4.31, +0.02 ms (0.004), from the air 5.42, -0.00 ms (0.004); top
step standing 6.92, -0.02 ms (0.003), from the air 10.19, -0.02 ms (0.002); 72 Hz throughout. Tried over the new look in two runs (arm order reversed in the second), GPU ms
a frame over it, mean of the two, and left out:

| Tried | Bottom, standing | Bottom, air | Top, standing | Top, air | Why not |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Cloud shadows: a drifting noise field dimming the meadow | +0.15 | 0.0 | +0.25 | +0.15 | Only shaders that read it darken, so walls, trees and flowers stay sunlit inside the shade; done for every lit shader it is a lighting change, not a grass one |
| Wind stepped at 8 frames a second, each clump at its own phase | 0.0 | -0.1 | 0.0 | 0.0 | Reads as dropped frames in a headset |
| Banded (toon) diffuse with smoothed bands | +0.1 | 0.0 | +0.25 | +0.15 | Darkens the blades and drops the backlight; nothing else in the scene is banded |
| Alpha-tested blade sprites | +0.75 | +0.35 | +2.65 | +5.05 | Breaks early depth, as row 4 says |
| Parting by 16 movers instead of two hands | +2.3 | +2.05 | +2.85 | +3.8 | Every vertex runs the loop at binning |
| Tips widened toward the camera with the wind | +0.1 | -0.1 | +0.15 | +0.2 | A fix for a camera without perspective |
| Light sampled at each clump's root | +0.05 | 0.0 | -0.05 | 0.0 | No visible change under the sun |
| Gusts as two crossed waves at diverging angles | 0.0 | -0.1 | +0.1 | -0.2 | The cross-wave of row 5 already breaks the bands |

Ruled out on this target: alpha-tested or dithered fades (break early depth on
the tiler), screen-space or impostor grass shadows, and specular on blades
(Ghost of Tsushima blends blade normals toward the clump's with distance
specifically to tame specular aliasing). Wind stays low-frequency and
world-anchored, so it moves the grass rather than the ground. Tiny Glade's public
talks cover its walls and lighting, not its grass.

Cost so far: on a Quest 2 the painted-meadow views of `demo/grass.json` take 9.1 ms
(standing) and 8.3 ms (kneeling) of the 11.1 ms GPU budget, with no late frame
(`perf/quest-2/grass/`, b4e2a39).

`demo/grass.json` paints a meadow, captures it from fixed cameras (with a hand
held in the grass for the last pair), and records two benchmark stretches;
`demo/wind.json` holds a view for a wind clip
(`scripts/render.sh demo/wind.json OUT --write-movie OUT/wind.avi --fixed-fps 30`).

### Anti-aliasing

Directive (verbatim): "I am seeing aliasing in the distance. Do we have anti-aliasing yet"

4× MSAA (`msaa_3d`) is on and works on the Frame: turning it off took a village over full
flora from 17.6 to 10.9 ms of GPU time a frame. What still shimmers far off was found in a
render matched to the Frame's eye (1728², 107° vertical), comparing 4× MSAA against a
16-sample reference while the view moves by a fraction of a pixel: the excess flicker is the
meadow. Hiding the grass removed 63–80% of it, the flowers most of the rest; walls, roofs,
ivy and foliage were not the source. The blades there are 2–3 px wide, so widening them
changed nothing; their bright tips and edge-on flower heads are what four samples cannot
hold still. So far grass tips settle on the blade's mid-tone by 20 m and flower heads take
the stalk's green over 10–30 m (`head_fade_begin`, `head_fade_end`): 23–29% less far
flicker, no change in GPU time on the Frame, nor on the Quest 2, where the same scene took
55.6 → 56.0, 47.3 → 47.4 and 42.2 → 42.0 ms of App= GPU time a frame on the ground, at the
edge and in the air, inside the 0.9 ms one build varies between runs.

Measured and left out, on the Frame in the same scene: FXAA over MSAA (+1 ms, and it blurs
walls and roofs) and render scale 1.25, the panel's own size (+3.7 ms). On the desktop 8×
MSAA left half of 4×'s excess flicker; its sample count is what tiled GPUs pay for per bin.

## Element inventory

Stage numbers refer to the build order at the end.

| Element | How the original does it | Our VR approach | Stage |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Freehand walls | Drag on the ground; gridless, follows terrain, stroke speed sets corner sharpness; bricks laid by GPU compute | Hold trigger and sweep the pointer over the ground (from the ground or from the air). The stroke is Catmull-Rom smoothed and courses of individual stones are laid live while you draw. A stroke near a standing wall merges into it (see "Walls meeting walls") | 1 (done) |
| Wall height | Height gizmo; walls from very low to very high | Grip a wall with both hands, one over the other, and draw them apart or together; height snaps to whole stone courses, so it grows and shrinks a row at a time. One hand's grip carries and turns the wall; two spread along it draw it longer or shorter | 1 (done) |
| Wall tops | Merlons on flat tops, can be turned off; wooden railings on half-timber | Per wall: capping slabs with merlons, plain capping, dry-stone coping (stones on edge, tall and short in turn), or a wooden railing on the capping (posts at most 1.4 m apart between the piers, a top rail and a middle one); the style tool on a roofless wall's top picks one. A half-timbered wall's walk is boarded and always railed in wood (see "Wall finishes") | 1 (done), 4 (done), 13 (done), 16 (done) |
| Wall finishes | Stone, plaster worn back to the stone, half-timbering, planks across or upright | The style tool's face wheel (the colour tool's picker with styles) on a wall, house or fence: stone; plaster, whole or worn back to the stones by a quarter, a half or three quarters; half-timbering, its walk boarded under a wooden railing; or planks laid across or upright, cut round the windows, doors and arches; a fence takes rails or planks across or upright. See "Wall finishes" | 16 (done) |
| Arches and gates | "Wall chemistry": a path through a tall wall makes an arch; wide gaps become arcades with pillars; doors widen into archways | A wall drawn over a tree or bush arches over it, shown live while drawing; one of 5 m or more over open ground opens an arch at the middle; the arch tool (its icon a wall with an archway through it) cuts an archway where it points on a wall, in the wall's own style (`WallLayout.ARCH_STYLES`: a round stone ring of tapered voussoirs closed by a keystone, every joint running through the arch's centre as a built arch's do, the wedges pressed face to face with no mortar between them (`WallLayout.MEET`), or on a timber-framed house a four-centred oak head on oak posts, framed by a stud either side and a rail above; the wall's level courses are cut to the arch's one outline, so they meet it with no gap), and on an arch of the wall's own (not a path's) fills it again, one undo step either way. Each opening carries a size, 1 a doorway's, down to a pet door's 0.4 (`WallLayout.openings`): a door's height counts from the floor it opens onto, so a floor raised over higher ground inside takes none of it; an arch of size k is the doorway's scaled k times, ring, voussoirs, keystone and posts, laid round by the wall's courses through the same outline, so a grand arch still reads as built. Wall chemistry, in either order (a path laid through a wall, or a wall drawn across a path): one arch where they cross, or, where the crossing runs 2.8 m or more along the wall (a wide path, or one meeting it at a slant), an arcade of arches 2.3 m apart on stone pillars. A crossing beside an arch already there joins it as an arcade, so no opening closes; undoing the path closes the arches it opened. A path into a house or tower opens a door instead (see Houses) | 1 (done), 5 (done), 18 (done) |
| Wall ends and corners | Neater cornerstones; towers by drawing a loop | Stone piers at wall ends and at the corners of a house | 1 (done), 4 (done) |
| Paths | Dirt strokes and wall crossings; dirt, sand, cobbles and stepping stones; vegetable gardens | Path strokes wear their surface, soft-edged, into the ground mask and open arches or gates through walls and fences. The style tool on a path turns it to dirt, sand, cobbles, flagstones or a garden bed, and paths laid after take the surface let go last; where two meet they blend across their soft edges. Across water a path lays stepping stones, and a bed's tilled earth sprouts rows of vegetables (see "Paths") | 5 (done), 19 (done) |
| Stone ground | Steep terrain turns rocky; dirt paths blend with the ground | The stone tool paints stone ground where it points, in the style the style tool last set (mossy or river cobble), fraying into the meadow stone by stone; a stroke begun on stone paints meadow back (see "Ground materials") | 7 (done) |
| Towers | A loop held down straightens into a circle | A wall drawn into a loop, round or any shape, stays a freestanding wall, roofless. A round tower is its own tool beside the house, a little tower under a cone in the ring, put up and carried as a house is: 4.5 m tall under a cone roof, 3.4 m across, spread every way at once by two hands (from 2.4 m across, no widest) and made taller as they draw apart upright. No door until one is made, as for a house. `demo/tower.json` builds a stone tower, a plaster one and a house | 4 (done) |
| Houses | Rectangle and circle footprints, resizable and rotatable, raised on columns | Circle footprints are the round towers above: a round house is one, its walls drawn down to a house's height by two hands upright. Raised, a house or a round one stands on its plinth, on stone pillars or on timber posts and beams, over a stone foundation the grip raises with it (see "Raising a house"). Its own tool, a little house in the ring. Its trigger puts up a square house where the hand points, and does nothing more: moving is the grip's. A grip anywhere on a house, roof included, carries it, turning it with the hand and raising it onto a plinth as the hand rises; the other hand's grip on it too sizes it, stretching the side nearest the line between the hands as they spread (each side from 2.4 m, with no longest or deepest) and making its walls taller as they draw apart upright (see "Moving and sizing a house" and "Raising a house"). Stone walls with corner piers and no door, level on its lowest ground; a door comes from the windows tool low on a wall or from a path running in, an arch from the arch tool. A path that runs into a house's or tower's wall, laid before or after it, opens a plank door where it meets it (`WallLayout.path_doors`): a single door as wide as the path, or past the widest door the wall's height holds, that many doors fused into one opening, the number nearest the path's width made as wide as it, fewer where the wall holds fewer; none where a door or arch already stands across the path. The door is the path's: the house stores none, and undoing or taking the path closes it. On the Steam Frame, governed, the stress scene's `doors/*` phases, a path into each of the village's 21 houses, hold 72 Hz (`perf/frame/stress`): 5.7 to 6.2 ms of GPU time a frame, no frame over 1.5× the refresh period at either landing, and one in the ground phase, of the game-wide single late frames | 4 (done) |
| Combining buildings | Buildings stack and merge into one structure | Every build stands on what the hand points at: the ground, a wall's top, a flat roof, or a house's roof, which the storey built there replaces; builds that overlap join into one (see "Buildings on buildings"). Flat roofs stack: a house or tower set down on a deck stands on it, and pushed flat it is a deck of its own, so smaller storeys rise in terraces (see "Flat roofs, platforms and dormers") | 4 (done), 13 (done) |
| Roofs | Tile-by-tile roofs; drag to change curvature; flat, peaked, curved, gable | Gable roofs on houses, cone roofs on towers, every tile laid individually as one MultiMesh, with lime torching under the tiles so no sky shows between them from inside. From outside every joint lies over a tile (each tile runs up under the two rows above, the eave row lies on an under-course, a cone's tiles lap their neighbours), and the walls' stones stop under the slopes at any pitch. A gable end continues the wall below it: a stone house's courses carry on up under the slopes, bonded as the next courses and set in the wall's mortar, a plaster house's plaster does, and on both a verge of stones runs up each rake over the courses' ends (European town houses of Cotswold stone or Provençal plaster). The style tool on a gable end picks that house's gable ends, and houses laid after take it: the wall carried up; boarded, upright planks under a barge board up each rake (a barn's end), painted as woodwork; or crow-stepped, the stone courses or plaster climbing past the slopes in capped steps, the roof stopping inside them. The gable ends count against the budget at the face each style lays, as wall (boards as stone wall: laid as stones are, fewer). Two grips on a roof, whatever the hands hold: drawn apart upright they raise the ridge and together lower it; spread across they bow the slopes out, squeezed together inward (`RoofLayout`). Brought all the way down it snaps flat: a stone-flagged deck inside the walls, rimmed by the wall's top (a cap top rises into a low parapet, a crenellated one keeps its merlons), so the house or tower becomes a terrace; its top then takes a style like an open wall's. The style tool offers tile or thatch on a pitched roof and a stone or wooden deck on a flat roof; only grabbing and pulling changes between flat and pitched, a tower's cone as a house's gable (see "Thatch"). Two grips on the eaves, past the walls, pull them out or push them back; pulled far on low walls the slopes come down to the ground like a tent. The windows tool on a gable's slope opens a dormer (see "Flat roofs, platforms and dormers") | 4 (done), 13 (done) |
| Windows, doors | Cottage, arched, arrow-slit, round and corner families that fuse, adapt and wear the plaster around them; doors where windows meet the ground; trapdoors in flat roofs; glowing at night; dressed where people live | The windows tool's trigger on a wall opens a cottage window where it points: a dark pane in a wooden frame with glazing bars, set back under a stone lintel and over a sill, the plaster worn around it. Pointed low on the wall it opens a plank door instead. While it points, the wall stands as the press would leave it, laid by the same code. Windows on one course standing together fuse into one opening with stone mullions, doors into a double door, and a window can stand over a door. Openings keep clear of wall ends, corner piers and arches; ivy grows around them; a press on one closes that light. Pointed at a gable roof's slope it opens a dormer instead (see "Flat roofs, platforms and dormers"), at a flat roof a trapdoor, and at a corner's pier a corner window. The style tool turns a window to another family: arched, an arrow slit or round (see "Window families"). At night a room's windows glow in a colour the colour tool picks. A grip on a placed one carries it along its wall, a window up and down it too, and two size it (see "Moving a fitting") | 6 (done), 13 (done), 17 (done) |
| Chimneys, flags, lanterns | Placed on walls and roofs; lanterns light the fog | The decorations tool's trigger places what suits where it points: a stone chimney with two clay pots on a house's roof, rising clear of the ridge; a flag on a tower's roof or a wall's top, waving; a lantern on an iron arm off a wall's face, or on a fence post. Lanterns are emissive bodies in a soft additive halo, and every one lights the ground and walls around it (see "Lantern light"). A press on one takes it away; a grip carries it over what it stands on (see "Moving a fitting"). Lanterns also stand on the ground (the Lantern and Lamp post styles), every lantern burns in one of the eight glow colours, lit, bright or dim, its flame and its light alike, a stroke of the decorations tool hangs a string of lanterns or flags, flags close together along a wall's top join into a run, and chimneys smoke (see "Lights, strings and smoke") | 6 (done), 21 (done) |
| Clutter | Benches, barrels, crates, stalls, pumpkins, nests and more gather round houses and paths; each can be moved, rotated, copied or deleted | A house, wall or path gathers its own small things as it is built: a bench by a house's front with pumpkins or a box of flowers the other side, barrels and crates at its corners, now and then a market stall or an anvil, a swallows' nest under its eaves; things along a wall's foot and nests on its top; benches, barrels, crates and pumpkins beside a path. A grip moves one along its build and out from it and turns it with the hand, a second hand's grip pulls a copy off it, and a press of the decorations tool or the eraser takes it away. Birds perch in nests and on lanterns and flags (see "Clutter") | 22 (done) |
| Fences | Same controls as walls, own tool; a path through a fence makes a wooden arch | The fence tool draws like walls (same stroke, merging and junctions) with a wooden profile: posts at most 1.6 m apart and two rails between, or planks (see "Wall finishes"). A path through a fence, in either order, opens a gate: two tall posts under a bent wooden arch | 6 (done), 16 (done) |
| Ivy | Grows over time; finds nearby walls through a spatial hash and climbs between them; avoids arches and windows | Every finished wall grows ivy over about half a minute: vines start at the foot of both faces (a house or tower under a roof or deck only its outer one: [inside before and after, outside after](ivy.jpg)), wander upward, branch, and steer around arch openings; leaves are instanced cards on the face (`IvyLayout`), kept out of windows and doors too | 3 (done), 6 (done) |
| Grass, flowers, bushes | Brush-painted vegetation, layered, painting again makes it bushier | Instanced geometry grass and flowers. Flowers and grass are two tools; the trigger of a hand holding one paints where it points; the brush widens with distance and painting a spot again thickens it. Flowers grow in clumps about 1.3 m across, each one of nine soft colours (white, butter, peach, pinks, lavender, violet, blues), so a stroke paints a few colour patches with nothing to pick | 1 (done), 3 (done) |
| Trees, bushes | Placed one at a time, random look, billboards that suggest leaves | Procedural trunks and lumpy foliage blobs in five species the style tool picks per tree; the trigger of a hand holding the tree tool plants an oak where it points. The tree's entry on the wheel opens two, trees and bushes; a bush is one blob like the meadow's own, placed through the same command, save and budget as a tree. Directive (verbatim): "Can you give us a way to place bushes, not just trees"; a grip on a tree or bush, the meadow's own too, carries it along the ground, and two grips on a tree size it by their span (see "Moving and sizing a tree") | 1 (done), 3 (done) |
| Boulders | Not in Tiny Glade | The boulder tool's trigger sets one down where it points, each a new shape from its own seed (`BoulderLayout`): a squat ellipsoid cut by six to ten fracture planes and a flat bed, its edges worn round by a soft minimum and its faces pitted by noise, so no two are alike and none is a ball. The seed alone is stored, with where it stands and its turn, so a boulder loads and networks the same everywhere. One hand's grip carries it, turning it every way with the hand, and two size it by their span, from 0.08 times up without bound (`WallTool.Sizing`, as for trees and openings, each of which one hand carries); its far-mesh distance grows with it. Directive (verbatim): "During that grab and move, boulders position and 3D orientation both map to the boulder, not just position and 2D orientation like when placing houses." Wherever it is let go over the ground it drops straight down until some point of it meets the ground, then sinks a fifth of how far it reaches below its centre (`BoulderLayout.rest`), so it rests whichever way it is turned; pushed into the ground it stays as deep as it was let go, down to the depth that leaves 15% of its height showing, so a ray still finds it to grip, delete or restyle and a mostly buried one still reads as a stone (directive (verbatim): "Let's allow players to mostly bury a boulder. It need not snap up after placing it halfway underground."); its depth is stored with it, and a ray meeting its buried part through the ground meets nothing; it rides the ground as it is reshaped, rising on a hill pulled under it and sinking into a hollow, and undoing the ground edit sets it back; the colour tool paints it any stone swatch, and the style tool gives it any of painted stone's styles (see "Ground materials"). It draws in wall stone, near as 1280 triangles and past 30 m times its scale as 80; the delete beam, the teleport ray and a hand stop at its own surface, and a player over it stands on its top. The budget counts a boulder by its scale squared (a metre-wide one a unit, a pebble a quarter) up to `Budget.BOULDER_MOST_US`, the most one costs however large (see "Boulder cost by size"), so the room alone bounds how many giants a garden holds, never how big one grows. A unit costs `Budget.COSTS.boulder`, the Quest 2's crowd price `demo/costs.json` fits (its records under "Build budget"), charged the same on every device as every budget cost is | 7 (done) |
| Terrain | Stroke-drawn hills with editable height and steepness; steep means rocky | Not a tool: a grip on the ground, whatever the hand holds, grabs it where it points (a pond's bed under its water too) and the hand pulls it up into a hill or pushes it into a hollow; farther ground grabs a wider hill, and taller hills widen so their flanks stay walkable. Grass, flowers, trees and walls follow the ground live; earth and then stone ground show on steep slopes | 7 (shaping done) |
| Water | Dug holes with a flat surface and normal-map waves | The pond tool fills the hollow beside the point it is aimed at up to that point's height, at most to just below its rim, with depth-tinted water, shore foam and ripples where a hand breaks it (see "Water") | 7 (water done) |
| Global illumination | Software ray-traced GI at quarter resolution, SH-encoded and denoised | Mobile budget: baked per-instance occlusion, sky ambient, and a one-metre probe volume of sky seen and sunlight bounced off sunlit stone, solved again on a worker when a build settles or the sun moves (see "Bounce light") | 2 (done) |
| Shadows | Soft sun shadows with temporal filtering, contact shadows | One stabilized shadow map across the garden; occlusion darkening at wall feet and corners from the probe volume | 1 (done), 2 (done) |
| Sky and clouds | Hand-tuned colour timelines per glade; clouds as pre-lit sprites on a cylinder | Sky shader gradient and painted noise clouds; zenith, horizon, sun and moon glow, stars, cloud light, fog colour, exposure and saturation follow the sun's elevation through one timeline of keys (`Daylight`), night included | 1 (done), 8 (done) |
| Fog | Froxel fog with density and height controls | Depth fog with aerial perspective and sun scatter, thinning as the eye lifts | 1 (done), 2 (done) |
| Post | Tilt-shift depth of field, custom tonemapper, grading, bloom | AgX tonemap and grading (done); glow cut for the Quest 2. No depth of field: in a headset your eyes focus for themselves | 1 (done) |
| Time of day | Continuous, lockable, sun-angle slider | A full 24-hour cycle. Grip any hand on open sky, over the mountains, which stand in front of it, or pointed at the sun or the moon wherever they stand, under the ground too, to grab the sky, and aim to turn it about the sun's arc, round the whole day: the sun rises on the left, stands high in front at noon and sets on the right; below the horizon it is night, and the moon, riding the same arc 100° behind the sun, stands high in front at dusk, a soft full disc with faint seas in a gentle halo, lighting the garden cool and dim but bright enough to build by while it is up; it sets soon after midnight, and the hours before dawn, with neither up, are dark but for lanterns and stars, the eye adapting so builds still read; a garden kept in those hours starts again at dusk; the point grabbed stays under the pointer, and moving the hand without turning its aim leaves the sky be; a soft click in the hand each hour. The light's colour and strength, sky, fog, ambient and exposure follow. Letting go turns every player's sky to it. Not an edit: undo leaves it be | 8 (done) |
| Seasons and palettes | Five glade palettes, colour wheel per element | The colour tool (a painter's palette held in the hand): its trigger on a thing opens a wheel of swatches over the controller, and twisting the wrist turns it like a dial, recolouring live; letting go keeps the swatch at the top, one undo step. The style tool (a wand held with a star at its tip) works the same way with a miniature of each style of what it points at (see "Styles"). On a build its stone, roof tiles, thatch, woodwork (fences, window frames, doors) or plaster (plaster walls and their gable ends) each take one of 6–8 curated swatches for that build alone (see "Colour per build"); on the meadow the wheel holds the garden's five seasons (spring, summer, late summer, autumn, winter), presets for grass, ground, foliage, ivy and how many flowers bloom. Laid parts keep their colour variation: a pick scales them onto the swatch | 8 (done) |
| Sound | Treated real recordings, granular bricks, adaptive music that thins while you build | Synthesised stone effects, spatialised at the stone that made them; the meadow's wind and grass turning with the head, birds placed in the world by day, owls and crickets by night; music as layered sections whose tune recedes while you build (see "Sound") | 9 (done) |
| Undo, erase | Animated undo; eraser splits walls into ruins | One history for every tool: a tap of left X (the Frame's D-pad down) undoes a step, right A redoes one; with others, each player's own steps (done); the delete tool takes away any tree or bush its beam strikes, the meadow's own included, a whole path or stone stroke (the ground under it coming back), and mows grass and flowers, the meadow's own included, under its ring while held (done), and is the eraser on walls: held on a wall, its beam sweeps along it and that stretch breaks away into a ruin (`WallLayout.ruins`), its ends stepping down course by course to a gap at the foot, stones fallen in the gap, ivy only on what stands; arches, windows, lanterns, flags and end piers in the ruin go with it. A tap breaks a 1 m gap; a sweep that leaves less than 0.8 m standing, or one on a fence, house or tower, takes the whole of it. Directive (verbatim): "make it so I hold the delete tool to use it so I can hold it down and swipe across things": one held stroke takes everything the beam passes over, walls, trees, bushes, paths, stone and grass alike, sent as one `batch` command, so one undo step (done) | 1 (done), 10 (done) |
| Save and load | Autosave after every action, backups, cloud | The garden's snapshot (`World.snapshot`, the commands that rebuild it, the same a late joiner gets) is written to `user://garden.save` after every frame in which a command changed it, on a worker thread, beside the file and renamed over it, so a process killed mid-write leaves the last one whole (`tests/test_kill.gd`); a quit writes one still due before the sounds wind down. It is loaded at start. Only your own garden is kept, never one you are visiting. A file of another format, or one the game cannot read whole, is moved aside to `garden.save.bad` before the garden is saved again, and the log says which. Undo history is not kept. Scripted runs and tests keep nothing unless given `--garden FILE`. The main thread's share (`snapshot()` and `var_to_bytes`, best of 10) took 0.4 ms on the desktop after the stress scene's densest `stress` step (radius 25: 24 walls, 973 m, 40k stones) plus shaped ground, a paint stroke and a path: 122 kB | 10 (done) |
| Wildlife | Sheep, birds, ducks, fireflies, deer, a fox | Animals arrive on their own, drawn by what you build: ducks fly in to a pond, fish swim in it, butterflies gather over painted flowers, fireflies come out over both at dusk and stay all night while the day's animals rest (no new arrivals, butterflies settled, conures roosting with only murmurs, ducks quiet on the bank), conures perch on a tree or wall, sheep graze beside a fence; deer and a fox come out of the trees round the meadow (see "Wildlife") | 11 |
| Photo mode | Camera modes and filters | The camera tool: a small camera on the controller, its lens along the aim and a screen on its back showing the lens's view; the trigger takes a 3200×2400 photo at full quality, saved as a PNG under `user://photos` (`Pictures/Cairnfold` in shared storage on Android) that records where and how it was taken (see "Photos") | 11 (done) |
| Painting | None | A big easel set up on the ground, painted with a brush held in the hand in watercolour, oil or charcoal (see "Painting") | done |
| Co-op | None | Several players build one garden and see each other as VRM avatars; every edit is a small build command the host orders; finding each other on the local network through the friends dial (see [multiplayer.md](multiplayer.md)) | co-op increment 1 (done) |

## Walls meeting walls

Report (verbatim): "I don't really... like that I can crowd... walls together pretty tight. If if... Tiny
Glade has shown that there is a solution to keeping walls from crowding together... then... I would be
interested in implementing that solution. Tightly-packed walls just don't really look very good"

What Tiny Glade's public material shows (developer patch notes and diaries on its Steam news page; no
numbers are published):

- A wall or fence being drawn can snap to an existing wall; the preview shows a solid line with a dot that
  follows the target wall's shape
  ([1.14.1a notes](https://store.steampowered.com/news/app/2198150/view/1806698490679275)).
- Holding a "disable snapping" key turns snapping off
  ([demo 1.5.4 notes](https://store.steampowered.com/news/app/2198150/view/6890025005352661540)).
- A "continue wall" gizmo exists and is offered walls to walls and fences to fences only
  ([1.8.5 notes](https://store.steampowered.com/news/app/2198150/view/6339469370197280611)).
- Walls and fences can merge: one fix names a crash "when merging two walls whose points already share same
  position" ([1.8.5 notes](https://store.steampowered.com/news/app/2198150/view/6339469370197280611)), another
  has merged fences inherit the colour of the fence merged into
  ([1.10.0b notes](https://store.steampowered.com/news/app/2198150/view/6931694080295313754)); what triggers a
  merge is not public.
- The developers hardened the game against tangled overlapping walls ("wall pasta") rather than refusing them
  ([dev update](https://store.steampowered.com/news/app/2198150/view/5125698536871949230)).
- Not public: snap distances, whether a crossing becomes a junction, any minimum gap between parallel walls.

Cairnfold's version, adapted to a pointer swept by hand, where a few centimetres of jitter are normal
(`WallPlan`, pure data; tests in `tests/test_plan.gd` and, through the driver, `tests/test_join.gd`):

- Any part of a stroke within 1.8 m (centre line to centre line) of a standing wall merges into that wall:
  the wall already stands there, so that stretch builds nothing. A merged stretch only ends once the stroke is
  2.2 m out, so a hand wavering near the edge does not flicker in and out. A stroke hugging a wall therefore
  adds nothing, and two parallel drawn walls never stand closer than a 1.2 m walkway (1.8 m less a 0.62 m
  wall); walls that meet do so only at a junction.
- A kept stretch that runs into a standing wall steeply (more than about 35° off the wall's line) is
  extended onto the wall's centre line and meets it in a junction marked by its end pier, moved aside if it
  would land in an arch. A stroke drawn away from a wall starts on it; one drawn across a wall, or past a
  wall's end, becomes two walls meeting it from either side. A stretch drifting in at a shallow angle ends
  free instead, so a wavering parallel stroke grows no spurs.
- Fences plan the same way, among walls and fences alike: a fence stroke near a wall merges into it or meets
  it at a junction, and a wall stroke near a fence does the same, so neither crowds the other. Fences never
  arch over plants.
- A finished wall arches over any tree or bush it stands on; with none, a wall of 5 m or more arches at its
  middle.
- The preview uses the same merging plan as release: merged stretches vanish, and junction piers and
  arches appear as the hand moves. Release smooths each kept span between its fixed endpoints and sharp
  corners before the command is sent. One stroke is one undo step, however many walls it built. The plan reruns only when the stroke
  moves, and each stroke point's nearest wall is found once, so a rerun costs about 0.1 ms beside 120
  standing walls and 470 plants on a desktop CPU.
- Not adopted: merging two walls into one polyline at their ends and the "continue wall" handle; junctions
  between separate walls give a similar look without rewriting a standing wall's stones. Towers and houses
  join where they overlap instead (see "Buildings on buildings").

## Buildings on buildings

Directive (verbatim): "Being able to build on buildings and combine buildings together is kinda... core to this
whole game. er- Well, it... It's core to the inspiration and it'll really make for a fun game when you can... make
buildings by combining multiple other buildings together"

Every wall, fence, tower and house is one kind of thing, a build, and two rules make them compose (`Massing`,
pure data; tests in `tests/test_build_on.gd`, and through the garden in `tests/test_world.gd`):

- A build stands on what it was aimed at. The ray of a hand holding walls, fences or the house stops at the first top it meets: a wall's top, a
  flat roof, or a pitched roof, which counts as the top of the walls under it. What is drawn there stands on that
  top, and its command carries the ids of the builds under it (`on`). A loop on builds stands level on the
  highest top under it, an open wall on the top under each of its points, and the ground where it runs off. A
  stroke begun on a top is drawn on that top's level, so a thin wall top is easy to follow, and it merges only
  with walls standing on the same build. Nothing on a top opens a door or an arch on its own.
- Builds that meet lay nothing inside each other. A build's stones, roof tiles and plaster inside a house or
  tower it overlaps, between that one's feet and its roof, are not laid; so the shared stretch of two houses'
  walls goes, the rooms become one, and their roofs meet in valleys, and a wall run into a tower ends inside its
  wall. A build standing on another takes away what of that one rises above the top it stands on under it: the
  merlons under a tower on a wall, the roof and its plaster under an upper storey (its eaves stay as a skirt).
- A build stands only on older ones, so what stands on what never loops. When a build changes or goes, every
  build it meets is laid again on a worker thread at the end of that frame, oldest first, so a storey rises with
  the house under it. A build taken away takes everything standing on it, all the way up: storeys, houses on its
  deck, the furniture on its floors and deck, and stairs whose ends rest on it, in the garden, its save and on
  every peer alike, and one undo brings all of it back. Each table of the garden names the field its entries
  stand on (`Commands.STANDS_ON`), so nothing new can be left standing on nothing. Builds that meet nothing lay as
  before, and two open walls never meet this way, so the stress scene's walls cost nothing more; its village
  stands two storeys high, each house crossed by a wing.

## Moving and sizing a house

Directive (verbatim): "Eventually I'd like to be able to resize a house" / "in each dimension"

Most things are grabbed with one hand and, while held, move up and down, left and right, forward and back, and turn;
two hands size them. A house or tower is one of them. It is held where it stands, as a boulder is: no handles, no
menu, nothing drawn but the house itself moving or changing. The house tool's trigger only puts a house up; it never
moves one.

- Carry: a grip anywhere on a house or tower, its walls, gables or roof, whatever the hand holds, takes it. It moves
  along the ground with the hand, farther for a far house by the reach gain a wall's pull has, and turns about the
  vertical as the hand turns; raised or lowered, it rises onto its plinth or comes back down (see "Raising a house").
  Its stones are carried as laid, roof, ivy, fittings, furniture and lantern light with them, riding the ground under
  it; only a change of plinth lays it again. Every build standing on it, storeys and walls all the way up, is carried
  with it and set where it is let go. One standing on a build keeps to that build's top. A grip on a door, window, arch,
  lantern, flag, chimney, dormer or piece of furniture takes that, not the house (see "Moving a fitting").
- Size: the other hand's grip on the same house joins the hold, and the two hands size it where it stands. On its
  walls, spreading or closing them moves those walls out or in along the side nearest the line between the hands,
  and only that side: hands across its front change its width, hands along its flank its depth. The nearer side
  wins outright, so a pair held a little askew still changes one dimension. A hand held still keeps its wall where it
  stands and the moving hand's wall follows that hand, so a house against a wall grows away from it. Drawn apart
  upright, one hand over the other, the hands make its walls taller, together shorter, a course (0.3 m) at a time,
  the roof riding the new top. Two hands never move, turn or raise it.
- A round tower has one footprint dimension: two hands spread its diameter, and it stays round; upright they set its
  height as a house's.
- The roof: both grips on the roof shape it instead (see "Flat roofs, platforms and dormers" for the eaves); a pair
  with either grip on its walls sizes it. Drawn
  apart upright they raise the ridge, together lower it, all the way down snapping it flat; spread or closed across,
  they bow the slopes out or in, and closing again takes the bow back.
- Letting go of one hand leaves the house in the other, carried from where it now stands.
- The bounds are those of laying out: sides and a tower's diameter from 2.4 m. No side, height or plinth has a
  greatest: the build budget, which charges walls, plinths and roofs by the square metre they lay, is what stops a
  house growing. A roof's pitch has no greatest either: a steeper roof lays more tile along its slopes, and the
  budget charges it so (`RoofLayout.stretch`).
- Everything on the house follows: the roof covers the new walls, keeping its slope (a gable's rise scales
  with the depth under its ridge, a cone's with its radius, a flat deck stays flat); doors, windows, arches,
  lanterns, flags, chimneys and dormers keep their place in proportion along their own wall or ridge, and furniture
  its share of the room, so the light a lantern casts inside stays where it hangs; builds joined to the house, or
  standing on it, are laid again against its new walls, and its ivy regrows.
- Letting go of both grips sets the house as one `reshape` command (its points, height, plinth, the fittings' places
  and its roof), with one more for each build carried on it, all one undo step, and every player sees it as they see a
  raised wall. Let go within 5 cm and a degree of where it stood, unsized, it is left as it was and nothing is sent, so
  a trembling grip is no edit. A house as held that would not fit turns the sight red, and a house the garden turns
  down goes back where it stood.

`demo/resize.json` widens, deepens and raises a house with doors, a lantern inside and a chimney, widens a
tower beside it, and looks inside at dusk before and after; `demo/grab-move.json` carries, turns, widens and heightens
a house, raises a roof, bows it and pulls its eaves, as hands do. `tests/test_controls.gd` does the same in tracked
hands, `tests/test_roof_grab.gd` the roof's and the eaves' holds, and `tests/test_house.gd` checks the rules and the
fittings headless.

Why one hand moves and two size: the grip is grab whatever the hand holds, and grabbing a thing should take the
thing itself, as it takes a boulder; one hand has a position and a turn to give, which is what moving needs, and a
pair has a span, across and upright, which is what sizing needs. The trigger is each tool's action, so the house
tool's trigger places and nothing else. One rule stretches both sides of every house (`WallTool.stretched`): the
side nearest the hands' line stretches by the ratio of their spread.

## Moving and copying

A grip on a wall, fence, house, tower or tree carries it to a new spot and turns it as the hand turns (see "Moving and
sizing a house" for a house's hold). A wall or fence turns about its middle, its openings, lanterns, flags and ruins
keeping their places along it; a house or tower takes its roof, fittings, furniture and what stands on it; a tree or
bush turns about its foot, its crown with it, its turn kept by the garden (`turn` of its `tree` command) through
restyles, undo and saves. Ivy roots by the build's seed and its place along the wall, so it climbs the carried build as
it did and stays grown. Let go, each is one command, one undo step, which every peer applies alike.

The colour tool copies one build's look onto another. A tap of its trigger (released within `PaletteTool.TAP` of game time, the
wheel never turned off where it opened) on a wall, house, tower or fence takes that build's look into the hand
(`PaletteTool.look_of`): its paint, face and wear, top, foundation, floor, gable ends and roof covering, and its beam
takes the look's stone or plaster colour. Each tap after on another build lays the look there as one `reshape`, one undo
step, as far as that build takes it (`PaletteTool.look_onto`): between a fence and a wall or house only the paint passes,
a roofless wall takes no covering, and a tower's walls stay bare stone. A tap on the meadow, or putting the tool down,
lets the look go. A tap still makes the build's swatch the one new builds take, as any press does, and a wheel closed by
putting the tool down taps nothing. The tap is timed in game time, so a script's tap is a tap on any machine.

`demo/move.json` carries and turns a house with its window, lantern and chimney, a wall with its lantern, flag and ivy,
and a tree, then copies the plastered, slate-roofed house's look onto a plain one and undoes it. `tests/test_move.gd`
checks each carry and turn with undo and redo, the copy and how far a look goes, and plays the stress scene's
`move/carry` phase: in the densest village the budget lets a player build, two storeys and a wing to each house, the
largest house is carried 40 m across it and turned round in 6 s. `tests/test_carry_plants.gd` checks a tree's turn, and
the co-op run has the joiner copy the host's house's look, carry and turn it and its own tree, then undo the tree's
carry, every peer alike.

Letting the house go cost the Frame one 22 to 25 ms frame in every run of the move section at 0bb0c15: the host
checked the batch's budget twice, applied it, and in that same frame laid the house again, laid its upper storey
twice (once for the batch, once as a build the house met), laid the wing it joins, played both settle sounds, and
lifted and laid the house's worn floor, each redrawing and uploading the whole ground mask. Now the host checks a
command once (a visitor checks before sending); a build the garden reshaped, or one meeting it, falls due
(`Wall.due`) and is laid from the frame after, in tree order, `Budget.RELAY_US` a frame and one at least, its sound
playing as it is laid; the ground wears a lifted mark's patch and a mark laid among older ones from the frame after,
once, with the marks laid last; a lay meant to settle that lands after its inputs changed is not shown, since the
lay replacing it settles, where showing it drew the house's stones apart from the glade's for a few frames (3 to 6
ms); and a hold lets its build and the storeys on it go from the glade in one flush. On the Frame, the move section
played after a village has been built once, as the stress scene plays it (records in `perf/frame/stress-move/`,
governed, and `perf/frame/stress-move-pinned/`, at step 0): the let-go and the phase's first second made no late
frame governed in three runs and pinned in two of three; the third pinned run made one 21.6 ms frame as the house was
let go, with 12.2 ms of scripts and physics and 10.2 ms on the GPU in the frame before it. The let-go's own frame now
spends about 2.3 ms on the command (cleaning, pricing and applying it), the frames after about 2 ms each laying one
build or wearing the ground. Governed, the ladder stepping from 3 to 1 at once during or after the carry (the GPU from
7.2 to about 10 ms) makes 25 to 34 late frames before it steps back.

## Moving a fitting

A grip on a placed fitting takes the fitting, not its build, and one hand carries it over the surface it was placed
on, moved as the hand moves, farther for a far one by the reach gain:

- A door, window or arch slides along its wall; a window also goes up and down it, staying a window, while a door
  and an arch keep to the floor.
- A lantern slides along and up its wall face, keeping to its side; on a fence it steps from post to post.
- A flag slides along its wall's top.
- A chimney or a dormer moves over its roof, a dormer from one slope to the other too.
- Furniture moves across its floor or flat roof's deck and a ground piece across the ground, each turning about the
  vertical as the hand turns; a piece at a house's foot slides along its wall.

It moves by 5 cm steps and turns by 5°, so a trembling grip is no edit and its wall is laid again only once a step;
furniture and ground pieces move their own node alone while held. Where it would not stand, by the checks its tool
places it with (an opening still opening without closing another, a lantern clear of every opening, a dormer
clear of the chimneys), it stops at the last place it stood. Let go, it is set as one `reshape` of its wall (or of the
decoration), one undo step, and a peer checks the reshape as any other. Two hands size an opening by their span, as
they size a boulder; every other fitting has no size, and the other hand's grip on it takes nothing.
`tests/test_fitting_grip.gd` carries each kind headless, and through the grip button a lantern.

## Lights, strings and smoke

- **Colour and brightness.** A lantern burns in one of `Palette.GLOWS`, the eight colours a window glows in, at one of
  three strengths (`Lamps.STRENGTHS`: lit, bright 2.2 times as strong, dim 0.45 times): the 24 swatches of
  `Palette.Element.LANTERN`. The colour tool on a lantern opens that wheel. A wall's lanterns share its swatch, kept in
  its paint like any material's, so a house's lanterns all burn alike; a decoration with a flame keeps its own
  (`lantern`), a string's lanterns too. Walls built, decorations placed and strings hung after take the swatch let go last. The flame
  shows the swatch as the wheel does, the glow round it widens and strengthens with the brightness, and the light baked
  into the lamp volume (see "Lantern light") is the colour times the strength.
- **Standing on the ground.** The Lantern style sets one down on its own iron foot, the Lamp post on an iron post on a
  stone foot; like every decoration they come as a present the style tool turns into them, on any mount.
- **Strings.** Held from one point to another, the decorations tool's trigger hangs a string between them when let go:
  each end tied where it points, or on a 2.6 m pole where a decoration would stand there outdoors (the ground, a
  flat roof's deck or a house's foot). A ray meets a string only about its rope and what hangs from it. It spans 1.5 to 20 m across the ground
  and climbs no steeper than it runs. The rope sags 7 cm a metre across, between 5 cm and 1 m (`DecorationLayout.sag`),
  on a parabola through both ends (`hang_at`), hung with a lantern every 1.1 m or a flag every 0.34 m, each a square
  hung from its corner in the flag colour after the last. A press let go where it went down places as before. A
  string is a decoration on the `span` mount, between its first end `at` and its second `to` with the poles `drop`
  under them, saved, shared, carried whole by a grip and undone like any; the style tool on one offers the two strings
  alone, and strings hung after take the one let go last. It stands free of the builds it is tied to.
- **Flag runs.** Neighbouring flags on a wall's top 4 m apart or nearer join into a run (`WallLayout.flag_runs`): a
  rope from pole to pole 1 m over the wall's top, sagging as a string does, hung with pennants in the two flags' colours.
  Each run is charged as a fitting.
- **Smoke.** Each chimney pot smokes (`RoofLayout.vents`, `Smoke`): five puffs, quads turned to the eye that the
  shader lifts 2.4 m, drifts downwind, swells and fades over seven seconds, lit as the hour lights everything else.
  The processor lays them once with the roof.
- **Budget.** A string is charged by the decoration rule (`Budget.decoration`): a piece, a solid for each length of
  rope, pole, lantern part and flag, a flame for each lantern; a 6 m string of lanterns on two poles costs 184 µs. The
  stress scene's `lights/*` phases, three houses with a chimney every 1.5 m along their ridges, a lantern every 1.5 m
  round them and strings between them and in rows across the meadow until the budget is full, at dusk, hold 72 Hz on
  the Steam Frame, governed, with no late frame ([record](../perf/frame/stress/20261026T024617Z-3e988be.json)): 3.6 ms
  of GPU time a frame from the air and on the ground, at step 16, where the ladder came down at the village and
  stayed.

`demo/lights.json` renders it at dusk and at night: a capped wall with three flags joined into two runs and red
lanterns, a house with two smoking chimneys and a blue one, lanterns standing on the ground lit, bright and dim, lamp
posts in green, teal and violet, strings of lanterns and of flags on poles, and a string tied from the wall's top to
the house's eaves. `tests/test_lights.gd` covers the looks and the light they bake, the strings' sag and what hangs
from them, their commands, the stroke, the colour and style tools on them, runs, smoke, the demo and the stress phases.

## Clutter

- **Gathered.** A house, wall or path gathers its own small things when it is built (`ClutterLayout`), worked out on
  the host from its command, so every machine lays the same; a build put back by undo or a snapshot keeps the things it
  had. A house on the ground: a bench beside its door, or the middle of its front, and two carved pumpkins or a box of
  flowers the other side; a barrel or a stack of crates at about half its corners; one house in four a market stall
  along its back, one in five an anvil at its end; a swallows' nest under its eaves on any storey, its back against the
  wall's stones and its top against the roof's underside whatever the roof's style, pitch or plinth. Along a plain wall's foot, a barrel, crates, flowers or
  a bench about every 5 m on either face, and a nest on its top about every 8 m. A path a piece beside it 1.2 m from
  its start and about every 7 m after, either side in turn, facing it, none within 1.5 m of its end, where it may run
  into a house. A fence or a ruin gathers nothing.
- **Kept.** A build keeps them as its `clutter`: a list of `Vector4(s, off, turn, style)`, the arc length along it, how
  far out it stands (from a wall's face to its middle, or from a path's spine; the sign the side), its turn from facing
  out, and a decoration's style (`DecorationLayout`, whose Crates, Market stall, Anvil, Flower box, Carved pumpkin and
  Bird nest stand on any mount as the rest do). A piece follows its build as it is carried, sized or reshaped. One in a
  doorway, past an open wall's end or where a ruin broke the wall away is not laid. A carved pumpkin's face is glass,
  glowing at night in its build's glow colour.
- **Laid with the build.** Each piece is laid as parts of its build, a wall's stones or a path's treads, so it draws in
  its build's chunks and costs no draw call of its own; the budget charges a part a piece as wall stone
  (`Budget.PIECE_M2`, 0.1 m², as a path's treads and crops are).
- **In the hand.** Every piece is a fitting of its build: a grip carries it along the build and out from it or back,
  0.05 to 3 m out from a wall's face or up to 3 m past a path's edge, and turns it with the hand, by 5 cm and 5° steps.
  The build is laid once without the pieces hands hold, which stand apart as their own nodes, so a step moves only the
  piece, as a held decoration's does; let go, the build is laid once more with it, after the garden applied the edit.
  Another hand's grip on a held piece pulls a copy off it, carried by that hand. Let go, each is one reshape of the build's `clutter`, one undo step; a press of the decorations tool,
  or the eraser, takes one away. A build holds at most 64.
- **Birds.** A bird perches on a flag's finial, a lantern's hood and in a nest, a house's under its eaves too, besides a
  wall's top.

`demo/clutter.json` renders two houses, a wall and a path with what they gathered, then a bench carried and turned, a
copy pulled off a pumpkin or a box of flowers, a piece by the path taken away, and the pumpkins at night.
`demo/roof-nests.json` shows a house's nest under each style of roof: tiled, raised, steep, swept, a tower's cone, a
flat deck and an upper storey's.
`tests/test_clutter.gd` covers where each build gathers, the schema, the budget, the layout, the targets, each edit with
undo and redo, a house put back keeping its things, the doorway, perches, a nest against its wall and every style of roof, the demo,
and the stress scene's `clutter/*` phases: two houses, a wall and a path with what they gathered, every house and wall
then given a piece every 0.7 m round its foot until the budget or its foot is full, then `clutter/carry`, three 2 s
carries and turns of a house's pieces, and `clutter/copy`, three copies pulled off them. `demo/clutter-cost.json` prices
the same garden on a Quest 2 bare of clutter and cluttered.

On the Frame, governed, the stress scene's clutter section played alone (its `budget` step, then from the `sun` step
before the village; `perf/frame/stress-clutter/`): when each step of the hand laid the house again, b4d160c made 4 late
frames carrying and 3 copying; held pieces standing apart made none carrying in three runs and one copying in one of
them, a 28 ms frame with 11.7 ms of scripts before it, as much as nothing held costs: one of the isolated late
frames most phases show.

## Raising a house

Directive (verbatim): "When placing a building, you should be able to raise it higher up by raising your controller up"

A house or tower on the ground can stand raised on a plinth above its lowest ground, as high as a build runs long
(`rise` on the build); the budget, which counts the plinth as stone wall, is what stops it. The hand carrying it
raises it (`WallTool.carry`): it rises as far as the hand rises, scaled by the reach gain as the carry is, a course
(0.3 m) at a time, never below its ground, its floor rising with it on the plinth; lowered, it comes back down.
Letting go sends it in the same `reshape` as the carry, one undo step, every player seeing it. Two hands never raise
it: drawn apart upright they make its walls taller instead (see "Moving and sizing a house"). A house standing on a
build stays on that build's top: it is not raised.

It never floats: its foot stands level, raised above its lowest ground, and the courses under the foot, the ones
a wall over a pond already lays down to the bed, carry on down to the ground all round as its plinth, following a
slope. They are the wall's own courses, the same stone, depth and mortar, shaded only by their height over the
ground, and stay stone under a plaster or timber-framed house. Its corner piers reach down with them. The floor ends
behind the shallowest mortar's face, so no joint shows it (`tests/test_floors.gd`, `tests/test_stone_house.gd`). A door or an arch opens from its floor and the plinth runs whole under
it, so nothing under the floor shows through (over stilts, the course or beams under the foot do); a raised house is walked into on its floor (`WallLayout.threshold`).
Report (verbatim): "the arch should terminate at the foundation of the building." The Style tool aimed below the wall foot offers stone or plaster for the foundation independently of the wall above, and under a house or tower stone pillars or timber posts (see "Stilts" below), on its own foundation wheel. The plaster is laid in the face's panels; the Colour tool uses the build’s shared plaster swatch. Foundation corner piers stay stone. The plinth counts against the budget as stone wall,
and a grip on it holds the house as one on its walls does. `demo/raise.json` raises a stone house on a slope and a
timber-framed one, and places a third raised; `tests/test_house.gd` checks the rule and the plinth,
`tests/test_controls.gd` both holds in tracked hands, `tests/test_keep.gd` that it is kept, and `scripts/coop.sh`
that every player sees the joiner's raised wing.

### Stilts

New houses and towers default to stone pillars when lifted. The foundation wheel also retains a whole stone plinth, plaster, and timber posts.
A build's `foundation` (`WallLayout.Foundation`) is one of a stone plinth, a plaster one, pillars and posts, which
the foundation wheel turns between, pillars and posts only under a house or tower; houses put up after take the
foundation last turned to, standing on its stilts once raised. A house on a build is never raised, so stands on none.

- Where they stand (`WallLayout.stations`): on every corner and evenly between, at most 2 m apart along the walls'
  centre line; round a round house, evenly all round. Each stands straight under the wall it carries.
- Pillars, 0.5 m square on a broader base stone, carry a course of stone running whole round under the foot; a stone
  house's corner pier, carried down, is its corner's pillar. Posts carry timber beams, one straight from each post to
  the next, round under the walls; a corner pier stops on the foundation above its post.
- The foundation always reaches the terrain, whether the floor is lifted or the walls stretched. Stretch changes wall height without changing the floor. Stilts stand in the raise up to 1.8 m (`STILT_SPAN`) over a stone foundation at least a course
  high, laid as the plinth is, following the slope. Raised further, the foundation rises with the house, so the grip
  that lifts the house sets the foundation's height under the stilts. Uphill, where the ground stands over the
  foundation's level, the stilts stand on the ground, shorter. A raise too low for stilts (under 0.9 m) stands on its
  plinth whole.
- Ivy roots at the walls' foot, not down the stilts, and the decorations tool sets nothing at a stilted foundation,
  where no face stands to back it. Exterior furniture stands on the terrain at its own position, independently of the house floor height; furniture moved inside stands on the floor.
- The budget charges the foundation as plinth, pillars and their course as stone wall by the face they lay, and each
  post and beam as a timber; stilts cost less than the plinth they open up.

`demo/raised.json` raises long and round houses on pillars and posts on a slope, one high enough that its foundation
rises, and turns a plinth to pillars with the style tool; `tests/test_stilts.gd` checks where the stilts stand, the
foundation, the slope, the beams and course, the budget and the command headless, `tests/test_style.gd` the wheel,
and `tests/test_raised_demo.gd` the demo and the stress scene's `raised/*` ring of long and round houses on stilts. On the
Steam Frame, governed, both views hold 72 Hz (`perf/frame/stress`): 3.5 ms of GPU time a frame and no frame over
1.5× the refresh period, the view's landing included.

## Floors

Directive (playtest, paraphrased): give buildings floors.

Every house and tower is floored (`WallLayout.floor_y`; tests in `tests/test_floors.gd`):

- At its foot, inside the walls and under them to just short of their outer faces, so nothing shows between floor
  and wall. It rides the foot: raised on a plinth, the floor is raised with it, and a stretched or resized house lays
  its floor again with its walls. Where the ground inside stands higher than the foot, as up a slope, the floor
  stands at the highest of it, so no ground shows through, on a bed reaching down to the foot that reads as a step
  at a door low on the downhill side.
- Flags laid in running bond on mortar that shows in their joints, or boards in staggered lengths: the floor's own
  material, apart from the walls'. Directive (verbatim): "Let's make the floor itself a thing that is swappable. You
  use the wand on the floor to select whether it is stone or wood. And that selection is orthogonal to the selection
  of the other building materials." The style tool on a floor turns it between the two (`planks` on the build), and
  houses and towers laid after take the floor turned to last; turning the walls never turns the floor, nor the floor
  the walls. Boards lay about as many pieces as flags (`tests/test_floors.gd`), so the walls' budget rates, measured
  over both, price either. Either is plain boxes, as timbers are, and casts no shadow, lying in the walls' own.
