  They take the build's stone or woodwork paint. Moss grows on stone only under the sky, so a floor under its roof and
  every piece of woodwork stay bare of it.
- A house or tower has one floor, at its foot, however tall it stands. Floors
  at every storey up the walls were cut: they did not fit the game.
- A floor is stood on: the teleport ray lands on the first floor it meets, and a point inside stands on the floor under
  it, so a raised house is stood in on its floor, not on the ground under it.
- Nothing grows through it: the meadow under a house or tower on the ground is worn bare, out to inside its walls,
  as a path wears it, as a mark in the order of its build command, so taking the house away brings the grass back.
- Two houses joined into one room never lay two floors over each other: the newer floor lays nothing in the older
  one's room, where the older floor stays whole.

`demo/floors.json` floors a stone house on a hump, a plaster one raised on its plinth and boarded, and a tower, and
looks inside the first two; `demo/floor-style.json` turns one house's floor from flags to boards with the style tool.

## Colour per build

Directive (verbatim): "If I make one wall somewhere, and then I make another wall elsewhere, and I select the color
on the first wall, it changes the color on the other wall too. I'd like color to be per wall instead of global"

Directive (verbatim): "Same for... buildings"

Colour belongs to the build, the one kind of thing every wall, fence, house and tower is (see "Buildings on
buildings"):

- A build carries its `paint`: a swatch for each material (stone, roof tiles, thatch, woodwork, plaster). Its roof, gable
  ends, window frames, doors and chimneys are part of it and take its paint; flags and iron keep theirs, and its
  glass takes the colour a room's windows glow at night (the colour tool on a window's glass picks it).
  A storey on a house, or a wing across it, is a build of its own and keeps its own colours. Stairs are builds
  too: their treads and stone railings take the stone swatch, wooden railings and ladders the woodwork's, laid into
  their stones, so a repaint lays them again whole.
- The colour tool's wheel on a build opens on that build's swatch, and letting go changes that build alone: a
  `reshape` of its paint, so it is one undo step, every player sees it, saves and joiners' snapshots carry it with
  the build, and undoing a delete brings the build back in its colours. There is no garden-wide build colour.
- A new build starts in the swatch last picked on this machine for each material, or the laid colours before any
  pick, so a village in one colour costs one pick per material and recolouring one house after leaves the rest
  as they are. What was last picked is the player's own, like the tool in hand: it is neither sent nor saved.
- The season stays the garden's: the meadow is one thing.
- A build's colour is baked into its pieces' instance colours as they are packed on the worker thread (`Wall`),
  so drawing it costs nothing per frame and the bounce light sees it. A repaint lays no stones: one pass over the
  build's packed pieces scales each by its material's code from the swatch it has to the new one, and its gable
  ends take their plaster as a per-instance shader value. The wheel shows its swatch on the build live this way
  and sends it on letting go.

## Thatch

Directive (verbatim): "Thatched roofs I want them as an option."

A pitched roof or a tower's cone is tiled or thatched (`RoofLayout.thatch`, part of the build command, so a `reshape`
changes it, one undo step, and every player, save and joiner sees it). The style tool's roof wheel offers tile and thatch on pitched roofs, each as a miniature, and stone flags or boards
on flat roofs. Flat decks take stone colours, or woodwork's when boarded. Selecting a covering preserves pitch and curvature; only grabbing
and pulling changes between flat and pitched; houses laid after on that machine take the covering last turned to, as they take a wall's material, so a
thatched village costs one turn. A flat roof is never thatched: it is walked on, and keeps the covering it had for
when it is pitched again, as a pitched roof keeps the deck it had. A thatched roof keeps its thatch when it is resized or reshaped.

Thatch is massing, not a texture on a tiled slope. Reed and straw roofs in England (Norfolk water reed, Devon combed
wheat reed, Cotswold long straw) share a shape that reads across a village: a coat about a third of a metre deep,
its eaves and verges rounded over well past the walls, and a ridge of its own standing proud, its lower edge
block-cut into points and pinned by hazel liggers. So a thatched roof lays (`RoofLayout._lay_thatch`, pure data):

- One mesh, the coat: each slope a grid from the ridge to past the eave and past both verges, lifted COAT off the
  roof's surface with a soft lumpiness, its edges turned under in a half-round onto an underside, so the eaves and
  verges are thick and round from below as from above. On every pitch the coat goes from the walls' faces to the
  eaves with no seam, so no wall shows through it (the gable courses rise under it as under tiles; the stone or
  board verge is left out, the coat rolls over the rakes; a crow-stepped end keeps its steps, the coat stopping inside them).
- The ridge: each slope's coat carried up to meet the other's over the ridge (on slopes steeper than 2.5 in 1 it
  stops short), under a roll along the ridge; down each side a band stands proud, its lower edge cut into points,
  held by two hazel liggers. A cone has a knob at its apex under its finial or flag.
- Colour: each roof weathers its own way from fresh reed toward grey-brown, its ridge, renewed more often, less;
  moss gathers on the gentler, upward faces of a weathered coat. The thatch shader stretches the detail noise along
  the way the straw runs (a vertex's tangent), so the coat reads as reed at arm's length. Thatch has its own swatches
  on the colour wheel (`Palette.Element.THATCH`): fresh gold, wheat, water reed, silver, aged.
- Shadows and bounce light read boxes, so slabs inside the coat (`RoofLayout.slabs`, drawn shadow-only as
  `Wall.Part.COAT`) cast its shadow and stand in for it in the bounce light; the coat itself casts none.

Cost: a thatched roof is one mesh drawn per build (a draw call each, not chunked like tiles) at about a third
fewer triangles than a tiled one. Both count the area along their slopes out to the eaves, and `demo/costs.json` fits
`thatch_m2` beside `roof_m2` on each headset (see "Build budget"): thatch fits cheaper than tiles on both, 1.14 µs a m²
against 2.35 on the Frame. A build's extra draw call costs less than the tiles it replaces, so the coats stay one mesh
a build. Past the stones' near distance the coat is drawn from rows `RoofLayout.FAR_STEP` (0.36 m) apart instead of
`COAT_STEP` (0.12), a fifth of the triangles: on the Quest 2 five thatched houses from the air read 7.66 ms drawn fine
at every distance against 6.62 coarse far off, over a bare 5.16, four pairs interleaved in one run, and look the same
there. Before that cut, hiding each part in turn put 2.6 ms of their 4.0 in the coat, its shadow slabs, liggers and
chimneys nothing measurable, and a far coat lit once a vertex with no texture reads cost 0.3 ms more than its own
shader. `thatch_m2` and `thatch_m2_near` in `Budget.COSTS` are this build's fit
([record](../perf/quest-2/costs/20261024T133151Z-14ca646.json)): 3.834 µs a m² from the air, down from 10.177, and
8.116 standing, where the village's fit had charged it nothing. The stress scene's village two storeys high,
tiled and then thatched, in viewport GPU milliseconds a frame, draws and primitives:

| Phase | Steam Frame ([record](../perf/frame/stress/20261023T001249Z-f1d7d2d.json)) | Quest 2 ([record](../perf/quest-2/stress/20261022T232134Z-276e1cf.json)) |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Tiled, from the air | 9.68 ms, 344 draws, 1.89 M | 251 ms, 343 draws, 1.90 M |
| Thatched, from the air | 10.23 ms, 417 draws, 1.78 M | 105 ms, 416 draws, 1.78 M |
| Tiled, standing | 16.41 ms, 235 draws, 2.99 M | 27.0 ms, 201 draws, 2.72 M |
| Thatched, standing | 15.49 ms, 331 draws, 2.40 M | 25.1 ms, 294 draws, 2.19 M |

From the air the Quest 2 draws the tiled village at under four frames a second, the thatched one at under ten; both
lie far past the budget, as the stress scene means them to.

`tests/test_thatch.gd` casts rays at every roof shape the game builds (gables shallow to 6 m steep, bowed and swept,
cones) and after resizes: straw a coat deep over every point of the roof, over the ridge, round the eaves and verges
and under the overhang; no wall stone through it; and the command, budget and swatches. `tests/test_controls.gd`
turns a house's and a tower's roof to thatch with the style tool in tracked hands and undoes it.
`demo/thatch.json` thatches a stone and a plaster house with the style tool, then a village around them, close and
from across it, by day and at dusk.

## Flat roofs, platforms and dormers

The original's roofs are more than pitched tiles: a house can be topped by a flat terrace walked on behind a parapet or
a railing, terraces stack, gable roofs grow dormer windows, eaves reach out or tuck in, and a roof pulled down low
makes a tent. Every one of these is a field of the build command (`World.state.walls[id].roof`, `top`), so it is one
undo step, every player sees it, and saves and joiners' snapshots carry it.

- Decks. A roof pushed flat is a platform inside the walls (`RoofLayout`, `deck`): stone flags in running bond on a
  mortar bed, or boards. Boards run along a house's ridge (straight across a tower) in rows of
  staggered lengths, 1.4 to 3.2 m, cut at the walls, on joists 0.6 m apart running the other way; a bowed deck's
  boards ride its curved surface as its flags do. The style tool's roof wheel on a flat roof turns between the two,
  each a miniature, and new houses take the deck last turned to on this machine; the colour tool paints a boarded deck
  in woodwork's swatches.
- Rims. A deck is rimmed by its wall's top: a cap top rises into a coped parapet, a crenellated one keeps its merlons,
  or the railing top stands wooden posts on the capping, at most 1.4 m apart and clear of the corner piers, under a top
  rail and a middle one that follow the wall from post to post. The railing is a top like any other, so an open wall
  takes one too, and under a pitched roof it goes, as merlons do.
- Platforms stack. A house or tower set down on a deck stands on its cap (see "Buildings on buildings"); pushed flat
  it is a deck in turn. The deck under it stays, as its floor, and what the lower rim had under its walls goes, so a
  smaller storey leaves a terrace round it behind the lower parapet or railing.
- Eaves. A pitched roof's eaves reach `overhang` past the walls' outer faces: 0.3 m as laid, from flush to 2.5 m. A grip
  on the roof past the walls holds the eave, not the roof over the walls (`RoofLayout.eave_way`): with both hands on
  eaves, each moving out from its walls, level, pulls them on a tenth of a metre at a time, back pushes them in, and
  the pitch stays (`WallTool.pull_eaves`). One hand on an eave carries the house, as one anywhere on it does. The slope carries on straight past the walls, so a long
  eave on a steep roof sweeps low.
- Tents. No eave reaches below what the walls stand on (`RoofLayout.foot`: the ground, under a raised house's plinth
  too, or the top of the build under a storey). A long eave on low walls stops where its slope meets the ground, so the
  roof comes down to it on both sides like a tent; a tower's cone does the same as a round tent. This is the roof the
  original makes by pulling a roof very low.
- Dormers. The windows tool pointed at a gable roof's slope opens a dormer there (`RoofLayout.dormers`, as chimneys
  are placed: along the ridge, across it to the dormer's front). A dormer is an upright boarded front with a cottage
  window, boarded cheeks back to where its eaves meet the slope, and its own little gable of tiles under a ridge
  running back into the roof; on thatch it stands out of the coat. Its front stands between the walls' centre line and
  as high up the slope as keeps its ridge 0.15 m under the roof's, clear of the gable ends; dormers on one slope keep
  0.25 m apart, and dormers and chimneys keep clear of each other. A roof too low for one turns the press down with a
  knock. A press on a dormer takes it away; resizing the house carries its dormers along as it carries its chimneys. A
  roof pushed flat, or pulled too low for its dormers, keeps them, unlaid and uncounted, and they stand again when it
  has room. A tower's cone takes none.

Cost: a dormer counts as a fitting, its front and cheeks as stone wall (boards are laid as stones are) and its slopes as
tiled roof; a longer eave counts its plan out to the eave, a tent's only down to the ground. A wooden deck counts as a
flat roof. The stress scene's
`roofs/*` phases fill its village with them in turn: three storeys decked in boards behind railings, each 0.72 the size
of the one under it; two-storey stone terraces behind parapets; and steep houses with two dormers a slope and 0.9 m
eaves, or one and 0.6 m.

`tests/test_roofs.gd` lays them headless: boards inside the walls at the deck's height, staggered, on joists, a tower's
inside its ring, a bowed deck's on its surface; a railing round a deck and on an open wall, gone under a pitched roof;
three platforms stacked, each on the one under it with its terrace kept; eaves out to their overhang and onto a gable
or cone; a low roof's eave on the ground and none under it; dormers' pieces in their room, their panes facing out, their
ridges under the roof's, room and spacing refused, on thatch looking out over the coat; the budget, the command and
the style wheel. Through the tools, the windows tool opens a dormer, takes it away and undo brings it back, two hands on
the eaves pull them out, and a deck boarded and railed takes a platform on it. `demo/roofs.json` renders dormers, a
boarded deck behind a railing under two stacked platforms, a tent, a round tent and deep eaves.

## Tools in hand

Directive (verbatim): "I'm thinking we represent each tool as an object in hand, or perhaps an object
attached to the little controller. I like the minimalist little controller geometry. When we select
something, like walls, we could display a representation of the item that we've selected … probably on
top of the little controller."

Each hand holds one tool, any tool in either hand, so the two can be dual-wielded: walls, fences,
windows, arches, houses, towers, stairs, decorations, paths, stone, flowers, grass, moss, trees, bushes, boulders, ponds, colour, style, camera, selfie camera, mirror, seeds, easel, the picture book and delete. A hand
holding a tool is drawn as that tool alone, with no controller, built from the same generators as the full-size
thing (see "Held tools and aiming"): a short wall of stones, a short fence, a wall pierced by a pair of windows, a low wall flying a
flag with lanterns on it, a bend of dirt, a disc of stone ground
fraying into meadow, three flowers, a grass tuft, a tree, three bushes, a pond in a
sandy dish, a painter's palette with dabs of colour, a small camera (for the selfie camera, its lens beside the screen on the back), a hand mirror, a clay dish heaped with bird seed, a paintbrush reaching past the hand for the easel, and a spade held blade ahead for delete. A new tool
pops up in place of the old one, which shrinks away, in under a fifth of a second. There is no text anywhere.

A hand can also hold nothing, for when the building is done and the player just wants to be in the garden.
No slot holds that: opening the wheel and letting go with nothing picked empties the hand (`Hand.Tool.NONE`).
An empty hand is drawn as the controller's own rounded body, with nothing on it, and no tool aims from it. It still does
what every hand does whatever it holds: its grip grabs what it points at, and a grab of the sky shows the sight at the
controller's front while in use. Picking any tool brings that tool's sight back. Nothing hides on a timer.

Directive (verbatim): "When both hands are empty self avatar should be displayed. Player should be able to see their own hands arms and bod."

With both hands empty the player sees their own avatar from inside it: its hands round the controllers, its arms and its
body, posed as everyone else sees it, with no controller body drawn over its hands; a hand a reach throws out from the
avatar's is drawn as the controller's body again (`Rig.shows_hand`). The eyes draw it headless (`Avatar.headless`): the
same meshes again on the avatar's own skeleton, on `Quality.EYES_LAYER`, which only the eyes see, with every vertex the
head or a bone under it moves drawn at the eyes, so what joins the head to the neck lies in planes through the eyes and
shows edge on. The cameras and the mirror see the avatar whole, head included, on `SELF_LAYER`. Holding a tool in either
hand hides it again. On a Quest 2 the body in view costs nothing measurable: in `demo/self-cost.json`, looking down at
the built-in avatar with empty hands, three phases each way read 6.08, 6.13 and 6.12 ms of GPU time a frame shown
against 6.14, 6.14 and 6.16 hidden, one more draw call and 384 more primitives
([record](../perf/quest-2/self-cost/20261030T022822Z-c3c7908.json)).

### Held tools and aiming

Directive (verbatim): "I've learned that it is I think it's more fun to aim down some sort of reticle or aiming
affordance than it is to use a laser pointer. I think each of our tools or at least many of them can be just the objects
themselves held in your hand No, uh, no hand object, just the object, just the tool you're holding"

Directive (verbatim): "The magic wand is a good example of this"

Directive (verbatim): "Teleport does not need the pointer" ... "Well, teleport doesn't need the ray."

No ray leaves any hand. A hand holding a tool is drawn as that tool alone where the hand is; the controller's body
shows only on an empty hand. Every tool that points, and an empty hand grabbing the sky, aims through one path
(`Hand.point`): a sight stands at the tip of what the hand holds (`ToolModels.tip`), tinted as the tool's ray was and red
when the budget refuses, and a mark sits where the aim meets something, growing past 4 m from the eyes
(`Hand.MARK_NEAR`) so it keeps its apparent size with no line leading the eye to it. Each tool's own highlight (the
delete ring, a wall's preview, a fitting's outline) is unchanged. Teleport draws nothing from the hand: its halo alone
shows where it lands.

The wand is the pattern: a thing whose shape already points. It is held pointing ahead along the aim, as long as a
hand's tool (`Hand.HELD`, three times its wheel icon), its star at the tip with the sight just past it; the spade is
held the same way, blade ahead. Every other tool is held as the miniature it was, and its sight stands just ahead of
it. The split, by how each aims:

| Tools | Held as | Aims with |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Style | The wand, star ahead, hand-long | Sight past the star |
| Delete | The spade, blade ahead, hand-long | Sight past the blade |
| Walls, fences, windows, arches, house, tower, decorations, boulders, stairs, paths, stone, flowers, grass, moss, trees, bushes, ponds, colour, mirror | Its miniature | Sight just ahead of it: these aim at the ground or a build, and their own previews mark the target |
| Camera, selfie camera | The camera | Its lens and screen, unchanged: it never had a ray |
| Seeds, easel, picture book | The dish, the brush or palette, the book | Unchanged: they work by reach and touch, not by aim |
| Nothing | The controller | Sight at its front, only while it grabs the sky |

The sight is a ring standing across the aim at the tool's tip, to look down the tool through, the same on both
hands. It is one small unshaded mesh per hand in place of the old beam, with the same mark at the target, so the
frame costs what the ray did: one draw each, under 200 triangles.

Other players see the held tool where the hand was: their avatar's hand folds away while it holds anything but a bird
(`HeldItem.replaces_hand`), as the player's own body does in a selfie. `demo/held-aim.json` renders the held tools,
the sight on each hand and the teleport halo from the eyes.

One value per hand, `Hand.tool`, is what the input code dispatches on and what the object on top and
the wheel show, so they cannot disagree. The hands start as walls in the right and the picture book in the left (see "Picture book").

Directive (verbatim): "In Half-Life: Alyx, you hold a button down, and then you move your controller
around a wheel to select an item. Uh, the button to be held down should not be the thumbstick, because
that's kind of hard to click. … And there should be eight items on the wheel. … And then that opens up
Um, either item, or it opens up the subtree. Now the subtree, has another four options."

Directive (verbatim): "I think it'll be a fun motion if the controller goes left and right, then left
and right. And so it- then the user will learn those combinations."

Hold a hand's wheel button and a wheel of eight icons stands upright where that hand is, facing the
head (`Hand.TREE`, `ToolWheel`). Sweeping the hand toward a slot makes it hot, and it grows; letting
go of the button takes the hot tool, and letting go with nothing hot empties the hand. A slot is a
tool or a subtree, drawn as its tools in miniature; every icon is tipped toward the eye, as a
controller's top is seen, on a pale disc. Each icon is a miniature of what its tool makes: stone ground's
is a disc of mossy stone painted by the ground's own stone code, fraying into meadow at the rim. Reaching a
subtree hides the rest of the wheel and opens its tools beside it.

The wheel's icons take no light from the garden. Directive (verbatim): "I think the lighting on the objects in
your inventory wheel need to be cleaned up. Some of them are too bright, some of them are too dark I think um it
would be worthwhile simplifying that system, whatever it is. Seems like there's some accretions going on in there I
don't think world lighting should be applied to those things at all. Not the things in your wheel". At night the
icons had split three ways: those with plain materials were drawn unshaded and so took the night's higher exposure,
paling toward white; the trees, bushes and stone fittings, drawn as multimeshes, and everything with a shader of its
own (stone ground, the book, stairs, windows, flags) still took the moon, the ambient sky and the lanterns' bounce,
and went near black. Now one rule draws every surface of an icon (`Hand.unlit`) in one look (`icon.gdshaderinc`):
its colour, shaded by a light fixed to the eye so its form reads, with no fog, and with the view's exposure and
saturation divided back out (the global `view_grade`, which `Look` sets beside the exposure), so an icon looks the
same at noon, under the moon and on a moonless night. A plain material gives its colour, picture and vertex colours
(`icon.gdshader`); the two surfaces that paint themselves, stone ground and the book's cover, each have a twin that
paints the same and ends in that look (`stone_disc_icon.gdshader`, `book_icon.gdshader`); any other shader gives its
vertex colours, tinted by the colour it names, and a lantern's glow, which has no colour of its own, is left out.
The pale disc behind each icon is opaque, so the meadow behind it no longer shows through darker at night. Reached by a sideways swing, three or
four stand in a column on the side away from that swing, so a deep pick zig-zags: right onto planting, left
onto trees. The top slot holds two, opening one to each side: up, then left for colour or right for style.
The slots never move, so each tool is one learnable motion:

| Slot | Holds |
| --- | --- |
| Top | Colour, style |
| Upper right | The cameras (camera, selfie camera) and the mirror, seeds, easel |
| Right | Flowers, grass and moss, the placed plants (trees, bushes), ponds |
| Lower right | The people (avatars to wear, nearby gardens to join), the picture book |
| Bottom | Delete |
| Lower left | Paths, stone, stairs |
| Left | Walls, fences, windows, arches |
| Upper left | House, tower, decorations, boulders |

Directive (verbatim): "Okay, two things regarding the style menu. The style icon, it just looks like another house, so it seems like to a player, it'll seem like you just pick the style icon to make a different style of house. But no, that's not it. Second, the style icon should be in the same grouping as the color selector icon. You go, right now it's up. So you would go up and then you'd select the co-, select color, or you'd select, select style"

The style tool's icon is a wand with a star at its tip, which no build resembles.

Report (verbatim): "It's not possible to select a tool in your left hand right now". Directive (verbatim): "I'd like you to uh holistically rethink the button mappings. Um. One restriction is, I don't want to have to press the thumbstick down to select a tool"

The wheel button is each hand's upper face button: B on the right, Y on the left (the Frame's B
on the right, its D-pad left on the left). Directive (verbatim): "Make it dpad left. To match the quest button position." The left wheel used to open on the left menu
button, which on a Quest 3 never opened it. Choices made where the directive left room, for a playtest
to revisit: the wheel is a flat disc standing vertical in front of the hand, 12 cm to a slot, like
Alyx's; selection is on release, as in Alyx; a subtree opens on reaching its icon; backing out of a
subtree means letting go and opening the wheel again.

Directive (verbatim): "I think making teleport its own item was- was a mistake for me. And I think we can get rid of it, and keep the- the old teleport"

Teleport is not a tool. The left stick pushed forward aims from the left hand whatever it holds, so no
choice of tools leaves you unable to move, and aiming at the ground is how you come back down. The same stick turns,
so one thumb moves the body and the right stick stays free for avatar menus.

Directive (verbatim): "Free up our right joystick in preparation then by putting our snap turning onto the left joystick."

Directive (verbatim): "When I'm teleporting... I'd like to be able to... hold forward on the teleport button... and then... lean my stick back just a little bit... and I'll teleport... less far when I do that. Teleport... For example, into the sky. slowly bring the stick back"

How far the stick is pushed sets how far along the aim the target lies, so the reach is the
same stick as the aim. The other stick no longer moves an air target nearer or farther: two controls
for one distance did not read together, and the air reach is a fixed 30 m to ease back from. A
released stick springs through every smaller deflection on its way to centre, so the target reaches
as far as the stick did 0.1 s before; the preview shown when the stick reaches its deadzone is the
one target, and the release lands there.

Report: the landing preview stood only part of the way along a cast across the map, short of where the teleport
then landed. It ran back toward the player as the released stick sprang through smaller deflections, while the
landing read the target from 0.1 s before; now the delay applies to the stick, so preview and landing are one point.

Directive (verbatim): "I'm testing out the teleport movement, the ability to teleport only a short distance, and in the sky it is what I wanted, but on the ground I would only like to teleport along the ray that I am casting. I don't want to choose a location on the ground that is closer to me. When I pull back on the thumbstick slowly after extending it, I want the teleport location to walk back along the ray that I'm casting, not along the ground. So the only way I would teleport to the ground is if I'm holding the thumbstick straight forward and then I release quickly. Otherwise I should be teleporting to a location along the ray that I have cast."

An eased target is a point on the ray whether the ray ends on the ground or in the air, and the
landing there is the same float over whatever floor lies below it. Only a release from full forward
reaches the ground the ray hit.

Directive (verbatim): "I just wanna be able to raycast onto a sloped roof for a tree or something and still actually go there, because that's what I would expect as a player"

The ray stops at the first solid thing it meets, the ground, a roof of any pitch or its gable end, the top of
an open wall, a tree or a bush, and a release from full forward stands the player upright where it hit. There
is no steepness limit: the player does not walk, so nothing slides off a slope. A wall's face lets the ray
through, so a door or window still aims at the floor inside.

Directive (verbatim): "I see that there's a distance limit on my teleport raycast. I can't teleport all the way across the map right now, but I would like to be able to do that"

The ray reaches as far as the map does: anything on it the player can see, one teleport reaches. An upward ray
also looks for ground across the map and floats only when it meets none. Hand tremor of a fraction of a degree
moves a target 200 m away by metres, so the aim follows the hand through a filter that holds still against small
wobble and catches up with a deliberate turn. Past 15 m the halo grows with distance and ignores the fog,
so it looks the same size far away as it does nearby. The march along the ray skips each 8 m block of ground
it stays above, so a ray across the whole map costs less than the old 80 m one did. `demo/teleport-far.json` aims
from one corner of the map to the other.

Report: with the mountains in place, teleporting far horizontally no longer worked; the ray seemed to hit the mountains.

Directive (verbatim): "I'd like mountains to behave like skybox as far as teleport is concerned."

Report: aimed toward the mountains' foothills, the teleport target vanished and there was no teleport; the cast
should clip to the map's edge.

Everything past the map's side, the mountains and the ground before them, is sky to the ray: it never meets them,
and its cast ends at the side. A ray that hits nothing on the map stands the player on the floor at the side where it
leaves; aimed above the horizon it floats them at the 30 m air reach or at the side, whichever is nearer, so the
player never stands or floats out past the edge. Ground, roofs, walls and trees on the map stay targets up to the edge.

### The landing preview

Directive (verbatim): "Let's uh, let's keep the destination halo, but let's make it give me some uh mockups, some ideas for something pretty to make it, something pretty and whimsical"

Directive (verbatim): "Sketch some more outside the box approaches, including the players own avatar. Consider cool visual effects if free."

Directive (verbatim): "Let's do avatar preview, but not ghostified. And show current pose."

Where a teleport will set the player down, their own avatar stands, drawn as everyone sees it (`Landing`): a second
copy of what they wear (`Avatar.scene`, `Avatar.tint`), turned to face as they face, its head and hands held each frame
where the player holds theirs now about the floor under their head, so they see themselves arrive before they go. Past
15 m it grows with distance, as the ring did. Nine looks were tried first, flowers, fireflies, dandelion seeds,
toadstools, petals, a ghost of the avatar, footprints, a sunbeam and a bubble; the plain avatar was kept.
`demo/landing-preview.json` aims beside the head and waves the other hand, so the preview shows in profile waving
with it. `tests/test_teleport.gd` checks it faces as the player does and holds their eyes and hands.

Its cost while aiming is a second avatar's draw, skinned on the GPU as every avatar is, with no shadow (`Avatar._dress`).
On a Quest 2 (`demo/landing-cost.json`: six seconds aiming a near preview, six at rest, four times; built-in avatar
0; ten runs alternating this build with a control of the same commit whose `Landing.put` returns at once, so the arc
still draws; each phase's first second left out) aiming costs the game's GPU 0.198 ± 0.004 ms a frame over rest with
the preview and 0.190 ± 0.002 ms without it (median per-second GPU time, mean ± standard error of five runs each): the
arc is about 0.19 ms and the preview +0.008 ± 0.005 ms, within noise of nothing against `Budget.line_ms()`'s 11.1 ms,
where the scene sits near 8.0 ms. Its per-frame pose adds no CPU time the runs resolve: script time aiming over rest
moved -0.12 ± 0.09 ms with it and -0.17 ± 0.05 ms without.

### Something underfoot

While the player floats more than `Rig.LIFTED` over the floor they were set down over, a soft round shadow lies on that
floor straight below their head, fading in over the first metre (`Underfoot`): standing on nothing tens of metres up is
the classic trigger of fear of heights and of lost balance, and the shadow ties the feet to the ground. Nothing marks
the air at the feet. The shadow is one unshaded quad, placed from the height alone, with no query a frame.
`demo/comfort-cost.json` on a Quest 2, 8 m over the stress garden looking down (a view already over budget either way),
bounds it at 0.35 ms of GPU time, so it is a per-build option, `Underfoot.SHOWN`. The eyes' solid test in the same run cost no GPU
time (12.31 ms tested, 12.41 not). `tests/test_teleport.gd` checks where it stands, that nothing else shows, and that
it hides on the ground.

Directive (verbatim): "I'd like to be able to use the grab button to grab the ground so ground terraforming becomes um... not a tool, it's just something that's always available. I just grab something. Grab the ground and push it down or pull it up"

Directive (verbatim): "The grab button should always be grab"

Directive (verbatim): "It doesn't grab and drag the tool, it grabs and drags the thing you're pointing at"

The trigger is the held tool's action; the grip grabs what the hand points at, whatever it holds, and
never builds. It takes the nearest thing its ray meets: a house or tower (by its walls or roof), a wall, a tree or bush, a
boulder, a placed fitting, or the ground (a pond's bed under its water), with open sky taken by the grip too. Shaping the ground
is therefore no tool (`GroundGrab`); the ground is the last thing a grip takes, after anything else
that claimed the hand that frame.

Each hand's grip is its own grab, because two grabs at once feel good: one hand can hold a pond's bed
while the other pulls a roof, a wall or more ground, or uses its tool, and each grab is its own undo step. Only what is already taken in that
same hand, or one thing in both, holds a grip back:

- Ground in both hands pulls two humps, one per hand; on the same ground they add, and each sets its
  own heights when let go.
- A hump being pulled is this player's preview, not the garden: plants, walls, boulders, easels and the player
  standing on it or touching it follow it, but every command, the animals, decorations standing on the ground, the
  lamps' and the sun's bounced light read the ground without it until its heights land, as every other machine does.
- A build the other hand holds, a wall, fence, house or tower: a grip on it joins in to size it, or on a roof with the
  first grip there too to shape the roof (see "Moving and sizing a house"); a grip pointed past it takes what it
  points at. One build is held at a time: a grip on another wall or house meanwhile takes nothing.
- A fitting the other hand holds: a grip on an opening joins in to size it; on any other fitting it takes nothing.
- The sky has one holder; the other hand's grip on it takes nothing.
- Undo and redo wait while either hand has an edit under way.

Directive (verbatim): "When grabbing and moving the sun... I don't think we should use translational movements of the controller, I think instead, the moving the pointer is appropriate for that. Additionally, I would like to not grab the sun, but just grab the skybox and rotate it"

A grip on open sky (no ground or build on the ray), or pointed within 7° of the sun or the moon wherever
they stand, grabs the sky, so a sun under the ground can be brought back by pointing at it. Held, the sky turns about
the axis of the sun's arc by however far the pointer turns about it, so the point grabbed stays under the
pointer as far as the sky can turn; the sun's disc, glow and light ride on it, and the hand's position
counts for nothing. The sight stays shown, since aiming is the control. The sky turns on round the
whole day, through the night and past midnight. The clouds stay put: they are weather, not the dome.

### Reaching far

First contact with grabbing should be satisfying: reach out to a building far away and take it, then stretch it or
move it, with no pointing, since pointing suits a gun and little else. Go-Go reach (Poupyrev et al., 1996) does that,
calibrated to each player's own arm so a child with short arms reaches as far as an adult (`Reach`).

- The hand follows the controller one to one out to two thirds of the player's arm. Past that, each further
  centimetre throws the hand much farther, along the line from the player's shoulder through the controller: by the
  square of how far into the last third the arm is stretched, up to 24 m at full stretch (`Reach.FAR`). Drawing the
  arm back reels the hand in along the same curve.
- The grip takes what the hand touches: the first thing within 0.3 m of it along its aim, before or after it
  (`Reach.TOUCH`), by the grip's existing order (a build, a fitting, a plant, a boulder, stairs, an easel, the
  ground). Nothing far is taken by pointing at it, and a reaching hand never grabs the sky, which only a pointing
  hand turns. What is taken then moves with the hand one to one, a house too, which a pointing grip moves farther the
  farther its middle is: the projection already carries a far hand far, so a held house moves, turns, is stretched by
  two hands and is reeled in by the existing operations. A hump of ground is as wide as one pulled from where the
  controller is.
- The arm is measured, never assumed: its length is the farthest the controller has been held from the shoulder,
  across the ground. The shoulder stands below the eyes and to that hand's side by fixed fractions of the arm, so a
  small player's shoulder is as near their eyes as their arm is short. Until the player has stretched out, the arm
  is taken as 0.36 of the eyes' height above the floor, a proportion a child and an adult share; any stretch past it
  replaces it, and while the arm is not stretched the learned arm shrinks by half a percent a second, never below
  that first guess, so the next player to wear the headset is learned anew within minutes and a hand held out never
  creeps. No age, height preset, profile or device enters it, and a child and an adult throw the hand equally
  far at the same fraction of their own arm.
- Across the ground, so a hand hanging at the side is never stretched, and reaching forward and down still
  throws the hand along the arm's line onto the ground ahead.
- How far the hand is thrown follows the stretch at 4 Hz, plus 2 Hz for each metre it lags, so a held arm holds the
  hand still against tremor and a deliberate reach catches up, as the teleport ray's aim does.
- Thrust reach (`Reach.Mode.THRUST`) throws the same hand along the same line by a different measure: how far the
  controller has been thrust rather than how far the arm is stretched. A move away from the shoulder faster than
  0.8 m/s (`Reach.THRUST_SPEED`) throws the hand 16 m farther per metre of it (`Reach.THRUST_GAIN`), a move toward it
  as fast brings the hand back as far, and a slower move carries the hand one to one, so after a thrust the arm is
  drawn back at ease with the hand left out. Thrusts add up to 24 m and back to the controller, no farther. A held
  house is brought near by thrusting in. The shoulder and the steadying are Go-Go's; the stretch counts for nothing.
- A thrown hand meets the world as a near one does: it stops at a wall, a roof, the ground, a trunk or an animal and
  feels it (see "Hands at surfaces"), and the grip takes what it is stopped at. A stretch or thrust past the
  surface carries it no deeper unseen, so drawing back or thrusting in takes it off the surface at once. The teleport
  ray is cast from the controller, never from a thrown hand.
- Only an empty hand reaches (`Hand.REACHES`): the far grip is what has moved to it so far. A hand holding any tool
  stays where the controller is and keeps its pointer, its grip included, as does a hand opening the wheel, so no
  hand is ever thrown out and pointing at once. Tracked hands reach as controllers do: the mapping reads the hand's
  pose alone.

Until a headset playtest keeps one, reach is a debug switch rather than a choice of builds: clicking the left stick
in while it is centred cycles every hand from Go-Go reach to thrust reach to the pointing grab they replace (`Reach.mode`), so one
session compares them; each owns the empty hand's far grip alone. No other action uses a stick's click, and a click while the stick aims a teleport or flicks a
turn, as a thumb pressing hard makes by accident, switches nothing; nothing shows the switch to a player. The picture book's pages still teach the pointing grab, and the tests that grip by pointing
check it. Once one is kept, the switch and the other go. `tests/test_reach.gd` checks the mapping, the
calibration of a short and a long arm, thrusts out and in, a far house taken, moved and reeled in through the grip
in each, a thrown hand stopped at a house's front, and the teleport ray cast from the controller of a thrown hand;
`demo/reach.json` films Go-Go and `demo/thrust-reach.json` thrust.

## VR controls

| Action | Control |
| --- | --- |
| Pick a hand's tool | Hold that hand's upper face button (right B, left Y; on the Frame right B, left D-pad left), sweep the hand onto a tool, or onto a subtree and back across onto a tool in it, let go |
| Teleport onto the ground | Left stick forward, with any tool: aimed straight from the left hand, it lands where it first meets something solid: the ground, a roof of any pitch, a wall walk, a flat roof or platform, stairs, a bridge or a tree's crown from above; aimed under a roof, as through a door or window, it lands on the floor inside; one that strikes a side, as of a wall or a crown, lands at its foot (see "Standing on what you built"); release to go |
| Teleport into the air | Aim above the horizon: with nothing on the map for the ray to hit, it targets the point in the air 30 m along it, marked by a ring there alone: the headset's depth places it, so nothing marks the ground below. Release to arrive there in one cut; the view then stays put until the player moves or acts |
| Teleport less far | Ease the stick back while aiming: full forward reaches the ray's target, and the target slides back along the ray as the stick comes back, down to a tenth of the way, floating above the top surface beneath it, the ground or what is built there, even when the ray ends on it. Release from there to go there: the snap back to centre does not pull the target in |
| Come back down | Aim the teleport ray at the ground |
| Turn | Left stick flicked left or right, while it aims no teleport: 30° snap turn |
| Turn round | Left stick flicked back: 180° snap turn, for seated play |
| Restyle | Trigger held on a hand holding the style tool, pointing at a gable end, a roof, a roofless wall's top, along a wall, at a floor, at a tree, a boulder or painted stone: twist the wrist to turn a wheel of miniatures of that thing's styles, live on the build; let go to keep the one at the top (see "Styles") |
| Draw a wall | Trigger held on a hand holding walls, sweep the pointer across the ground, or along a build's top to build on it; release to finish. Strokes under 1 m are discarded, and a stroke near a standing wall merges into it |
| Move a wall or fence | Grip pointing at a wall or fence, whatever the hand holds: it moves along the ground with the hand and turns about its middle as the hand turns, the gain growing with distance so far walls are easy to carry; let go to set it there, one undo step |
| Size a wall or fence | Holding it with one grip, grip it with the other hand too: spread or close the hands to draw it longer or shorter along its line, about its middle; one over the other, draw them apart for a taller wall or together for a lower one, a course at a time (a fence keeps its height) |
| Shape a roof | Two grips on a roof, whatever the hands hold: drawn apart upright for a taller ridge, together for a lower one, all the way down snapping it flat; spread across for convex slopes and squeezed for concave slopes (stone decks bow too). Two grips on the eaves, past the walls: each hand out from its walls pulls them out, back pushes them in |
| Put up a house or tower | Trigger of a hand holding the house or the tower: a square house or round tower stands where it points, on the ground or on a build's top for an upper storey, its front square to the head. The trigger only places it |
| Move a house or tower | Grip anywhere on it, roof included, whatever the hand holds: it moves and turns with the hand, the gain growing with distance, and rises onto its plinth as the hand rises, or comes back down; let go to set it there, one undo step |
| Move stairs | Grip on a flight, landing, bridge or ladder, whatever the hand holds: it moves with the hand and turns as the hand turns, the gain growing with distance, its lower end never under the ground; let go to set it there |
| Widen stairs | Holding stairs with one grip, grip them with the other hand too and pull the hands apart, or push them together to narrow them; a bridge narrowed all the way is a single rope |
| Size a house or tower | Holding it with one grip, grip the same house with the other hand: spread or close the hands across its front for width, along its flank for depth, or across a tower for its diameter; one over the other, draw them apart for taller walls or together for lower ones (see "Moving and sizing a house") |
| Paint flowers or grass | Trigger held on a hand holding flowers or grass, pointing at the ground; the ring shows the brush, which widens the farther you point |
| Paint stone ground | Trigger held on a hand holding stone, pointing at the ground; a stroke begun on stone paints it back to meadow instead. The ring shows the brush, which widens the farther you point |
| Plant a tree or bush | Trigger of a hand holding trees or bushes, pointing at the ground |
| Set a boulder down | Trigger of a hand holding boulders, pointing at the ground, takes up a metre-wide boulder of a shape never seen before, in the stone swatch walls are being laid in; carry and turn it as a grip carries a placed one, and let go to set it down there. A tap sets it down where it points |
| Move a tree or bush | Grip pointing at a tree or bush, the meadow's own too, whatever the hand holds: it moves along the ground with the hand, the gain growing with distance as a wall's does, and turns about its foot as the hand turns; let go to set it there, one undo step |
| Grow or shrink a tree | Holding a tree with one grip, grip it with the other hand too: spread or close the hands and it scales by their span, from 0.4 to 3 times the size it grew at; letting one go leaves the other carrying it at its new size |
| Move a boulder | Grip pointing at a boulder, whatever the hand holds: it moves with the hand, up off the ground too, the gain growing with distance, and turns about its centre as the hand turns, every way, so it can be tipped onto a face or stood on end. Let go and it drops straight down onto the ground under it, keeping its turn, or, pushed into the ground, stays as deep as it was let go, with at least 15% of its height showing: it is never left in the air (directive (verbatim): "Boulder should not be placeable in mid air") |
| Grow or shrink a boulder | Grip a boulder with one hand, then the other: spread or close the hands and it scales by their span, from a pebble of 8 cm up to a hill, a giant costing no more than one 3.5 times a metre wide; letting one go leaves the other carrying it at its new size |
| Move a fitting | Grip pointing at a placed door, window, arch, lantern, flag, chimney, dormer or decoration, whatever the hand holds: it moves with the hand over what it stands on, the gain growing with distance, and stops at the last place it fits; let go to set it there, one undo step (see "Moving a fitting") |
| Size a door, window or arch | Holding an opening with one grip, grip it with the other hand too and spread or close the hands: it scales by their span, and letting either go leaves the other carrying it (two hands size a tree or boulder the same way, one `WallTool.Sizing`). It grows every way from where it stands, from a pet door's size to the largest that still opens without closing another: an arch about its springing up to its ring under the wall's top, so a tall wall carries a grand arch; a door or window up from its sill. A path's arches are the path's and take no grip |
| Shape the ground | Grip held pointing at the ground, whatever the hand holds (a pond's bed too): the ring shows the hill's width; raise the hand to pull a hill, lower it to press a hollow, with a soft click in the hand for every quarter metre the ground rises or sinks; release to set it. Each hand grabs on its own, so both can shape the ground, or one holds it while the other grabs or builds something else |
| Fill a pond | Trigger on a hand holding the pond tool, pointing at the ground beside a hollow: the hollow fills to the height of that point, as its pale sheet showed (see "Water") |
| Delete | Aimed at a lantern, window, arch, flag or chimney, the ring encloses that fitting; a press takes only it on release, even if the hand moves while held. A body hit encloses the whole house or tower. Stairs are separate targets. Fitting deletion uses the same first-surface query as placement and the normal shared command/undo history. Trigger of a hand holding delete: hold it and sweep, and everything the beam passes over goes in that one stroke. Along a wall the stretch swept breaks into a ruin as you sweep; a tap breaks a short gap; a sweep leaving nothing worth standing, or any touch on a fence, house or tower, takes it whole. A tree or bush the beam strikes goes, and so does the path or stone ground it meets, whole; elsewhere on the ground it mows the grass and flowers under the ring, raggedly at its edge. Ruins and taken plants show as it goes, paths and stone go when you let go; the stroke is sent as one command when you let go, so one undo puts all of it back. Held pointed up at the sky, an orb swells at the beam's end for 2 s and then the whole garden is wiped (see Wipe); letting go or lowering the beam first lets the orb go |
| Wipe | Directive (verbatim): "This world is really full of stuff, and I don't really have a great way to delete it all. I think I need a way to Wipe the whole world". One command (`wipe`) takes the garden back to bare meadow: everything anyone placed goes, walls, stairs, paths, stone, easels, decorations, boulders, planted and painted plants, ponds, ground edits and each player's standing mirror, and so do the meadow's own flowers, bushes and trees; its grass and the season stay. The wipe empties one list, everything that stands what players place (`World.holders`), so nothing placeable is left out; `tests/test_wipe.gd` places one of each and wipes, as host and as a visitor. A fresh garden still starts with the meadow's scenery; a wiped one is kept wiped. It is one undo step: undo rebuilds the garden from the snapshot the wipe took, as a late joiner's is. Anyone in the garden, host or visitor, can wipe it |
| Undo | Tap left X (the Frame's D-pad down): one step back per tap, across every tool (walls, towers and houses drawn, raised, arched, roofed, restyled, erased into ruins or deleted, trees, paint strokes, stone ground, paths, ground shaped, ponds dug, fences, windows and decorations). With others, your own steps only, skipping any another player has built on since (see [multiplayer.md](multiplayer.md)) |
| Redo | Tap right A: one step forward per tap. A new edit after an undo drops what could be redone |
| Choose an avatar | Hold the wheel button, sweep on into the hand mirror: the wheel becomes a dial of every avatar, built in and loaded, starting on the one worn; twist the wrist to turn it and let go to wear the one at the top |
| Join a garden | Hold the wheel button, sweep on into the gate: a dial of your own garden's cairn and every garden near, starting where you are; twist to a player's figure and let go to join their garden, or to the cairn while visiting to go home |
| Throw seeds | Trigger of a hand holding seeds: a handful leaves the dish the way the hand is swinging, or falls from a still one, and scatters where it lands (see "Feeding") |
| Lay a path | Trigger held on a hand holding paths, sweep the pointer over the ground; the ring is the path's width, wider the farther you point. Through a wall it opens an arch or an arcade, through a fence a gate |
| Draw a fence | Trigger held on a hand holding fences, swept like a wall |
| Open a window or door | Trigger of a hand holding windows, pointing at a wall: a window of the family last turned to where it points, a door if that is low on the wall and the family opens one; on a corner's pier, a corner window; on a flat roof, a trapdoor; beside another window of its family it fuses with it; on a window or trapdoor it closes it |
| Cut an arch | Trigger of a hand holding the arch, pointing at a wall: an archway through it where it points, shown on the wall before the press; on one of the wall's own arches, the press fills it. A path's arches and gates stay the path's |
| Hang a string | Trigger held on a hand holding decorations, from one point to another 1.5 m or more across: let go, a string of lanterns or flags hangs between them, on a pole where an end is the ground |
| Decorate | Trigger of a hand holding decorations, let go where it went down: a chimney on a house's roof, a flag on a tower's roof or a wall's top, a lantern on a wall's face (on the side the hand is) or a fence; a present at a house's foot, on open ground, on a house's floor or on a flat roof, its lid drawn with the Style tool's own wand and turned to face the hand; the preview is the present a press places. Style turns it into any decoration on any of those surfaces (planter, bench, rain barrel, woodpile, steps, gatepost lantern, standing torch, campfire, chair, table, settle, bed, shelf, rug, stool, chest, potted plant, candle stand, table and chairs, garden bench, trough planter, parasol, string lights, lantern, lamp post); on one already there it takes it away |
| Turn the sky | Point either hand at open sky, whatever it holds, grip and aim: the sky turns with the pointer, carrying the sun round the day and the moon 100° behind it |
| Pick a colour or season | Trigger held on a hand holding the colour tool, pointing at a wall, roof, gable, fence, window, door or lantern, or the meadow: twist the wrist to turn the wheel, let go to keep the swatch at the top on that build (or, on the meadow, the garden's season) |
| Copy a look | Tap the trigger of a hand holding the colour tool on a wall, house, tower or fence: the beam takes its colour. Each tap on another build lays that look there, one undo step; a tap on the meadow lets it go (see "Moving and copying") |
| Take a photo | Trigger of a hand holding the camera: the screen on its back shows the shot for a moment |
| Stand a mirror | Trigger of a hand holding the mirror, at the ground: it stands there facing the player; at its glass, it is put away |
| Record a video | Hold the trigger of a hand holding the camera: its shutter button glows red while it records; let go to stop |
| Set up an easel | Trigger of a hand holding the easel, pointing at open ground with no easel near the hand: it stands there facing the player |
| Move an easel | Grip pointing at an easel's legs or tray, whatever the hand holds, with no brush, palette or canvas within the hand's reach: it moves along the ground with the hand and turns about the vertical as the hand turns, the gain growing with distance, its painting and kit with it; let go to set it there, one undo step, unless that is within 1.4 m of another easel, when it goes back. The other hand's grip on it does nothing: an easel has one size |
| Paint | At an easel: grip at the pot for a small round, big round or flat brush, at the sticks for charcoal, at the watercolour box or the oil palette to carry it in that hand; dip the brush in a pan or an oil colour for that medium, in the glass to wet it; touch the canvas and push in to press harder (see "Painting") |
| Fresh sheet | Grip on the canvas's face and pull well away: the sheet tears off, one undo step |

### Tracked hands

Directive (verbatim): "Let's support hand tracking."

Put the controllers down and the game plays the same with bare hands. A tracked hand stands in for the controller it
replaces: each gesture presses the input that controller's finger would, so every action in the controls table
(`Controls.ACTIONS`) reaches the hands through one small table beside it (`Controls.HAND`), and a control added for
controllers is played by hand with nothing more. No tool and no action knows which it is reading.

| Gesture | Presses | So it |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Thumb to index | The trigger | Uses the held tool: draws, plants, paints, takes a photo, deletes |
| Fist | The grip | Grabs what the hand points at: the ground, a build, the sky, a brush at the easel |
| Thumb to middle | The upper face button | Opens the wheel; held, sweep the hand onto a tool and let go |
| Thumb to ring | The lower face button | Undo on the left hand, redo on the right |
| Thumb to little | The stick, pushed by the hand (the left; the right does nothing) | Held, the hand moved from where it pinched pushes it: forward 10 cm is the stick full forward, so a hand pinched and pushed out aims a teleport, eased back toward where it pinched brings the target back along the ray, and letting go goes; a swing sideways snap-turns that way, and one back turns round |

A pinch closes when the thumb tip comes within a fifth of the hand's length (wrist to the middle knuckle) of a
fingertip and opens past two fifths, so it neither flickers nor sticks; only one is held at a time, the first to
close. A fist is the middle, ring and little fingertips all curled within half the hand's length of the palm; it holds no
pinch, since the thumb lies against the index in a fist. The hand aims where it points: its pose is the palm's, the
fingers ahead and the back of the hand up, as a controller is held, so a tool aims, twists (colour, style) and sweeps
the wheel with the same code and the held tool stands in the palm. Nothing on a bare hand rumbles; every cue still
sounds.

Which a hand is comes from what the runtime reports, never from the headset: a hand is played by its joints while the
runtime reports them as seen from the hand itself (OpenXR's unobstructed hand-tracking data source), or, where it
cannot say where they come from, while it reports no controller pose for that hand. One hand can be bare while the
other holds a controller.

Choices left to the in-headset check: the pinch distances, whether an empty bare hand should still show the
controller's rounded body (it does now, one look either way), whether the aim needs steadying against joint tremor as
the teleport ray has, and whether the picture book's pages, which show controllers, should show hands to a player
without them. `tests/test_lessons.gd` plays every page twice, once by controller and once by tracked hands made into each
page's gestures (`HandShape`), and checks the same effects; `tests/test_hand_tracking.gd` checks which joints the runtime
reports play a hand. `demo/bare-hands.json` plays a session by bare hands alone (pick, plant, draw a wall, undo, redo,
pull a hill, teleport, turn, pick with the left hand) and digests the garden after each, on a headset or the desktop.

## Picture book

Report (verbatim): "a picture book does sound kind of fun. It could be really cute. Maybe a picture book of little videos"

Report (verbatim): "When a When a player spawns in, they spawn in with the book in their hand with the page open. They can turn the page with their other controller."

Report (verbatim): "It spawns in in the left hand. Tutorials will prefer to use the right hand when possible. So the player can be looking at the book and trying the things in the tutorials"

Report (verbatim): "Just for a little artistic flourish, let's actually spawn in with the book closed, and we'll have some visual indicator that the player should use their other hand to open the first page"

Every player, a co-op joiner too, starts holding a small cloth-bound book shut in the left hand (`Book`, the tool
`Hand.Tool.BOOK`). Its ribbon bookmark hangs from between the pages, swaying and glowing softly; the right hand touching
the cover or the ribbon swings the cover open onto the first page, the board overshooting a little and settling. A sweep
of the other hand leftward across the open pages turns to the next page and rightward to the one before, wherever on the
spread it starts; a pass of that hand through the book from behind turns the page it comes out over, the right one on
and the left back, at any speed or angle, since the book finds where the hand's path crossed it between readings; one
sweep or pass turns one leaf, so the hand lifts off the pages before the next, and the stroke back from a turn never
undoes it. A hand resting on the pages, hovering over them or passing beside the book turns none (`tests/test_book_turn.gd`). Back from the first page shuts the book. No button is pressed, so the right hand's tool never acts while it reads, and the left hand's grip still
grabs and its stick still teleports. Picking another tool for the hand tucks the book away (it shrinks into the hand in
a fifth of a second); the wheel's lower-right slot holds it beside the mirror and the gate, and taking it brings it back at the page it
was left on. The book is each player's own and is never sent to others.

Each page is one control a player would not guess from the tool in hand, shown as a little live picture with no words:
ghost controllers act it out on a loop over a patch of meadow set with the game's own tool models (`LessonScene`), so the
pictures always show the game as it is built and no image or video is kept in the repository. The controller is generic,
with only what every supported controller shares: a trigger under the index finger, a grip under the middle finger, a
thumbstick and two face buttons (`Pad`). A pressed part sinks and glows and a soft ring pulses out of it; a held part's
ring fills as the hold is timed, so the sky wipe's two seconds can be seen. A trigger or grip page looks from beside and
under the hand, so the pull shows; a button or stick page looks from over it. A page uses the right hand wherever the
control allows, so the player can hold the book in the left and try the move with the right while looking at it.

Directive (verbatim): "Let's make the book pretty"

Each picture fills its page inside a deckled, inked frame with sprigs over its corners, and is painted rather than
pasted on blank paper: the page's shader (`book_page.gdshader`) lays a watercolour world behind the live render (a sky
of the page's own hour of the morning with soft clouds, two bands of far hills on the horizon the picture's camera
sees, and a brushed straw-and-green meadow running to its foot) and inks a line around everything the render draws.
The ghost controllers are drawn 1.7 times life size, a pressed part swells and its rings are wide enough to read at
arm's length, and each camera frames the controllers' whole bodies. The undo page leaves a pale ghost where the build
stood until redo brings it back, the wipe takes each build away in a puff, and the sky page's sun crosses a sky of the
hour it sets. `demo/book-view.json` renders every spread from the player's own eye with the book held to read.

| Page | What the hands do | Why it is not obvious |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Pick a tool | Hold the right upper face button, move the hand onto a slot, let go | The wheel opens only while its button is held |
| Teleport | Left stick forward, a halo shows where it lands, let go to go there | Movement is not a tool |
| Undo, redo | Tap the left lower face button, then the right one | Two hands, no tool shows it |
| Shape the ground | Right grip on the meadow, raise the hand | The grip shapes the ground whatever the hand holds |
| Turn the sky | Right grip on open sky, sweep the pointer | The sky is grabbable and carries the sun |
| Twist to choose | Hold the right trigger on a build with the colour tool, twist the wrist | A wheel turned by the wrist |
| Move and size a house | Right grip on a house carries it; the left grip joins, the hands spread, then the right rises | One hand moves, two size: a grip, not the house tool |
| Wipe the garden | Hold the right trigger with delete pointed up at the sky for its whole two seconds | A hidden hold on the sky |

Left out on purpose, as fun to find or plain from the tool on top: petting, feeding and calling animals, wildlife
arriving, throwing seeds, painting at the easel and tearing off a sheet, a path opening an arch or gate through a wall,
windows fusing, lanterns, photos, visiting, snap turning, each tool's ordinary trigger action, and the colour wheel on the
meadow turning the season.

The pages are one table, `Lessons.PAGES`: each page a script of both hands' poses and of the actions held, and the
garden it starts from. The same script drives the page's ghost hands and `tests/test_lessons.gd`, which plays it through
the real hands and tools in a garden and checks the effect the page shows (the wipe page's input wipes, the house page's
input carries a house, stretches it and makes it taller, and so on), and that every action it presses is still in `Controls.ACTIONS` on that
hand. A control changed or removed turns `scripts/test.sh` red until its page is redrawn.

Only faces of an open book render, each every other frame, and only while the book lies within 60° of where the head
faces; a shut or tucked book renders no page. On the
desktop renderer (RTX 2080, 1600×1200, `demo/book-cost.json`, the renderer's timestamps around each viewport), a page
renders in 0.28 ms at 384×288, so an open spread costs its pages 0.17 ms a frame; the garden's own view rose from 6.85 ms
with the book tucked to 7.16 ms with it shut in the hand and 7.5 to 8.0 ms open, the paper's shading over its pixels.
The painted pages (384×358) cost no more: run beside the earlier build, four times each under the same load, the garden
view read 8.07 ms against 8.14 open on the first spread and 8.13 against 8.40 on a later one, its pages 0.21 ms a frame
in both; the backdrop is painted only where the picture leaves paper showing, and the paper's grain lost an octave and
the ink line reads the picture's alpha by its screen derivative to pay for it.

On a Quest 2 (`scripts/bench.sh`, App GPU time with the book open on its first spread paired against it tucked away in
the same run) these pages cost 2.4 ms a frame (2.36 and 2.48 ms in two runs of six pairs over a bare meadow), a fifth of
the budget: the paper's procedural paint and the cover's stamp, evaluated for every pixel of both eyes each frame, and
the layers under the leaves shaded before the leaves covered them. Now each page face and the front cover's top are
painted once into textures as the game starts (`book_paint.gdshader`, `Book._bake`); the page shader reads its paint
and paints only the picture's backdrop live, where the picture leaves it showing. The book draws its top layer first
(`Book.Layer`), its page blocks have no top or bottom, its boards' insides are only the margin around the pages and the
endpaper hides under a leaf. The same pairs now read 0.90 and 0.76 ms; in the stress scene's `book/` phases
(`perf/quest-2/stress/20261019T163748Z-10ca290.json`) the open book costs 1.2 ms over tucked, shut 0.4 ms and mid-turn
1.7 ms, the page's own render about 0.3 ms of the open book's.

## Flat-screen controls

With no headset at start the game comes up on the flat screen (`Flat`, `Controls.FLAT`): a player who walks,
looks, takes photos, visits and feeds the animals, and never edits. Its controls stand in for a controller's through
the same tool entry points; the only tools it holds are the camera, the seed dish and the mirror, raised in the
right hand before the eye, or none, both hands then hanging at its sides, as other players see it.

| Action | Mouse and keyboard | Gamepad |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Walk | W, A, S, D or the arrows | Left stick |
| Look | The mouse, once a click in the window takes it; Escape lets it go | Right stick |
| Take up the camera, the seed dish, the mirror, or none, in turn | Tab or the right button | Y |
| Take a photo, throw a handful of seeds or stand the mirror | Left button | Right trigger or A |
| Record a video with the camera up | Hold the left button | Hold the right trigger or A |

With empty hands, F or gamepad X plays the human-recorded wave. Taking up a tool
ends it. In VR, wave with the tracked hand.

The seed dish is held out low in the view and kept level (`Flat.DISH_HELD`), an offer to the animals as a
controller's is (see "Feeding"). Holding it, looking down stoops the player by up to 0.8 m (`Flat.STOOP`), no faster
than 0.5 m/s, a calm move, so the dish comes within a duck's or a sheep's reach of the ground and nothing is startled
by the stoop.

### Hands at surfaces

The hand you see stops where the controller pushes into something solid, as in Half-Life: Alyx: the ground,
a wall or fence (not its doors, windows or arches), a roof, a trunk or an animal. It slides along the surface
to the point nearest the controller and is back on the controller as soon as the way is clear; held more than
1 m away it lets go. Leaves and pond water are passed through and felt. Every tool still aims, points and grabs
from the controller itself; the sight stands on the tool you see, and other players see that tool. A hand thrown out
by a reach (see "Reaching far") is the exception: it never lets go however far it is held, slides along a surface
only as far as it is swept across its throw, so it is never carried over a roof, and while it holds nothing it stops
at the surface itself, its throw cut short there, so it grips and feels the wall it meets rather than reaching
through it. It runs after the hands move and before any tool reads them.
`HandStop` sweeps a ball the size of the hand from where it was to where the controller is each frame, against
the world near that sweep alone, so no physics bodies exist.

The eyes are tested the same way: while they are inside the ground, a wall or fence, a roof, a trunk or a boulder
(not leaves or water), the view dims to 90% black over 0.15 s and clears as fast once they are out. The view is never
moved to push the head out, which would be motion the player's head did not make. `tests/test_hand_stop.gd`
checks an eye in a wall and under a hill.

Touch is felt through one table, `HandStop.FEEL`, keyed by the surface touched: a first touch pulses with how
fast the hand came in and the surface's hardness, and moving along a surface, or through leaves or water,
plays its grain: stone rough and grainy, plaster smooth, wood and roof tiles ridged, ground soft, bark coarse,
leaves light, water quiet until a quick stroke ticks it faintly, and the animals' coats gentle (sheep wool, duck down, conure feathers).
An animal is a `Critter` whose `coat` names its surface.

Buttons are named as on the Steam Frame: A, B, X and Y on the right controller,
a D-pad on the left. The Frame binds its own interaction profile (Godot 4.6's
`frame_controller_valve`): B opens the right wheel, D-pad left the left wheel,
A redoes and D-pad down undoes. X, Y and the other D-pad directions have separate inputs
and no game action. On Quest the Touch bindings arrive unremapped, and the Touch Plus (Quest 3) and Touch Pro
profiles are bound exactly as the Touch is, so neither runtime falls back to it.

One table, `Controls.ACTIONS`, says what each hand's inputs do, and both hands are alike: trigger for the
tool, grip to grab, upper face button held for the wheel. The lower face buttons walk the history: left X
undoes, right A redoes. The left stick moves the body: forward teleports from the left hand, left or right turns,
back turns round; a stick flick reads as a button (`Hand.FLICKS`). No action reads the right stick, kept for avatar
menus. `Controls.DEVICES` lists what each shipped device's controls arrive
as, so each device's bindings derive from the one table; `tests/test_bindings.gd` holds every control of
every device and checks it does its action alone, that every action reaches a control on every hand it
names, that no wheel opens on a stick click, and that each device's profile binds every control. The Frame's
bumpers, Menu and View, the
Quest's left menu button and the right stick, flicked or clicked, do nothing; the left stick's click is the reach
debug switch (see "Reaching far").
The Frame binds bumpers and Menu to their own inputs so they cannot open a wheel accidentally;
the system buttons remain reserved for the runtime. Changing how a
thing looks is a tool, not a button: every style change lives in the style tool. `tests/test_controls.gd`
taps every Frame button with walls built, picks every tool from the wheels, and walks the history back
and forth.

### Styles

The style tool is the colour tool's picker with styles for swatches (`PaletteTool`): its trigger held on a
thing opens a wheel of miniatures of that thing's styles, twisting the wrist turns it, showing each on the
build live, and letting go keeps the one at the top, one undo step. What it offers depends on what it points
at: a gable end, its house's gable ends (the wall's, boarded, crow-stepped; houses laid after take it); a
house's or tower's roof, its covering: tile or thatch on a pitched roof or a cone (see "Thatch"), a stone or wooden deck on a flat one, since only grabbing and pulling changes its shape; a roofless
wall's top, merlons, plain capping or coping; a wall's or fence's face, its finish (see "Wall finishes"; walls, houses
and fences laid after take the finish turned to last); a window, its family, the lights fused with it alike (see
"Window families"; windows opened after take it); a house's or tower's floor, flags or boards, apart
from its walls (see "Floors"); a flight, landing or bridge, its railings, stone, wooden, rope or
none (stairs laid after take them); painted stone or a boulder, its stone style from the one table (see "Ground materials"; stone painted and boulders set down after take it); a tree, its species: an oak's round crown, a
fir's dark cone, a poplar's column, a birch's pale trunk under a light open crown, a copper beech's low
wine-red dome. Every species is the same trunk and eight foliage blobs placed differently, so a tree changes
species in the slots it holds and a forest costs what it did: on a Quest 2, `demo/forest.json`'s full tree
pool drew 24.3 ms of GPU a frame with every species in turn, against 25.2 ms all oaks. Planted trees and the meadow's own are oaks
until styled. A tree's size is apart from its species: two grips grow or shrink it in the slots it holds (see "Moving and sizing a tree"),
and the frame budget charges one unit per planted tree at every scale (the measured fit below). Ladders and bushes have no style. The style tool only restyles: it never cuts or fills an opening, which
is the windows' and the arch's work. Report (verbatim): "I dont understand why the style selector is adding
doorways. It seems the style selector seems to add archways to the house for some reason. Is that an
accident" Directive (verbatim): "Yeah that would make more sense." (to taking arches off the style wheel as
their own tool). A garden's arches are stored on the wall as where they stand, never as a face, so saves made
while the face wheel cut them load with them standing. Directive (verbatim): "I think plaster is just another style
btw." Directive (verbatim): "Let's add tree styles. Each tree style is a species."

### Moving and sizing a tree

A tree or bush is held as a boulder is, by grabbing it: a grip on its trunk or crown, the meadow's own too, whatever
the hand holds, takes it, and it moves along the ground with the hand, farther for a far one by the reach gain a wall's
pull has; raising or lowering the hand leaves it standing on the ground. It moves whole, its trunk, crown and shade
together, keeping its shape, species, size and moss, standing on the ground where it is carried. The other hand's grip
on the same tree sizes it by the hands' span, from 0.4 to 3 times the size it grew at; a bush has no size, so two hands
on one change nothing. Letting go of one hand leaves it in the other, carried from where it now stands.

Letting go of both sets it as one `tree` command, its place across the ground (`at`) and its size, one undo step; let go
within 5 cm of where it was and unsized, nothing is sent, so a trembling grip is no edit. One let go over water goes back where it stood, as a tree is never planted
in water. The garden keeps each moved tree's or bush's place with its group (`places`, beside `species`, `scale` and
`moss`), so a snapshot, a late joiner and a reload stand it there; a garden swapped wholesale grows the meadow's own
where they grew. Moving one regrows nothing: Flora shifts its instances, their anchors and its solid's place where a
ray finds it (`Flora.move`), and refits only its own shade while it is held. `tests/test_carry_plants.gd` checks the
carry, the pair, undo and the command a peer takes.

### Cost by distance

Directive (verbatim): "We also use distance lod elsewhere, right? So is density pricing applicable to other renderables too?"

Stone walls, plaster, fences, roofs and thatch are: masonry pays a far rate on all of it and a near excess on the 3 by 3
chunks around the dearest eye (`Budget.Near`). `demo/build-distance.json` measures what else draws by distance: each
cluster at the meadow's centre seen from a spot, then hidden from the same view (`layer`), twice; standing N m off
looking at it, out of view (looking away 6 m off, moss 3 m), and 22 m up N m off. `scripts/distance.py OUT RECORD`
reads it. Microseconds a unit on a Quest 2 at the fit step, the larger pair
([record](../perf/quest-2/build-distance/20261024T112939Z-d20f721.json)):

| Cluster | 3 | 6 | 10 | 16 | 24 | 32 | 48 | 64 | Out of view | Air 32 | Air 64 |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Ivy on 117 m² of 4.5 m walls, a wall m² | | 4.53 | 4.62 | 1.68 | 0.36 | | | | 0 | 0.46 | |
| 16 boulders 1.5 m wide, a unit (scale²) | | 37.5 | 28.9 | 36.5 | 29.7 | 25.1 | 32.4 | 9.3 | 3.5 | 27.4 | 10.7 |
| 256 moss patches in a 2 m disc, a patch | 7.00 | 4.78 | 4.33 | 3.42 | | 1.35 | | | 0.12 | 1.03 | |
| 256 moss patches in a 6 m disc, a patch | | | | 2.51 (8 m: 1.93) | | 2.36 | | | | 0.94 | |

- Boulders cost the same out to 48 m: their far mesh begins at 30 m times their width, 45 m here. No view sees them
  cheaper but one past every boulder, so they keep one price, now the dense near cluster's 37.518 µs (was 25.861).
