is measured in the rig's space, so a teleport or snap turn just before the trigger throws nothing. A landed
handful is an offer like a calm hand, so the same appetites find it: a conure flies down to peck at one on dry
ground, a duck swims to one on its pond, a sheep walks to one beside its pasture, each animal passing over a
handful it cannot reach for the next, taking a grain every `1 / Handful.PECKS` seconds while it eats, and leaving
when its fill is up or nothing is left. Uneaten, a handful is gone 50 to 70 seconds after landing, shrinking and
sinking away over the last six. Eight handfuls are kept (`FeedTool.HANDFULS`) and reused, another throw taking up
the oldest. A throw is where it left the dish, its velocity and a random seed for its spread, and goes to every
other player (`Session`), whose game throws the same handful, so every player sees the same seeds land in the same
places and the fish swarm them; a player's throws reach the others at most ten a second. The animals are each
player's own, so which grain each fish takes, and when, can differ a little from one player to the next.
`scripts/coop.sh` checks that a handful the host throws on the joiner's pond lands within 3 cm alike for every
player and draws every fish of that pond on each.

Butterflies and fireflies eat nothing from a hand. Fish eat only thrown seeds lying on their water. A held seed dish
costs what any tool model does;
each animal's look for a hand holding seeds is a distance check against at most two hands, the other players' dishes held out
and eight handfuls.
A handful is one MultiMesh draw call of 28 grains of 10 triangles, casting no shadow: at most eight draw calls
and 2,240 triangles, its grains rewritten only while flying or fading.

Budget: each animal is at most a couple of thousand triangles, skinned or vertex-animated, one
draw call, and only a handful are ever in view; what that costs a headset is for the stress scene's
wildlife phases to show once they run on one. Ducks are capped at two families of at most five
(`Wildlife.FAMILIES`, `DUCKLINGS`): ten draw calls and about 6,000 triangles at
most, one shared mesh per kind and one material. Butterflies are one MultiMesh capped at 24
(`Butterflies.CAP`): one draw call of 22 triangles each; the CPU steers them and the
vertex shader beats their wings. Fireflies are one MultiMesh of camera-facing quads capped at 48
(`Fireflies.CAP`): one draw call, additive glows that cast no light. Fish are one MultiMesh capped at 24
(`Fish.CAP`): one draw call of 86 triangles each, no shadows; the CPU steers them and the vertex shader
beats their tails. With no seeds on the water a frenzy costs a check of the eight handfuls a frame; in one, a fish
looks over the grains only when it picks the next. Conures are capped at three
(`Birds.CAP`): three draw calls of about 1,560 triangles each, one shared mesh and material, casting
no shadow. Sheep are capped at eight (`Herds.CAP`, flocks of `Herds.FLOCK`): eight draw calls and
about 7,000 triangles at most, one shared mesh and one material, no shadows. Deer come at most three at a time
(`Roamers.HERD`) and the fox alone: four draw calls of about 2,100 and 1,850 triangles, each kind's surfaces
merged into one mesh coloured per vertex (`Beast`), shared with one material, no shadows; each animal poses
its own skeleton of about 50 bones from its clips, with nothing riding the bones. Frogs, snakes, squirrels
and owls are one `Crowd` a kind, unshadowed: four draw calls of 772, 680, 968 and 884 triangles an animal, at most
16,944 triangles with every kind at its cap; the CPU steers them and writes each one's pose once a frame. Every
animal of a kind shares its mesh and material, through `Critter`, `Crowd` or, for the deer and fox, `Beast`.

What the four cost the Quest 2 (`demo/shy-cost.json`, `bench.sh`, the ladder pinned at its fitted step, 15): three
ponds, four planted trees and a wall, seen from 1.65 m with the frogs, snakes and squirrels in view by day and the owls
come by night, each shown and hidden twice (`layer` "shy"), median GPU milliseconds a frame
(`perf/quest-2/shy-cost/20261028T153345Z-a89d89b.json`):

| View | Hidden | Shown | Draws, hidden to shown | Primitives added |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Day, a | 5.93 | 6.25 | 58 → 63 | 6,459 |
| Day, b | 6.17 | 6.34 | 59 → 62 | 3,617 |
| Night, a | 6.03 | 6.19 | 55 → 61 | 9,441 |
| Night, b | 6.15 | 6.28 | 57 → 62 | 5,886 |

They add 0.13 to 0.32 ms against the 11.1 ms line, and the frame holds 72 Hz (p50 13.9 to 14.0 ms) shown or hidden.

On the CPU, `Wildlife` looks the walls and trees over twice a second for perches and pastures, and
between looks the animals use what it found. A wall's perch points read every stone, several
milliseconds for a long wall, so the wall works them out on a worker thread once its stones are laid
again, and not while a newer preview is being laid; a crown's are worked out when a blob of it comes
or goes and stand on the ground under its trunk as it is. At each look every sheep not leaving checks
its ground and its way, and a sheep checks walls and water whenever it picks where to go and, for
every nudge apart, where it would stand. Water along a way is looked for closely only in the grid
squares a pond reaches (`Water.crosses`).

## Night stars

The sky shader draws soft points from view direction alone, with fixed warm/cool tints and
brightness variation. `Daylight.light(time).stars` fades them through twilight and makes
them zero by day; the shader skips their calculation then. They have no position-dependent
parallax or animated twinkle. Clouds and the moon glow soften them.

`demo/stars.json` pairs stars off/on twice with the whole view looking up at midnight,
from 0.2 m above dry terrain. On Quest 2 at 72 Hz and render scale 0.7, the pairs measured
6.09 → 6.41 ms and 6.04 → 6.40 ms of GPU time per frame: +0.32 and +0.36 ms.
Median frame time stayed at 13.93 ms and 13.92 ms respectively, with zero late frames in
all four intervals. `perf/quest-2/stars/` records the run. There is no separate sky entry
in `Budget.COSTS`; this is fixed scene cost, not a charge per garden object.
`demo/stress.json` includes the same upward midnight view.

At that low dry-ground pose, `test_stars.gd` checks that the head stays put, with no
teleport fade or underwater state. The 2 cm near clip leaves terrain below the eyes;
underwater effects require an actual pond above them. No separate lying-pose classifier
is introduced. The upward captures show the sky clear of vegetation at the sampled spot.

## Photos

The camera is a tool on the wheel (`PhotoTool`). Held, a small camera stands in the hand with
its lens along the aim and a screen on its back toward the eye, showing what the lens sees. The
lens keeps the horizon level however the wrist rolls, so photos never come out tilted; pointed
straight up or down, the top of the controller is up. The trigger takes a photo
(`PhotoTool.PHOTO_SIZE`), saved as a PNG named by the date and time under `user://photos`, or
under `photos/` in the `--capture-dir` of a scripted run. From the Linux package that is
`~/.local/share/godot/app_userdata/Cairnfold/photos`. An uninstall erases an APK's user data and
its external files directory, so an APK saves photos to `Pictures/Cairnfold` in shared storage
(`Main.photos_folder`), which outlives an uninstall and shows in the headset's own gallery. Godot's
own file access reaches shared storage only in Download and Documents, so a photo goes through
java.io (`Main.write_bytes`), which scoped storage lets write the app's own pictures with no
permission. The garden save lives in the external files directory (`Main.player_path`), where it
can be copied off before an uninstall. The screen holds the shot for a
moment with a soft flash, and the hand gets a short buzz on the press. Either hand can hold a
camera, and each camera's screen shows its own lens's view.

Report (verbatim): "Well, I'm thinking whenever someone wants to take, export their painting from the easel they'll be
taking a picture of it So, I think a picture of an easel pretty much one-to-one captures intent to capture the easel's
value I just want to be able to export it in nice high resolution when a picture is taken of it"

Directive (paraphrased): export the painting's height map beside it, the height the canvas's shader builds its normals
from, as a 16-bit greyscale PNG; no normal map, which the height gives. A wide shot with a canvas incidentally in view
must not export it: a canvas is what the photo is of when it covers about a quarter of the frame, facing the camera.

**Decision: a photo of a painting is its export.** No button or menu: the shutter is the intent. A canvas whose quad,
face toward the lens, covers `PhotoTool.SUBJECT` (a quarter) of the photo's frame or more is what the photo is of
(`PhotoTool.coverage`: its corners projected through the photo's lens, clipped at the near plane and to the frame,
signed by which face is seen). Each saves beside the photo as `NAME-canvas-N.png`, its paint flat and unlit at the
canvas's own 1536×1152 (`Brushwork.SIZE`, the resolution it is painted at, about 1000 texels a metre), and
`NAME-canvas-N-height.png`, its height off the board as 16-bit greyscale, 0 to 65535 for 0 to
`Canvas.TOP` (8.25 mm), as its comment says. Both are drawn from the canvas's own textures by `Canvas.flat_view`, with
the colour and height `paint.gdshaderinc` gives the canvas in the garden, each canvas on a frame of its own after the
photo's, then read back and encoded on a worker thread like the photo. Godot writes no PNG deeper than 8
bits, so `PhotoMeta.gray16` writes the height map, its CRCs read off a native gzip trailer. `tests/gpu_photo_canvas.gd`
holds both files to the canvas's textures texel for texel. On a Quest 2 (stress run on 3281abe, two painted easels before the camera, each
shutter press saving both) `camera/shutter` held its longest frames where the run before held them, p99 195 ms
against 192 ms, the photo's own frame; its median rose with every phase of that run (`hands/walls`, before the easels,
16.7 to 19.7 ms). Occlusion is not asked: a canvas hidden behind a wall but
filling the frame still saves.

Directive (paraphrased): a turned-around version of the camera that acts as a selfie cam; while using it you can
preview what it sees, so you can hold it out and take a picture of yourself. Placement (paraphrased): it goes in a
subtree of the camera's entry on the wheel, not a new top-level slot.

The selfie camera is the same tool on the same code path: the camera's entry on the wheel opens two, the camera and
the selfie camera, whose lens sits beside a smaller screen on the back and looks back along the controller at the
holder (`PhotoTool.lens`). Held out, its screen shows the holder as a mirror would, so leaning left moves them left on
it; the photo, taken on the trigger as the camera's is, is not mirrored. The player's own avatar, whole, head
included, stands in its view as in the camera's, its holding hand folded into the wrist. Its screen's view is drawn as
the camera's is, the same size and rate, so it costs what the camera does (`camera/selfie` in the table above).
Measured on a Quest 2 in the stress run, GPU time a frame: the scene with no camera held 27.7 ms
(`hands/walls`), one camera held 30.3 (`camera/ground`), the selfie camera held out 30.7 (`camera/selfie`),
and a camera in each hand 33.2 (`camera/two`): each held camera's view adds about 2.7 ms averaged over
the frames, the selfie camera no more than the camera.

The stress scene on 7e3167d on each headset, the bench's GPU time a frame (the median of each phase's seconds after
its first: the Quest 2 runtime's `App=`, the Frame's fdinfo, unworn) and its most draw calls in any second, the
seconds with a photo's render included; the line is `Budget.line_ms()`, 11.1 ms:

| Phase | Quest 2: GPU ms, draws | Steam Frame: GPU ms, draws |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `hands/walls` (no camera) | 26.9, 229 | 18.3, 247 |
| `camera/ground` | 28.8, 421 | 19.4, 442 |
| `camera/shutter` | 29.2, 572 | 18.7, 592 |
| `camera/selfie` | 29.1, 564 | 19.2, 580 |
| `camera/two` | 31.0, 427 | 20.4, 445 |

Each photo the selfie camera took there shows the holder's avatar. It still draws a few calls fewer than the camera
(the Frame's preview seconds: 428 against 438), since its lens looks back into the walls the player stands among and
culls more of the garden than the avatar adds. Every camera phase is over the budget by what the scene under it
already costs (`hands/walls`), which no camera change can recover. A later Quest 2 run read the same: `hands/walls` 26.1,
`camera/ground` 28.2, `camera/recording` 29.3, `camera/two` 35.0, while a camera held in the light garden of the
`held/` phases holds (`held/camera` 6.6, `held/selfie` 6.7).

On the Frame at 09f8b53 a held camera's view cost 1.7 ms over `hands/walls`, its preview drawn on one frame in three,
so about 4.6 ms a draw: the preview draws the walls' stones again, and hiding them took a camera's whole cost but
0.4 ms. Two cameras drew on two frames in three (2.4 ms). Previews now take turns on the one frame in three, so a
second camera costs nothing more; each preview redraws at 12 fps while two are held, and a recording camera keeps
every turn, its video's frames.

Directive (verbatim):
> I'd like to be able to take a super graphics quality photo that just turns everything all the way up, no level of detail or anything; it can take a long time to render that frame. ... I would like to take very fancy photos. Also give a way to embed metadata about where and how the photo was taken, that'd be cool. I don't know if you can embed a whole world in it, but if the world is small enough, then maybe you could.

**Decision: every photo is the fancy one.** One shutter, one rule, no setting: the trigger always
draws the photo as well as the game can, whatever step of the ladder ("Automatic quality") the
headset stands on. A photo is 3200×2400 at 8x MSAA, its meshes at full detail
(`mesh_lod_threshold` 0) and its textures filtered 16x; it sees grass, flowers and ivy to the
garden's edge, grass blades whole at every distance, every stone with its full mesh and the
foliage's finest blobs; and its sun casts shadows to the garden's far corner
(`Look.SHADOW_REACH`) in four blended splits drawn into the eyes' atlas. The eyes' views
and a scripted run's view captures (`Main.capture`) are drawn as the ladder allows, as before.

How one scene is drawn two ways: what the ladder cuts lives on `Quality.LADDER_LAYER`, which the
headset's cameras see, and a second, uncut instance of it on `Quality.FULL_LAYER`, which only the
photo's camera sees. A wall chunk's stones and ivy each get one more `MultiMeshInstance3D` sharing
the near instance's multimesh with no visibility range; a grass or flower cell out of reach moves
to the full layer instead of hiding; the sun has a twin on the full layer, a child of it, lit as
it is. The grass, flower and ivy shaders shrink nothing for a camera seeing the full layer
(`CAMERA_VISIBLE_LAYERS`). Which blob mesh the foliage draws, which both views share, is set for
the photo's one frame only, after every script has run (`RenderingServer.frame_pre_draw`), and put
back after it draws; the headset sees that one frame drawn so too.

What it costs: nothing until a hand first holds the camera. Its screen is then a second view of
the garden at `PhotoTool.PREVIEW_SIZE` without MSAA, out to the eye's own far clip (`Rig.FAR`, the
one view range the eye, the screen and the photo share), redrawn on one frame in three:
that frame pays the scene's draw calls and shadow pass again at a small size. A camera in each hand
draws two such views, taking turns on that frame, so no frame draws both.

A photo cannot be drawn within a Quest 2's frame budget: Godot draws a viewport whole on one frame, and on a Quest 2
making the photo's 8x MSAA view and its `PhotoTool.PHOTO_SHADOWS` atlas took 60 to 250 ms of a frame, its draw 40 to
80 ms of GPU time and its read 50 to 70 ms. Kept between shots, the view's buffers left the system short enough of
memory that the engine's own code was paged back in on the main thread behind the photo's writes (a 148 ms frame).
So a photo draws a small warm view first, at preview size, until its pipelines are ready; then the player's view fades
to black over `PhotoTool.PHOTO_FADE` (`Rig.cover`: black is never brighter than what it covers), holds while the view
and atlas are made, the photo drawn and read back whole and any canvas in it flattened, its two views read back
together, and fades back one frame after the reads land: that frame copies them out of the GPU's memory, 50 to 70 ms.
The view is let go once the camera's screen stops showing the shot. Converting, encoding and writing
the PNGs runs on a worker thread, a MiB at a time to flash (`Main.SYNC_BYTES`). No frame drawn under the cover moves
the quality governor (`Quality.covered`): a missed refresh there had stepped the garden down two rungs. Measured on a
Quest 2 at 72 Hz with `demo/photo-shots.json`: no frame over 50 ms while the view showed, and the view black, from the
end of the fade out to the fade back, for 220 to 280 ms for a photo of a canvas and 130 to 150 ms for a selfie.

Where and how it was taken (`PhotoMeta`): any PNG reader sees `Software: Cairnfold <rev>`; an
iTXt chunk keyed `cairnfold` holds JSON with the lens's position, rotation and field of view, the
time of day, the garden (its host, whether visited, its command count, the meadow's and the
wildlife's seeds), the build's revision, when it was taken, and the settings above. The garden
itself, as it is saved (`World.snapshot`), packed with zstd, goes in a private chunk `neSt` when
it packs to at most `PhotoMeta.WORLD_MAX` (4 MiB); a bigger garden is left out and the JSON's
`world` says how big it was. A garden of walls, houses and painted plants packs to a few
kilobytes; ground shaped across the whole meadow adds its heights. `cairnfold -- --photo FILE`
opens a photo's garden at its time of day, seen from its lens, and keeps nothing of it; a photo
without one opens nothing.

### Video

Directive (verbatim):
> I'd like to be able to record video. The video one might be a little harder, so if it's too much of a mess, maybe we just leave that out.

Holding the camera's trigger past `PhotoTool.HOLD` (0.3 s) records instead of taking a photo, until it is let go
or the camera is put away; a tap still takes a photo, on release. The shutter button glows red while it records,
and the hand gets a buzz at the start and the end. The video is an AVI of JPEG frames (`Avi`) with the game's own
sound, saved beside the photos, named by the date and time; it plays in common players and imports into editors.

What it costs, and why it is this way: the video is the screen's own view. The screen already draws a second view
of the garden on one frame in three; while recording that view is drawn at `PhotoTool.VIDEO_SIZE` (640×480)
instead of 256×192, and every drawing is read back from the GPU asynchronously and handed to a worker thread,
which encodes it as a JPEG and appends it to the file, one task at a time so frames stay in order. So a video
costs the frame no extra draw of the scene, only the larger preview's pixels and the readback. A frame is a
moment on the video's own clock (`VIDEO_FPS`, 24, a third of 72 Hz): when play runs slower, the last frame is
written again for the moments no drawing came back for, so a video always lasts as long as it was held. More
than `VIDEO_QUEUE` frames waiting to be written drop the newest instead of holding play up. Sound is taken from
the master bus (an `AudioEffectCapture` there while recording only) as 32-bit float stereo. A second of video is
about 1.1 MB; plain AVI stops at 1 GiB, about 15 minutes, where recording stops by itself. On an APK the file
is written through java.io into `Pictures/Cairnfold`, as a photo is.

Measured with `demo/video.json` (`bench.sh`): the governor first settles on the bare camera-less scene
(`camera/none`), the ladder is then held at the step it stands on (`"step": "held"`), and the held camera pans
over a wall and a pond twice without recording and twice recording, milliseconds:

| Headset | Step held | None: GPU | Held: GPU, frame p50 | Recording: GPU, frame p50 |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Steam Frame, unworn, 72 Hz (ladder at its top step) | 0 | | 5.25, 13.92 | 5.31, 13.90 |
| Quest 2, 72 Hz | 11 | 9.43 | 10.22, 13.78 | 10.76, 13.89 |
| Quest 2, 72 Hz | 9 | 9.64 | 10.50, 13.73 | 10.97, 13.84 |

Both hold the Quest 2's 11.1 ms at the step the scene holds without a camera. An earlier run here held the ladder
at the top step, which the Quest 2 holds for no garden, and read 27.7 ms: the scene's cost at that step, not the
camera's. The first second of a recording reads high (29 ms) as its view's buffers grow to `VIDEO_SIZE`.

Where the camera's time goes on the Quest 2, read per render pass with the runtime's GPU profiler (`ovrgpuprofiler
-t`) at the held step: the preview is one more pass, 2.0 ms at 256×192 (1.1 of it the binning pass, the scene's
geometry run once more to sort it into the pass's one bin) and 3.5 to 3.8 ms at 640×480 (four bins); it draws on
one frame in three, so 0.7 and 1.2 ms a frame. Its sun shadow pass is 0.12 ms, the own avatar's skinning 0.05 ms a
dispatch, and the sky alone 0.15 ms a drawing: what it costs is the near garden on layer 1 (terrain, walls, near
grass and flowers), which the same pass without it draws in 0.9 ms. The video's
four bins are what recording adds: each bin draws the geometry it sees again, so a video small enough for one bin
would cost what the screen does, at a quarter of the pixels. What else would cut the cost is drawing less of the
garden in the camera's view or drawing it less often; each shows in its screen and its video, so neither is
taken while both views hold the budget.

On the Frame recording holds 72 Hz with no late frame, at 0.06 ms of GPU time, and every one of a clip's
137 frames is a fresh drawing. The file on an APK lands in `Pictures/Cairnfold` beside the photos and plays back
whole.

Left out: Godot's MovieWriter, which renders the main view at a fixed time step and so slows play to the
encoder's pace, and platform video encoders, which Godot does not reach from a script on either target.

### A photo's memory

Godot 4.6 keeps every MSAA sample of a view in memory (its own TODO in `render_scene_buffers_rd.cpp`; no transient
attachments), so a photo at 3200x2400 and 8x MSAA takes about 8 bytes a sample: on a Quest 2, `dumpsys meminfo` read the
game's GL memory 413 MB before a photo and 928 MB while it drew. In the stress scene, after the pricing gardens, the
headset had 546 MB available when `camera/shutter` took its first photo: the system killed a dozen apps to free the
rest, the game stalled 1.1 s (a 1078 ms script frame, then frames of 542 and 588 ms), and the headset's shell drew in
its place (#110). On a meadow with 1043 MB available the same photo drew on.

So a photo's MSAA (`PhotoTool.photo_msaa`) is the finest, 8x at most, whose buffers take no more than half the memory
the system reports available (`OS.get_memory_info`), and the PNG records the MSAA it was drawn with. The Frame and the
desktop always have the room for 8x; a Quest 2 drawing after a dense garden steps to 4x, its buffers 246 MB.


## Mirror

Directive (verbatim): "avatar[s] only sounds good." The mirror reflects people and nothing else: a place to see the
avatar one wears, and the others' beside it.

The cameras' entry on the wheel opens the mirror beside the selfie camera (`Hand.Tool.MIRROR`). Held, it is a hand
mirror (`ToolModels.mirror`, one model for the hand, the wheel and the garden); the trigger stands a mirror where the
hand points, its glass turned to the player, and pressed again elsewhere moves it there. The trigger on its glass puts
it away. It is the player's own, as a camera is (`MirrorTool`): no edit to the garden, so no command, save or other
player sees it.

Standing, it is the hand mirror made large (`MirrorTool.STAND`): an oval glass 0.8 m by 1.2 m topped above a tall player's
crown, so heads at eye height fall in its broad middle and a player a step or two away sees themselves whole. The glass shows every avatar the game draws,
each player's on `Quality.PEOPLE_LAYER` (set as `Avatar.open` dresses it, so any avatar file that opens appears with no
change here) and the player's own on `SELF_LAYER`, lit by a light of its own (`MIRROR_LAYER`, which no other view sees)
before a plain backdrop. Each eye sees it through a view of its own (`Budget.MIRROR_VIEW`): a camera at that eye
reflected in the glass, looking out of it, whose frustum is the glass's rectangle with its near plane on the glass
(`MirrorTool.reflection`), so each eye's view maps onto the glass by the glass's own coordinates (`mirror.gdshader`,
which picks the view by `VIEW_INDEX`), and the two eyes see the depth a mirror has. A flat screen has one view. Put
away it holds no view; standing, a view draws only while the glass is on screen and the eye before it. The people's
pipelines for its views compile as the game loads (`MirrorTool._ready`).

## Painting

Directive (verbatim): "There's a feature that I would like. That is a big painting easel that you can place on the ground and paint on. I'd like the painting to be pretty expressive. For example watercolors or just multiple different mediums."

An easel (`Easels`) stands where the easel tool's trigger sets it, its canvas 1.5 by 1.125 m at standing height,
tilted back 10°, with a tray in front holding a painter's kit for each medium: a pot of brushes; a black box of
watercolour pans beside a glass of water; a wooden oil palette with its eight colours squeezed out round the edge; and
three sticks of willow charcoal laid 10 cm apart, from long and thin to short and thick (24 cm by 7 mm, 15 cm by
13 mm, 8 cm by 22 mm), so the sizes read apart at a glance. An easel holds one of each tool. Nothing
is picked from a menu (`EaselTool`): a grip at the pot takes a small round, a big round or a flat brush, and at the
charcoal that stick; a grip at either palette takes it by its thumb hole, paint up
and reaching toward the other hand, and it stays in that hand as a brush does, so the other hand dips into it, until
a grip at its place on the tray sets it down. The palette a brush dips into is its medium: a tip anywhere over a pan
or an oil colour, or pushed on through the palette under it, loads that pigment in that medium, wiping off the other.
Each new contact after lifting adds 10% load, capped at 100%, on either palette.
The added pigment mixes with the paint already held, weighted by their loads in optical density
(the existing subtractive mix). Five blue taps followed by one yellow tap leave 60% load,
five parts blue to one part yellow. Each tap mixes its 10% with the held load before capping,
so a full brush can still change colour. The box shows its watercolour mix; the glass wets the brush
for watercolour, thinner each dip, and its water clouds with what is rinsed off. Oils mix as a painter mixes them, on
the oil palette's open board, a paint surface like the canvas (`Easel.board`):
painting onto the board lays strokes there as on the canvas, and touching paint on the board or the
canvas loads the brush with it, with a dip's sound and feel, so working one colour into another mixes them. The board draws its paint as the canvas does
(`paint.gdshaderinc`): a colour on it shows as it will on the canvas, never tinted by the wood under it, and stands up
off the board as thick as it lies. Charcoal takes no paint; a stick's thickness sets its mark's width.
A tool a hand shows is off its easel, as everyone in the garden sees it (`HeldItem`), so no one takes it twice: a brush
or stick is back while its hand shows anything else, and for good once that hand takes another; a palette goes back
when set down.
A stick of charcoal is held leaned back 45° from the hand's aim, its tip raised, its back end in the hand whatever its
length: aimed at the canvas it meets it at 45°, and a wrist turned up as far lays its side flat on it.
The brush
reaches past the controller; touching the canvas paints, pushing in presses harder and widens the mark, moving fast
skims the paper's tooth, and the brush runs dry as it paints. A grip on the canvas takes its sheet and pulling it
well away tears it off for a fresh one. A garden holds four easels at most, 1.4 m apart.

Held brushes and charcoal use the hand's swept collision solver against the face and
edges of the canvas and of the oil palette's board, wherever the palette is. Bristles deform up to 12 mm, then the visible hand and brush stop
while the tracked controller can continue. The rigid brush shaft and charcoal also
stop at the surface. Pressure is how far the tracked tool is pushed past the surface
it paints, by one curve for every tool, the brush and the stick's tip and side on
the canvas and the oil palette's board alike (`Brushwork.pressure`): a graze
presses at 5%, easing up to full at 20 mm (`Brushwork.PRESS_DEPTH`). A firmer press
lays a wider mark and, for charcoal, reaches deeper into the paper's tooth and
darkens more; the stick's grain pulse in the hand grows with it. Charcoal
leaves a faint first touch and deposits further pigment only with contact travel.
Its footprint follows the stick's angle: narrow at the tip, long and narrow across
its side. A stick on its side lays by how far its line sweeps, so one turned about
its middle draws a disc and one turned about an end an arc, the footprint turning
with it the short way (it looks the same half a turn round). Contact angle is kept
in stroke samples, so replay and peers lay the same marks. `demo/canvas-contact.json` captures the stopped tools and compares
charcoal taps, drags, tip strokes and side strokes.

An easel moves as most things do: a grip on its legs or tray, whatever the hand holds, carries it along the ground
with the hand, farther for a far one by the reach gain, and turns it about the vertical as the hand turns, its painting,
palettes and brushes riding with it (`WallTool.grip_easel`). Let go, it is set there as one reshape of where it stands
and which way it faces, one undo step, or put back where it stood if that is within 1.4 m of another. The canvas, the
pot, the palettes and the charcoal keep their own grips (`EaselTool.claims`): within the hand's reach of any of them a
grip paints, and the canvas hides the legs behind it from a ray. An easel has one size, so a second hand's grip on it
takes nothing.

Report (verbatim): "Okay, I'm looking at the easel and I have a few feedbacks. Um ,one, they're, the planks that support the easel are clipping through the the canvas. So, they're covering the canvas at a piece
Um, when I go to grab the paints, I'm grabbing in by the wrong side, I'm holding the little paint uh- palette by the painty part. Should be grabbing it by another part"

Report (verbatim): "It doesn't seem like I'm able to select different colors by putting the paintbrush inside of the the paint on the palette
It just stays gray"

Directive (verbatim): "Um, so I like it how you can grab the paintbrush and it sticks in your hand, but I would like to be able to stick the palette in your hand as well. Right now if you grab the palette, it doesn't stay in your hand. If you let go, it gets put back down"

Directive (verbatim): "Can we try to get that more intuitive for an artist. For example normally oil paints would be a separate pallet from watercolor paints. In for a physically inspired workflow. Also I was unable to read that the cylinder on the left was a cup of water until you told me. Might need a little work on making those items more interpretable."

Report (verbatim): "I think I may have gotten stuck at one point using what looked like a paintbrush but was actually charcoal. That may not have been exactly what happened though."

Each kit reads at a glance from standing height at arm's length, judged on renders at headset scale
(`demo/easel-kit.json`) by a reader given only the pictures: the glass holds blue water under a bright rim, the pans
sit in a white box, the oil colours stand up as glossy dollops, and the charcoal is bare crooked grey-black sticks with
no handle or ferrule, so it is never taken for a brush. Each palette is one or two baked meshes coloured by vertex.
On a Quest 2 (`demo/easel-kit-bench.json`, `perf/quest-2/easel-kit-bench/`) the kits drew 6 to 30 fewer calls than
the one palette, tin and jar before them, with GPU time within 0.11 ms in every easel view.

Directive (verbatim, voice relay): "There should be only one palette per easel when you from an easel, it should take that palette off of the easel. There should also be only one type of paintbrush per easel. When you take a paintbrush off of the the easel, that paintbrush should disappear. Same with the charcoal pencils And the watercolor palette"

Directive (verbatim, voice relay): "Please space the charcoal pencils out a little wider and make it so you can grab ones of different sizes Also, for pencils, when you grab them, I want them to be leaned back about forty five degrees to make it easier to paint with the side of the pencil. And I want to be able to select pencils of different size. Third, there's a little bug I notice with the charcoal pencil where if you paint with its side of the pencil it, it stays, it it cannot rotate while you are uh, while you are drawing with the side of the pencil. I'd like to be able to draw a circle or an arc with the side of the pencil by rotating it while painting with its side"

Directive (paraphrased): oil mixing should work like real life: paint taken on the brush is painted onto the mixing
surface, which is how colours mix, and touching that part of the surface with the brush picks up the paint there.

Directive (verbatim): "I'd like painting the palette to be as satisfying as painting the canvas So You can probably
share some code between there. I mean, I imagine. People might want to be able to paint on their little palette and
then show their friends. That'd be cool"

The board is a paint surface as the canvas is: a `Canvas` of 384 by 256 texels at the canvas's millimetre a texel
(`Easels.BOARD`), a little larger than the palette, its paint laid by the canvas's compute shader (`paint.glsl`) and
drawn over the wood within the palette's oval and clear of its thumb hole (`board.gdshader`, coloured and raised by
`paint.gdshaderinc`). One touch paints both (`EaselTool._touch`): a brush or stick stops at its face, the bristles
bending against it, the hand feels it, and it lays strokes there as on the canvas, oil's bristle streaks standing up
as impasto, watercolour as on paper, sent to the garden as `paint` commands on the easel's `palette`
(docs/multiplayer.md): every player's board replays them, a reload lays them again and undo takes them back one at a
time. The board differs from the canvas only in its size and its wood: wood shows round its paint, it lies flat, so
very wet paint on it does not run, and charcoal stops at it and feels it as at the canvas but marks only paper, so a
hand reaching for the palette with a stick leaves no line on its paint.

Any brush on either surface takes up the paint under it. The GPU holds that paint, so the surface reads it back without
waiting on it (`Canvas.probe`): each frame the tip is on it, the texels under its footprint are copied into a
64-texel square read back asynchronously, there a frame or two later. While the painter's brush lays a stroke, the
read is of the surface as it stood before that stroke (its checkpoint), what the brush lays over rather than what it has
just laid. The brush's colour moves toward that paint's as fast as a brush soaking up 1.5 times its mean height would,
half that pace stirring even a full brush; stirring keeps the brush's load, and a clean brush comes away full. A
stroke keeps the paint it began with, so once taking up has moved the brush's colour 0.15 (optical density) from it,
at most six times a second, the stroke ends and the next lays the brush's new paint: stirring lemon into blue makes an
olive green over a few strokes, and a clean brush touching it comes away loaded with it. A brush's stroke waits for the first read, so its first dab
already carries what the brush took up. Colours mix by optical density, as on the canvas. A photo of the palette
saves its paint beside it as one of a canvas does (`-palette-N.png`, and its height).

What it costs: the board's paint is the canvas's dabs over 384 by 256 texels; oil never flows, so a board costs its
strokes' dabs, one transparent quad per easel, and a 64-texel readback a frame while a brush is on it. On one Quest 2
(`demo/easel-bench.json`, the fitted quality step, `perf/quest-2/easel-bench/`), main before this change and after it
read 5.04/5.20, 5.29/5.31 and 5.04/5.19 ms of GPU time painting onto the palette, and 5.05/4.96, 5.04/5.18 and
5.04/5.11 with the painted palette in view, every phase holding 72 Hz; the paint phases over budget after are among
those over before. In the stress scene a view of a palette painted with 60 strokes read 7.05 ms (`easels/palette`). With the canvas and the board
painted by one touch (the canvas's brush now reading back what is under it too), two runs each of main and of the
change on one Quest 2 read the palette's mix phases within 0.11 ms of main and the canvas's oil and replay phases no
dearer; the charcoal phase read 11.95 and 11.75 ms against main's 11.16 and 11.30, with no cause found in the
charcoal path, which reads nothing back.

The stand's legs run parallel to the canvas a few centimetres behind its backing, so no plank crosses the painting
from anywhere a player stands. A well counts as reached over its whole footprint and through the board, because no
hand stops at a surface it cannot feel.

The canvas is simulated on the GPU (`Canvas`, `paint.glsl`), about a millimetre a texel (1536 by 1152), so fine
strokes hold up at arm's length in the headset. Colours are optical densities, mixed and layered by Beer-Lambert (a
Kubelka-Munk-like subtractive model without scattering): blue and yellow make green, glazes darken. Watercolour is a
water and pigment grid after the shallow-water models painting programs use: water flows to lower neighbours, into
dry paper only past its tooth, so edges stop raggedly where the brush left them; pigment rides the water; water
dries a share at a time and faster near an edge, so the edge holds and pigment piles into a darker rim; pigment
settles into the paper's pits (granulation) and the rest as the water thins; a clean wet brush lifts colour; very
wet paint runs down the tilted canvas. Oil lays opaque paint in bristle streaks, standing up as impasto the light
catches, picking up the colour under it as the brush empties. Charcoal catches on the paper's peaks, reaching the
hollows as it is pressed harder.

Relief normals are derived from neighbouring height texels into a mipmapped texture. Its mip levels average
normals without renormalizing; their shortened length measures unresolved slope variance, which increases
roughness rather than allowing the gloss to sparkle. Dabs, clears and restores update the affected normal texels
and their mip footprints. Water flow preserves oil height, so it does not rebuild relief. Frozen replay views
keep the matching normal mip chain alongside pigment.

What it costs: flow runs only on 64-texel tiles a wet brush touched in the last six seconds, two steps a tick at 60
ticks a second. Every canvas together runs at most `Budget.PAINT_STEPS` texel-steps of flow a frame, 300,000, split
evenly among the canvases flowing; past it the paint dries slower instead of the frame running long. A tick dearer
than a canvas's share runs over several frames, each flow step a band of rows at a time: a step reads only the texture
the other writes, so its rows run in any number of parts to the same result (`tests/gpu_canvas.gd` dries a painting
run 400,000 steps a frame bit for bit to one run whole), and what comes after it on that canvas waits its turn. A dab
costs a texel-step for every texel of its rectangle. The painter's own dabs land whatever they cost; a replay catching
up charges its dabs against the canvas's share, so it lays a painting no faster than the frame holds. A dry canvas
costs its drawing only. The quality governor reads the viewport's GPU timer, which leaves out the canvases' work, so
the canvases time their own with GPU timestamps and the governor adds them (`Canvas.gpu_ms`).

On Quest 2, `demo/easel-bench.json` holds the four-easel garden at 89.2% of the build budget. With filtered
relief and 300,000 shared flow texel-steps per frame, every phase holds the 11.11 ms cap: four easels in view
10.25 ms, four painting 9.59, their drying 8.54, watercolour 9.32, oil 9.23, charcoal 9.21 and replay 9.06
(`perf/quest-2/easel-bench/20261027T232557Z-ebe7c04.json`). The unchanged-build comparison measured watercolour
12.95 ms and oil 12.35 (`20261027T230812Z-daab9f9.json` in that directory). Keeping the million-step allowance
with filtered normals updated only when height can change measured watercolour 11.63 and oil 10.91
(`20261027T231401Z-150cacc.json`); the shared flow allowance supplies the remaining headroom. A dry canvas
needs no normal rebuild. Simultaneous wet paintings take longer to flow and dry when their work exceeds the
allowance; oil's height and pigment equations are unchanged.

The flow shader reads the immutable water input and paper through integer texture fetches. Empty texels whose
four neighbours cannot supply water write zero to the next buffer without evaluating flow or settling; every
other texel runs the same equations. Canvas resolution, wet-tile margins and drying ticks remain fixed. `demo/canvas-water-bench.json` reproduces the loaded big-water-brush phase on a single easel; run it with
`CAIRNFOLD_SCENE=demo/canvas-water-bench.json scripts/bench.sh OUT --android APK` on a Quest.

On Quest 2, the loaded-water phase measured 15.52 ms before these changes, against the then 12.50 ms. A first candidate
measured 12.11 ms but a repeat took 12.64 ms, so the empty-region check was simplified again: it fetches four water
values without querying their tiles. The next two runs measured 11.51 and 11.68 ms; the latter used the original
four-phase fixture and held all phases (idle 10.23 ms, charcoal 10.52/10.35 ms). It recorded 29 late painting frames
against the baseline's 220. The records, including the repeat failure, are in `perf/quest-2/canvas-water-bench/`.

GPU timestamps on an instrumented baseline put water flow at a median 5.883 ms per painting frame and dabs at
0.020 ms (medians of per-second averages); the rest of the frame was not decomposed. Either optimization alone
still exceeded budget (13.42 ms with the original empty-texel exit alone, 13.16 ms with sampled reads alone).
Passing the GPU-time gate does not establish zero missed frames. Original and optimized textures matched bit for
bit at 16 wet/dry snapshots across watercolour, oil, charcoal, edge strokes and complete drying.

On the Steam Frame (`demo/easel-bench.json`, 72 Hz, the then build budget 12.5 ms of GPU time), frame milliseconds at the
median and 99th percentile, late frames, and viewport GPU milliseconds, for the canvas's budget before (8 million
texel-steps a frame catching up, at most 4,000 dabs) and after (one budget of 3 million, dabs charged):

| Phase | Before: p50, p99 | Late | GPU | After: p50, p99 | Late | GPU |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Watercolour | 13.91, 16.66 | 0 | 10.02 | 13.86, 16.16 | 0 | 10.02 |
| Drying | 13.88, 15.94 | 0 | 9.03 | 13.90, 15.21 | 0 | 9.05 |
| Oil | 13.88, 17.34 | 0 | 7.98 | 13.89, 16.82 | 0 | 7.99 |
| Charcoal | 13.87, 17.65 | 0 | 7.96 | 13.86, 16.94 | 0 | 7.95 |
| Replay after an undo | 27.77, 55.44 | 274 | 15.95 | 13.84, 16.27 | 1 | 9.30 |

Catching up at 8 million texel-steps the replay ran at half rate throughout, its flow taking 5.5 to 7.9 million a
frame and its dabs up to 1.1 million more; five more runs before the change read 58 to 279 late frames. The one late
frame after is the undo itself, 33 ms applying the command. The replay now takes longer: eight seconds in, about half
the bench's strokes are back on the canvas.

Brush load stays constant while painting, including across separate strokes. A paint-source tap adds
`EaselTool.DIP_LOAD` (10%) after lifting, on oil sources and watercolour pans alike;
the jar fills the brush with water, and switching medium starts a fresh 10% paint load;
a clean brush picking paint up from the mixing board fills too. A sideways wipe or a dab pressed into the cloth on
the tray lip removes a quarter of the load, once until the brush lifts, down to a 15% dry-brush floor.
A brush already at or below the floor stays unchanged. Only an actual unload gives the wiping hand a distinct
45 ms pulse, without sound. The cloth takes a smear of the current colour. Charcoal is unaffected.
The cloth folds over the tray lip: its inner edge rests on the tray, its middle rests on the wooden lip,
and its outer edge hangs vertically down the front. It shares the easel transform at every player height.
On the brush itself the painted part of the bristles empties from the ferrule, the last paint staying at the tip, and loses its gloss as load drops,
without a text readout.
`demo/brush-load.json` captures the same stroke and bristles at full load, after one wipe and after three.
`demo/towel.json` views the supported cloth from three angles and two player eye heights, then dabs and wipes it.

Every stroke is a build command (`paint`, docs/multiplayer.md): the brush's medium, colour, strength and load, and
its samples (where on the canvas, how hard, the flat brush's angle, at which tick), packed in two ints each.
`Brushwork.Stroker` turns samples into dabs the same way on every machine, and every dab lands at a known tick of
the canvas, so replaying a kept painting (loading, joining, or after an undo) lays the same picture the painter saw;
a replay on this machine's GPU matched the live painting bit for bit. The painter's own canvas lays the stroke as it
is painted; other players' canvases play it at the pace it was painted. Undo takes the last stroke off.

Each time a canvas comes to rest at a stroke's end, with no other stroke under way on it, it keeps a checkpoint: a
GPU copy of its wet and dry paint (the paper never changes), its tick and its wet tiles, under a hash of every stroke
laid so far. A rebuild (undo, redo, a stroke the garden turned down) goes back to the checkpoint of the longest
prefix of the strokes it is given, or to a blank sheet, and replays only the rest, so undoing the last stroke is one
texture copy however long the painting is. Paint still wet at the checkpoint carries on drying from the same tick, so
it dries to what a fresh replay dries to, bit for bit (`tests/gpu_canvas.gd`). A canvas keeps six checkpoints, the
most recently made or used, each two 1536×1152 half-float textures (28 MB), made only once that canvas is painted
on: undo steps back six strokes at a copy each, and further back replays from the nearest checkpoint kept or from
the blank sheet. `demo/canvas-undo.json` times undo at 10, 50 and 200 strokes: how long from the undo until the
canvas shows the painting without its last stroke, before (a full replay) and after (a checkpoint), on the Steam
Frame unworn and on a Quest 2:

| Strokes | Frame before | Frame after | Quest 2 before | Quest 2 after |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 10 | 3,393 ms | same frame | 7,128 ms | same frame |
| 50 | 35,623 ms | same frame | 69,869 ms | same frame |
| 200 | 149,686 ms | same frame | 295,263 ms | same frame |

Before, the canvas showed the old painting for the whole replay (the 10-stroke row replayed 9, the bench's brushed
stroke not having landed that round). After, the restore runs in the frame that applies the undo, logged in the same
millisecond, and a screen recording of the Quest 2 at 200 strokes shows the stroke gone from one 33 ms video frame to
the next. On the Frame no frame of the undo/50 or undo/200 phases ran late after the change; on the Quest 2 the late
frames in the three seconds after an undo come no faster than in the three seconds before it, the drying canvas's own.

`demo/easel.json` sets up three easels and paints a landscape on each, one per medium; `demo/oil-mixing.json` paints
blue and then yellow onto the oil palette, stirs them green, dabs a clean brush into the green and paints with it; `demo/easel-bench.json`
measures painting on a headset, and `demo/costs.json` what an easel costs a frame.

## Verifying on a headset

`scripts/package.sh linux-arm64` exports the aarch64 build (the game binary plus its
library art in `steam/`). Installed as `~/cairnfold` on the headset, it starts from a
VR shortcut in the Steam library that runs `cairnfold/cairnfold`. `cairnfold -- --script
FILE --metrics FILE --capture-dir DIR` plays a scripted session through the same entry
points as the controllers (teleport, float, draw a wall), logs per-second frame
times and saves view captures. `scripts/test.sh` runs the headless layout tests;
`scripts/render.sh` plays a script on a desktop renderer; `demo/flora.json` paints,
plants trees and lets the ivy grow; `demo/round-wall.json` draws a round wall that stays a wall, a house with no door, one with a door added and one a path runs into; `demo/tower.json` carries a tower to where it stands; `demo/gables.json` looks up at a stone and a plaster house's gable ends and a barn's in each style; `demo/thatch.json` thatches a stone and a plaster house and the village around them; `demo/houses.json` draws a round wall, builds two houses and
walls in each top style, and shapes the roofs; `demo/paths.json` lays paths through walls (an arch, an
arcade), draws a wall across a wide path, raises the ground under a path, runs a narrow path into a house (a door) and puts
another house up over a wide one (a double door); `demo/fittings.json` opens windows
and doors in a wall, a house and a round wall, hangs lanterns, flies flags, builds a chimney and draws a fence a
path crosses; `demo/stone.json` paints stone ground into the meadow, paints part of it back and
walks the history; `demo/wheel.json` opens the right wheel and zig-zags to trees, windows and delete, empties the hand, then goes up to colour and style; `demo/photo.json` takes the camera from the wheel and takes two photos, each beside the view through the same lens as the headset draws it, the second at the ladder's bottom step; `demo/video.json` lets the governor settle on the scene without a camera, holds the ladder there and pans the held camera across a wall and a pond, twice without recording and twice recording, so `bench.sh` compares the two with the scene alone; `demo/fireflies.json` digs a pond, paints flowers and drags the sun down to dusk; `demo/ducks.json` digs a pond and waits for ducks (`--seed N` fixes which arrive);
`demo/birds.json` digs a pond, plants trees and draws a wall and a fence, waits for the conures and looks
at a perched one from in front and from the side;
`demo/feeding.json` builds a pond, trees, a wall and a fence, waits for the animals, then holds seeds out to a conure, the ducks and the sheep, throws handfuls to the sheep and onto the pond and watches the uneaten ones sink away;
`demo/sheep.json` draws a fence, waits for a flock and looks at it close up and from afar (`--seed 40` puts sheep in the close views);
`demo/horizon.json` looks at the mountains from the meadow, the rim and the air, at morning, dusk and dawn;
`demo/daylight.json` drags the sun from dawn to dusk and on to midnight, looks at the moon high at dusk, the moonlit midnight and the dark before dawn, opens the colour wheel, recolours a roof, a wall's stone,
a fence and a gable, and walks the meadow through the seasons; `demo/paint.json` paints two walls and two houses
each in colours of their own; `demo/stroke.json` takes two trees, a wall stretch, a path and meadow in one held delete stroke and puts them back with one undo; `demo/ruins.json` erases stretches of walls into
ruins and walks the history. Run with `--garden FILE`, it keeps its garden there, and `demo/garden.json`
run with the same `--garden FILE` loads it back and writes a digest equal to the first run's `redone`.
