### Local play-session history

Session logging is off by default. A `record-sessions` file in the player folder (`Main.player_path`: user data,
or on a Quest `/sdcard/Android/data/io.github.bddapbot.cairnfold/files/`, which adb reaches) opts that device in;
explicit `--record-sessions` writes it, and `--forget-sessions` or removing the file opts out. A path in the file, or
`--session-directory PATH` with the opt-in, chooses another local folder. Enabled play records the one-second rows
`Metrics` writes (the same rows as a bench's `--metrics` record) in the player folder's `sessions/`, without
sending them anywhere: a dev tool pulls them off a device of ours, and `scripts/sessions.py DIR` reads them. Each
row's `activity` holds that second's frames by what the player was doing (`Metrics.ACTIVITIES`): frames, late
frames, the worst, a half-millisecond frame-time histogram and summed CPU and GPU ms, so a session's in-motion p99
merges exactly.

Beside the rows the same chunks hold what a replay starts from and plays: a `replay` record once per session, with its
seeds (the global generator's, which `Main` draws and sets as it starts, and the wildlife's) and the garden it began
in (`World.snapshot`, deflated), and with each row an `input` record of every frame since the last, deflated and
base64'd. A frame (`Replay`, laid out in each chunk's start record) is the frame's delta, the head's pose and each
hand's controller pose in the play space (each the basis and origin as the engine holds them, so a replay puts them back
bit for bit), its stick, the inputs the controls table reads on it, held or not, and whether it was tracked, bare and
reaching: 174 bytes, written on the log's worker thread. With each row too goes a `net` record of every message the network brought since
the last (`PlayLog.heard`): each the frame it came in before, its sender, the player it is about and the networked call,
listed in the record's JSON, with their arguments as they arrived in Godot's own binary encoding (`var_to_bytes`),
deflated and base64'd, which `Replay.messages` reads back. That is every build command the garden applied, each about
the player who made it: elsewhere as the host sent it (`_commit`), on the host as it ordered it (`ordered`, from 0, this
machine, beside the `_propose` it came in as), so a replay tells what a host ordered from what it was sent. With
them go the refusals and the garden a host sends a joiner, each remote player's appearance, poses, held items, throws and
spring grabs, players coming and going, and this machine joining a garden (its address and port), going home, or
being dropped by the garden it visited. Beside them, from this machine, go the player's own look as the session begins
and whenever it changes (`look`: their built-in avatar and colour, or their file, as its hash) and the headset leaving
focus and coming back (`paused`, `resumed`). A `checks` record keeps what the session's state has
come to every `Replay.CHECK_EVERY` frames, and the garden's digest whenever it is kept, for a replay to compare
(replay.md). An
avatar file a message carries is kept as its SHA-256 and length, never the file. Every networked method records what it
hears before anything else (tests/test_net_record.gd holds them to it). `scripts/coop.sh` records every player's session
and checks that each record holds every build command the host ordered after that player came, every player's among
them, and every other player's poses. Nearby gardens' beacons and the avatar previews fetched from them are not kept. Sixty-four chunks of at most 4 MiB retain recent history;
rotation can discard the beginning of a long session, and `scripts/sessions.py` counts the frames lost. Each chunk repeats the
session start, build revision and device model; a chunk is renamed into place
holding that start record, and each second's lines are flushed together as they are written. A quit the
engine reports (the window closing, the XR runtime ending the session) writes the end
record first; a missing end means the process was killed (Godot does not report
SIGTERM), not a measured end time.

No recording holds microphone or voice audio unless a performance take is armed: a `capture-performance` file
(`PlayLog.ARM`) a dev tool puts in the player folder for one acted session. The switch never sets it and the game
never writes it. The first session to start after it removes it at once, whether or not that session records, so the
arm covers that session alone; an arm older than ten minutes (`PlayLog.ARM_SECONDS`) is removed unused, so one left by
a take that never started records no one. On a recording device that session's `Take` plays the microphone into a muted
bus of its own, so it is never heard, and hands what it captures to the record; the log's worker writes it as a mono
16-bit WAV at the mix rate, `SESSION.wav`, beside the chunks, its header whole after every write. Every chunk's start
record names the take and its rate, and a `take` record the input frame its first sample came in before. When the
record ends `Take` frees itself and its capture; the muted bus stays, since the microphone's last buffer, fading
out, would play on the master bus if its own were gone. A take goes from the device when the ring drops the last
of its session's chunks. The project enables audio input, which opens no device until a take plays the microphone.
The APKs our headsets run declare the microphone permission and the store and Steam ones do not
(`scripts/android.sh` checks both). Android grants it only by asking the wearer, and once granted the engine keeps the
microphone open past the take, so `Take` never asks: the dev tool that arms a take grants it beforehand
(`adb shell pm grant io.github.bddapbot.cairnfold android.permission.RECORD_AUDIO`), and without it the take records
nothing. tests/test_take.gd plays a tone where the microphone would be heard and finds it in the armed session's take,
none of it leaving the take's bus, and no take in the session before it or the one after.

Replaying a record, on the build that made it or a later one, and where a replay diverges from its session:
[replay.md](replay.md).

Monotonic time between frames measures delivered application cadence, not CPU work or compositor
reprojection. `cpu_ms` summarizes Godot's process-time monitor; `gpu_frame_ms`
summarizes available viewport GPU timer readings. Unsupported GPU readings are
null. Kernel fdinfo probes run only for explicit benchmark output, so ordinary
local logging does no per-sample filesystem reads on the frame thread. Runtime dropped/reprojected counts are unavailable through this recorder;
`late` is an application-cadence estimate, not a runtime dropped-frame counter.
Context includes category counts, settled masonry instance counts, tools and
visit/player state. A row lists each frame over `Metrics.STALL_MS` (50 ms) in `stalls`: its length, what the player was doing, the
script time read on it and on the frame after (the engine reports a frame's script time once it has finished), the
pipelines compiled, and what the game noted as running long in it (`Metrics.note`: a build command applying, a garden
restored, a style wheel opening, a photo drawn at its finest, a wheel opened), so a stall in a played session is named
from its record. Time the tree spent paused (the headset off, the app in the background) and the game's loading is
`paused_s`, never a frame. `logging_us` measures the previous second's sampling and
worker submission work, excluding per-frame sample collection. Disk writes run
on the engine worker pool, with at most one pending sample; a slow write skips
samples instead of delaying a frame. `writer_us` measures the previous write
and `skipped_log_samples` records any skipped samples. Explicit `--metrics`
benchmark output remains available separately.



## Paths

The path tool wears its surface, soft-edged, into the ground mask: dirt (trodden earth), sand, cobbles (the river
cobble stone style), flagstones (the mossy one) or a garden bed (tilled earth, darker and lumpier). The style tool on
a path opens a wheel of the five, each a miniature of that surface; letting go resurfaces the path (a `reshape` of its
`surface`, one undo step), and paths laid after take the surface let go last. A garden bed is the path tool on its bed
surface: one stroke tool, one history, one budget. Each surface wears its kind of ground (`PathLayout.wears`: earth,
sand or stone), so a later path lies over an earlier one down its middle and the two blend across its soft edge, every
texel there part of each, the noise breaking the line; lifting the later gives the earlier back. Cobbles and
flagstones lie on worn earth, so the stone tool painting meadow back over them leaves the path's way worn, and the path
still there to pick. Paths open arches beneath walls and
can be selected with Delete; undo restores the path and its crossings. `demo/dirt-path.json` shows the dirt
surface, and `demo/paths.json` every surface, a junction of four, stepping stones across a pond and three beds.

Dirt reuses the ground material and adds no path draw calls on gentle ground; sand and tilled earth are a few more
ALU operations on the mask texel the terrain already reads; cobbles and flagstones are painted stone and count their
ground as painted stone does (`stone_m2`, held to `Budget.STONE_VIEW_US` with it). Where the path rises more than one
metre in two, horizontal stone treads cover the slope, overlapping so no ground shows between them, and a timber
stringer runs the length of each flight along either edge, from the ground at its foot to the ground at its head,
standing a little proud of the treads it carries. They follow the terrain when a ground edit settles, and disappear
when it is flattened. Paths reserve their whole surface as stone face in the build budget, so shaping the ground
cannot add unbudgeted steps.

Where the path runs over water at least 4 cm deep, it lays flat stepping stones a stride (0.7 m) apart along its
spine instead of treads, stepping a little to either side in turn, each standing on the bed with its top 5 cm out of
the water; they come and go as a pond fills or drains under the path. A bed lays no treads: its rows run along it 0.4
m apart, as many as its width holds, a plant every 0.32 m staggered row to row, each row one crop chosen by the bed's
seed (cabbages, lettuces, carrots or leeks), none in water; on a steep slope the rows simply climb it. A plant is a few coloured pieces drawn as wall stone
(`PathLayout.crop`): a cabbage's pale head in three spreading leaves, a lettuce's rosette, a carrot's fronds over its
orange shoulder, a leek's pale stem under a fan, so beds add no draw calls, and casting no shadow they add no shadow
instances either; the budget reserves every piece of a bed at a wall stone's share (0.1 `wall_m2`). A path's treads,
stepping stones and crops are laid on a worker thread and placed when done: a 20 m bed's 1,200 pieces took 8 ms to lay
on a desktop's main thread, and the Quest 2's cores are slower.

Cost on a Quest 2 (`bench.sh OUT --android APK` with `CAIRNFOLD_SCENE=demo/materials-cost.json` at `Budget.fit_step()`,
the bench's GPU ms a frame): the bare meadow read 5.29 from the air, 4.78 looking over the beds' ground and 5.41 down the
cobbled ways, against 5.34, 4.83 and 5.39 on the build before the surfaces, so the sand and tilled shading costs the meadow
nothing measurable; with the stress scene's materials laid, the same views read 7.11, 8.85 (six full beds filling the
view) and 6.43, every one inside the budget ([record](../perf/quest-2/materials-cost/20261025T131357Z-76d9f9d.json)). The stress scene's `materials/aerial` and `materials/ground` phases look over
the meadow's paths in cobbles, flagstones, sand and dirt, stepping stones across a pond and six full beds. On the
Steam Frame, governed, both hold 72 Hz ([record](../perf/frame/stress/20261025T142017Z-1e4323e.json)): 4.6 ms of GPU
time a frame from the air and 4.5 on the ground, at step 6.

## Stairs

Report (verbatim): "I liked the stair slash bridge object that you had in a previous build. It didn't work in that build, but I did like the idea. Um, and I would like to see it in the real game"

The stairs tool lives beside paths on the tool wheel. Hold the trigger at one end,
point at the other, then release. Either end can be ground, a wall top, a flat roof
or an existing landing. A level stroke along the ground is a landing, and one between two high points hangs as a
bridge (see "Bridges"); a near-vertical run becomes a wooden ladder. A flight, landing or ladder runs as long as a wall
can, its treads and railings charged by the metre against the build budget. Railings are stone until
the style tool turns them wooden or rope or takes them off, and the colour tool paints stairs as
any build (see "Colour per build" and "Styles"). Stones
appear in order from the lower end. Delete takes a flight, ladder or landing whole;
undo, redo, autosave and joining a garden preserve it like other builds.

A flight or landing is walkable: its treads are what a point over it stands on,
and the teleport ray stops at its stones like any other solid, so a player lands on
the tread, the bridge's deck or a rung. Stairs stand where they were drawn and take
no part in wall arches; the builds whose tops their ends rest on are their supports (`on`),
and taking one away takes the stairs. A grip on any of their stones, whatever the hand holds, takes them as it takes a
boulder (`WallTool.carry_stairs`): they move with the hand, a far flight farther per metre, and turn about the vertical
through their middle as the hand turns, their lower end never under the ground; the other hand's grip on them too
widens or narrows them by the hands' span, from 0.6 to 3 m. Letting go sets them as one `reshape` of their ends and
width, one undo step; `tests/test_stairs.gd` carries, turns and widens one. `demo/stairs.json` shows a bridge over a pond between two walls, a flight up
to it, a ladder and a flight onto a flat roof, then teleports onto the bridge; the
stress scene fills the build budget with stairs, ladders and landings, beside a
stepped path up a hill, for its `stairs/*` views. On the Steam Frame, governed, both views
hold 72 Hz with no late frame: 8.9 ms of GPU time a frame from the air and 8.0 standing
(`perf/frame/stress`), the ladder settling two steps from the top.

## Bridges

The stairs tool hangs a bridge where a stroke spans a gap: its run at least `StairLayout.HANG_LEAST` (2 m), rising no
more than `HANG_SLOPE` of it, and its middle half either crossing water or standing `HANG_CLEAR` (1 m) over the ground
and any build under it, along its centre line and both edges, as between two wall tops, roofs, platforms or the banks
of a pond (`StairLayout.sag_over`). One drawn along a wall's walk or a floor is a landing on it. The tool works this
out from what is under the stroke as it is drawn, carried or widened, and the build keeps it as its `sag`: how far
the deck hangs below the line between its ends at the middle, 7 cm a metre of span (`SAG`) down to what keeps the
deck 15 cm over the ground, water or build beneath, no less than 3 cm and no more than 2 m (`Commands` refuses any
other). The top an end rests on, or a bank's lip up to 30 cm over the line, may stand into the deck's first and last
quarters, so a bridge from bank to bank across a pond lies almost flat just over the water. Anything else is a
flight, landing or ladder as before. Like the ends of any stairs, the sag stays as it was set when the ground or
water under a bridge changes later; carrying or widening it works it out again.

The deck follows a parabola through both ends (`StairLayout.deck`): planks 18 cm deep a plank every 22 cm, each tilted
to the hang and set a little askew, on two ropes running under their ends. Each end is tied to a post on either side.
The railings, from the style tool's railing wheel as on a flight:

- **Rope**: handrails strung along the hang, a hanger down to the deck rope every 60 cm or so, on wooden posts.
- **Stone**: the same rope handrails and hangers, tied to stone pillars at the ends instead of the posts.
- **Wood**: wooden posts and rails following the hang.
- **None**: the deck on its ropes.

At the least width (0.6 m) a bridge is a single rope down the middle, tied to a post at each end, with no planks or
railing; the style tool has no railings to offer it. Rope keeps its hemp whatever the colour tool paints, and the
colour tool pointed at it paints the bridge's woodwork: planks, posts and rails. A player stands on the deck where it
hangs (`StairLayout.surface`), or on the single rope, and the teleport lands on it, the gaps between planks included
(`StairLayout.solids`). A bridge is charged as a landing of its span and width, railings included.

A wall drawn across a pond arches over the water (see "Stone in ponds"): one arch over a narrow crossing, an arcade
on pillars standing in the pond over a wider one.

`tests/test_bridges.gd` checks where a stroke hangs, the sag and the planks along it, each railing style, the single
rope, the command's checks, the tool hanging one between two wall tops and laying a landing along the meadow and
along a wall's walk, the colour tool on a rope, the demo with its teleport and the stress phases. `demo/bridges.json` hangs a bridge in every railing style and a single rope between two
walls over a pond, one from bank to bank across another, and a wall across a third arching over the water, then
teleports onto a bridge. The stress scene's `bridges/aerial` and `bridges/ground` phases hang a bridge from bank to
bank across every pond in the meadow's ring, the railing styles in turn. On the Steam Frame, governed, both hold
72 Hz with no late frame ([record](../perf/frame/stress/20261025T165208Z-b4e2a39.json)): 3.5 ms of GPU time a frame
from the air and on the ground, at step 16, where the ladder came down at the village and stayed. The same phases
alone with the ladder pinned at its top step hold too, with no late frame, at 9.2 ms from the air and 9.0 on the ground.

## Standing on what you built

A player stands on what they build as on the ground: a wall's walk, a flat roof's deck, a platform stacked on it,
a flight's treads, a bridge's deck and a house's floor. Two things decide it.

- Where the teleport ray lands. A full stick lands where the ray stops on anything solid: the top of a wall,
  deck, tread or bridge, just as it lands on the ground. A ray that stops on a side instead, a surface steeper than 70 degrees such as a
  wall's or a house's face or a ruin stone's (`Rig.STEEP`), lands at its foot, `Rig.STAND_OFF` back along the ray,
  rather than standing the player in the air against it; struck more than `Rig.CLIMB` over that foot, the player
  floats there at the height struck.
- What a point stands on (`Rig.floor_under`): the highest top at or just below it, the ground, a house's floor,
  a flat roof's deck, a stair's tread, a boulder or a wall's walk (`WallLayout.walk_under`: the cap or coping
  along its centre line, or the step a ruin's end has come down to; never a fence's rail, or walls under a roof
  or deck). An eased target's height is counted from that top, not the ground under it, and its ring sits on it
  when it comes within `Rig.LIFTED`. A flat walker on the desktop walks along a wall walk instead of dropping
  through it.

`tests/test_teleport_solids.gd` lands the ray and hovers over each, and `tests/test_standing_demo.gd` plays the
tour. `demo/standing.json` renders that tour: down onto a railed wall walk, a bridge between two walls, a
hover over a flat roof then onto its deck, a platform stacked on it and a flight's tread, each seen from where the
player then stands. The stress scene draws a crenellated wall through the roofs village, from house corner to
house corner, raises its middle stretches to 4.5 m, and measures `standing/along` (down the street from the walk)
and `standing/across` (over the roofs) from a standing player's eyes on it. On the Steam Frame, governed, both views
hold 72 Hz (`perf/frame/stress`): 6.2 ms of GPU time a frame, and no late frame in the half second after the
player lands on the walk (the bench's land columns: worst frames 18.8 and 16.8 ms against the 20.8 ms line). Three
single frames of 21 to 22 ms fall three to five seconds after a landing, the GPU idle half the period, as in
most of the stress scene's phases.

## Timbered plaster houses

Directive (verbatim): "I like timbered on plaster houses in general, even the walls of plaster houses."

Rectangular plaster houses, and open walls, have dark oak sole plates, corner posts, opening studs,
head and sill rails, mid rails and braces. The wall's resolved openings drive the
frame; resizing moves the openings and regenerates the frame together. Gable
posts continue the wall's post positions, with rails and rakes following the roof.
Stone houses retain their existing masonry. Boarded gables retain their boards;
stepped plaster gables keep their crow steps above the framing.

Fachwerk crosses in both rows are the timber pattern. Timber is one of the face wheel's finishes (see "Wall
finishes"); the choice is retained through save, undo, visits and resize. Point the colour tool at a beam to choose its dark
oak colour independently of plaster, window frames and doors.

`demo/timber.json` captures the front, gable and lantern-lit dusk view.

A timber is its own masonry part. Near, it is a plain box: the lumpy stone mesh it used to borrow cost nine
times the triangles and read no different on a planed beam. Far, it is the coarse stone in `timber_far.gdshader`,
which reads its bounce and lantern light once a vertex and no detail texture, which a few pixels wide averages to
the beam's own colour. It casts its shadow with the stones. A roofed wall's top rail sits 4 cm under the eaves so
the box's square edge stays under the tiles. The captures match the earlier framing at all three views, near and
from 20 to 40 m.

Its cost was measured on Quest 2 B at the bottom quality step in the stress scene's densest village (21 two-storey
houses with wings, radius 20, 3,906 Alsace or 4,914 Fachwerk members), swapping the frame's drawing at runtime
between the earlier path, this one and none, three times over in alternating order, GPU ms per frame over none:

| | Alsace aerial | Alsace ground | Fachwerk aerial | Fachwerk ground |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| lumpy stone part (before) | +4.8 | +0.9 to +1.0 | +6.1 to +6.3 | +1.1 to +1.8 |
| box near, coarse stone far | +5.6 | +0.9 | +7.6 | 0.0 |
| box near, `timber_far` far | +3.5 to +3.8 | +0.4 | +4.8 to +5.6 | -0.4 to +0.1 |
| box near, nothing far | +0.1 | -0.3 | +0.8 | -0.7 |

Shadows, draw calls and triangles cost nothing measurable; turning the beams' shadows off left the totals within
0.5 ms. What remains is the far beams' pixels: a beam a few pixels wide is shaded once for itself and once for the
plaster under its edges. A flat-lit far beam still cost +2.2 to +2.6 ms, and drawing them first changed nothing,
so the floor for beams drawn as geometry is about 1 µs a member from above. Below it the far frame would have to
be painted into the plaster it lies on.

Since the Quest 2 draws 2x MSAA (above), the same village costs less, and painting the far frame into the wall was
tried and not kept. Six rounds of paired runtime swaps on Quest 2 B, from the air at the bottom step, GPU ms over no
timber (mean of the six, its standard error):

| | Alsace aerial | Fachwerk aerial |
|---|---|---|
| box near, `timber_far` far | +1.9 (0.6) | +2.2 (0.7) |
| far frame painted into the wall | +1.5 (0.9) | +1.5 (0.5) |

The no-timber baseline itself moved 3 ms between rounds. The painted frame cut the wall's plaster, mortar and the
stones under each member along the members' lines and drew those on their edges and diagonals in a far shader, and
the gables carried theirs as distances in their mesh; the near view and the frame's far look matched. It saved
0.4 to 0.7 ms, within a standard error, for some 800 lines: in this village most members cannot be painted, since a
lower storey's face stands behind the courses its upper storey lays down over it, and a joined wing's wall stands in
front of the house's, so those members stay boxes and keep most of the cost. The budget charges `Budget.TIMBER_US` a member, the
worst measured with the box path (2.2 ms over 4,914 Fachwerk members, 0.45 µs each), rounded up.


### Archway GPU cost on Quest 2

`demo/arch-cost.json` builds 36 timber houses with four doorway-sized arches each.
It alternates plain, arched, plain, arched, plain, looking from above and along the
street at each stage. Run it with `CAIRNFOLD_SCENE=demo/arch-cost.json
scripts/bench.sh OUT --android APK`, with `adb` addressing the Quest 2. For the
historical build, replace each `fit` opening step with its former `arch` operation,
retaining `index` and `s`; the houses, views, timing and ordering stay the same. Each phase settles before recording 25 seconds. The headset
runs at the Quest 2 fit step, 72 Hz, with its clocks held at level 4 by
`scripts/bench.sh`; the render target is 1008 × 1108 per eye. The first two samples
of each phase are discarded. An arched phase's median minus the mean of its two
neighboring plain medians, divided by 144, is the net GPU cost per placed arch.
This includes the wall pieces removed and added around each opening, visibility,
and ivy changes; it is not an isolated cost of the arch mesh or a cost per visible arch.

On `a3454786`, the aerial pairs add 22.6 and 21.5 µs per arch in the Quest runtime's
`App=` timer, and 26.4 and 24.8 µs in the viewport timer used by the governor.
The ground pairs add 16.1 and 3.3 µs (`App=`), with 16.2 and 3.7 µs on the viewport
timer. The ground view's spread is retained rather than averaged into a precise
price. The aerial plain phases range from 60.79 to 61.30 ms and the arched phases
from 63.99 to 64.30 ms: this unbounded dense village is a cost sweep, not evidence
that it holds refresh. The benchmark checks that the game finishes and that no
other process or tracked hand contaminates the labeled phases.

The same scene on `027eb120`, immediately before the timber arch implementation,
measures the former stone ring on those timber houses. Its aerial pairs add 9.1
and 10.8 µs per arch (`App=`), or 12.9 and 12.9 µs on the viewport timer; ground
pairs are -2.8 and -0.4 µs (`App=`), or -4.0 and +0.5 µs on the viewport timer.
Both builds use the same render size and quality ladder. Each number subtracts
that build's own plain baseline: other changes between the revisions affect
whole-frame cost, so their frame totals are not a timber-versus-stone comparison.

`demo/arch-sizes.json` repeats the experiment on nine 20 × 6 m houses with 9 m
walls and two openings each, at the same fit step on `a3454786`. It measures two
plain/arched pairs per size, with a final plain phase to bracket the last pair.
The table retains the range of the two paired results, in GPU µs per placed arch.
The widest ring nearly spans its 20 m wall; the clear opening spans are below.

| Material | Size | Clear span (m) | Runtime `App=` µs/arch | Viewport µs/arch |
| --- | ---: | ---: | ---: | ---: |
| Timber | 1 | 1.50 | -28.6 to -11.1 | -30.7 to -18.4 |
| Timber | 2.67 | 4.01 | +16.9 to +24.4 | +26.0 to +29.3 |
| Timber | 8.9 | 13.35 | +47.8 to +48.1 | +41.1 to +49.7 |
| Stone | 1 | 1.50 | +1.9 to +44.4 | -0.3 to +52.4 |
| Stone | 2.67 | 4.01 | -54.7 to +49.2 | -50.3 to +49.2 |
| Stone | 7.28 | 10.92 | -314.2 to -301.9 | -340.9 to -328.0 |

These are net scene differences, including the surrounding wall and ivy and
ambient motion. The small stone cases are too variable to support a precise
coefficient; their spread is part of the result. A negative result does not mean
an arch mesh renders for free: opening the wall changes the rest of the scene too.

On that build the existing timber charge grew by 1.008 ms for the 144 arches, or 7 µs each.
The aerial viewport differences exceed that charge. In the size sweep, the
largest timber opening reduces the existing member charge by 9 µs per arch while
adding up to 49.7 µs on the viewport timer: the residual is 58.7 µs. That is the
largest residual across both current-build sweeps. `Budget.ARCH_US` rounds it up
to the next ten microseconds and reserves it for each explicit arch separately
from timber members. This covers the observed pairs rather than fitting a size
curve to twelve noisy observations; a large opening keeps its arch allowance
even when it removes more wall than it adds. The Frame shares that
allowance until measured there. `demo/stress.json` includes stone and timber
arches at doorway, 4 m, and near-wall-spanning sizes. Its four-house arch section
measures 27.50 ms aerial and 15.84 ms ground in the runtime timer, also an
unbounded sweep rather than a refresh-rate pass.


## Wall finishes

A build's faces are one `WallLayout.Finish`, with `wear` for plaster: the face wheel of the style tool, which is the
colour tool's picker with styles (`PaletteTool.FACES`), offers each on a wall or house, and rails or planks on a fence.
Walls, houses and fences laid after take the finish let go last. The colour tool colours each as its material: stone,
plaster, the oak of a frame, and planks as woodwork.

- **Worn plaster** shows the stones behind it over a quarter, a half or three quarters of the face above its stone foot
  course, more toward the foot (each course's share is `wear` × (1.5 − its height over the wall's)), taken where a
  smooth noise over the face is lowest, so the patches carry on from course to course. Stones laid there stand back
  3 cm behind the plaster's face on mortar set back as far, and run 4 cm on under the plaster round each patch. The
  gable ends stay whole.
- **Half-timbering** frames plaster in oak on a house's outer faces, and on both faces of an open wall, straight or
  curved: there each member lies on the face through its own middle, and the rails break at every post. Its walk is
  boarded in three runs of planks under a wooden railing, where a player stands, and a ruin breaks the frame, walk and
  railing with the courses.
- **Planks** across lie in rows two to a course, staggered 0.9 to 1.8 m long; upright, side by side 0.2 m wide, each
  running up through every course it spans whole. Either is cut where the courses are, round windows, doors, arches,
  pillars and ruins, over a stone foot course, with oak heads, sills and casings at the openings and a dark backing
  behind the joints; a planked house's gable ends are boarded.
- **Fences** take planks across, seven between each pair of posts in the rails' place, or upright on the rails.

Planks, a gable's, the walk's and a fence's included, are plain boxes drawn as timbers are. Every straight piece laid
along a wall (a plank, the walk's, a frame's rail) is cut where the wall has turned `BOARD_TURN` along it
(`WallLayout.straight`), and an open wall's frame stands a post there, so none stands off a curved face. The budget
charges worn plaster as plaster plus the stone it shows as stone wall, a planked wall as plaster for its backing plus
each plank at `TIMBER_US` (a planked house's gable ends are boarded whatever they were turned to, and count as stone wall), a planked fence its
planks besides its fence, and a timber frame its members on each face and its walk's planks. Over the stress village
of the `village/*` phases (two storeys with wings), the budget charges plain plaster 19.70 ms, plaster worn by a
quarter, a half and three quarters 22.11, 24.52 and 26.93, timber 22.16, planks upright 23.27 and planks across 29.48:
the `finishes/aerial` and `finishes/ground` phases stand it in planks across, the dearest finish over its stone.
`tests/test_finishes.gd` checks every finish clear of every opening on a house and a bent wall, the wear, the planks,
the frame and walk, fences, the command, the budget, the wheel, `demo/finishes.json` and the stress village. On the
Steam Frame, governed, both `finishes/*` views hold 72 Hz (`perf/frame/stress`): 4.8 to 5.5 ms of GPU time a frame, no
frame over 1.5× the refresh period at the view's landing, and one in the phase, of the game-wide single late frames.

## Window families

A placed opening is its arc length, the height it was placed at, its `WallLayout.Family` and its size. Besides the
cottage window, a window is:

- **Arched**: a light 0.6 m wide and 1.2 m tall under a half-round head, a ring of wedges round the head standing
  3 cm proud of both faces, on a sill. Low on a wall it opens as a round-headed door, its planks each reaching up into
  the head.
- **An arrow slit**: a slot 16 cm wide and 1.2 m tall between dressed jambs of pale stone, long and short in turn,
  under a lintel and on a sill, its glass set deep toward the room.
- **Round**: a circle 72 cm across, ringed all round with wedges, crossed by bars.
- **A corner window**: a light on each face of a corner meeting at a stone post, a lintel and a sill on each face
  running over the corner, the corner's pier standing under and over it. The windows tool opens one where it points at
  a corner's pier, on a house, tower or a bent wall but never where a timber frame's corner post stands.

Families that open no door (slits, round and corner windows) open no lower than a door's sill, wherever the hand
points. Windows of one family on one course fuse as cottage windows do, a ringed light keeping its own ring and a
slit its own jambs, the lights a mullion apart: a course of stone between ringed lights and slits. Families never fuse
with each other, and a corner window with nothing. A ringed light cuts each course by the widest its light reaches
across the course, or the narrowest its ring does where that is wider, and the wall's infill behind the ring closes the
rest, as behind an arch. The style tool's window wheel (`PaletteTool.Restyle.WINDOW`) offers the families an opening
still opens in where it stands, a door staying a door.

**Trapdoors.** The windows tool on a flat roof lays a trapdoor on its deck (`RoofLayout.hatches`, placed as chimneys
are, kept inside the walls and clear of chimneys, a tower's flag and each other): a lid of boards in an oak curb, on two iron strap
hinges, with a ring. A press on one takes it away; a grip carries it over the deck.

**Glow.** A room's glass (a house's or tower's under a roof or deck, its dormers' too) shines at night in its build's
glow colour (`Palette.Element.GLOW`, eight swatches from candle amber to violet), picked by the colour tool on the
glass; a window in a wall standing open does not. `Daylight.lit` brings it on as the sun sets from 6° to −4° below
the horizon, and `Look` hands it to the stone shader as `window_night`, `Lamps.NIGHT_GLOW` strong, on top of the light
of lanterns inside (see "Lantern light"): it costs the shader one more multiply and add on glass alone.

**Lived-in houses.** A room with a door or an archway placed, or one standing on another build, is lived in, and its cottage
and arched windows grow curtains drawn to either side of each light under a pelmet, in front of the glass on the face
looking out, in one cloth colour per house; three in four of them a box of flowers under the sill; and one of them a
line on two iron arms over it, its washing waving as flags do.

The budget charges each arched or round window's ring as a fitting, each dressed window of a lived-in house as one and
its washing as one, and each trapdoor as one. `tests/test_windows.gd` checks each family's cut and fusing, doors low on
the wall, the corner window, the glow and its colour, the dressing, trapdoors, the command, the budget and the tools;
`demo/windows.json` renders every family by day and by night, the window wheel and the glow wheel; the stress scene's
`windows/aerial` and `windows/ground` phases look over a village opening windows in every house, flat roofs with
trapdoors, by night. On the Steam Frame, governed, both hold 72 Hz with every window glowing (`perf/frame/stress`): 5.2 ms
of GPU time a frame, no frame over 1.5× the refresh period at the ground view's landing, and one in the aerial phase, of
the game-wide single late frames.

## Moss

Moss shares the grass branch on the garden wheel. Hold the trigger to paint a low,
opaque carpet on the ground, including painted stone. Its twelve-triangle patches
sample the ground at each vertex, so they follow slopes and later sculpting. The
same `planted_water` / `water_depth_at` path as grass and flowers turns a submerged
patch into a thinner, darker algae film that sways gently along the pond bed.
No second water query is maintained on the CPU.

Point at a tree and press the moss trigger to coat its bark. The tree command
stores the coating alongside species and scale; its shader uses local bark UVs,
so the coat stays with the trunk through resizing and species changes. Undo,
redo and saved or shared gardens retain it. Ground moss uses the normal seeded
plant strokes, ghost pool and delete brush. Other raised surfaces are not coated.

The painted moss pool holds 256 patches (3,072 triangles), in one draw with no
shadows, plus a transient ghost pool capped at the same size while painting. Growth uses the
existing 1.5 ms work slice. `Budget` charges a patch its dearest measured Quest 2 cost, 7.001 µs (1.79 ms for the full
pool; "Cost by distance"). `demo/moss.json` shows painted stone,
coated and resized trunks, and algae below water. The flora stress phase fills
moss alongside the other plants. `tests/test_moss.gd` measures headless pool fill
cost and checks placement, budgets, tree resizing, undo and restore.


### Removing a ruined wall

Delete pointed at a fallen stone highlights its whole wall. Press and release
removes that wall in one command; one undo restores the exact ruin. Standing
stones still break away only along the swept stretch. Rubble made during a held
partial teardown does not turn that stroke into a whole-wall removal.
The shared building picker intersects a ruin's remaining masonry and rubble,
so an empty gap no longer behaves as a full-height wall. The fitting-delete
highlight also encloses rubble's owning wall. `demo/rubble.json` exercises the
picker across dense ruined walls; the same phases are in the stress scene.

`demo/ruins-budget.json` holds four 16 m walls, each broken into a ruin, to the build
limit and measures them from 5 m: 9.38 ms GPU without Delete and 8.52 and 8.53 ms with
its beam through a gap and on fallen stones. Seven such walls take 0.977 of the limit,
which refuses an eighth; ruining four of the seven measures 9.38 and 9.53 ms
(`perf/quest-2/ruins-budget/`). From that view, over the meadow's viewport time, four
standing walls add 2.31 ms and breaking them into ruins 1.86 ms more, while the limit
charges all four 1.75 ms: dear in a view they fill, yet a full limit of them still holds.

### Drawn wall curves

Releasing a drawn wall filters hand jitter and cuts corners within each smooth span,
then stores the resulting curve in the wall command. The existing pier bend selector
chooses the sharp vertices that remain fixed; endpoints and snapped junctions also
stay fixed. Wall consumers linearly sample those same stored points, so rebuilding,
saving or sharing a wall does not smooth it again. Paths retain their own interpolated
curve. `tests/test_wall_smoothing.gd` checks jitter, sharp corners, endpoints and the
longest stroke's command size.

Avatar choice and joining a garden turn on the style tool's dial (`Dial`), the one
component every twisted choice uses. The inventory wheel's people slot holds a hand
mirror and a gate; reaching on into either, as into a subtree, closes the wheel and
opens that dial over the controller while the wheel button stays held. The mirror's
dial holds the built-in figures, the loaded VRMs and, off Steam, the visitor's name,
starting on the one worn; the gate's holds your own cairn and the nearby gardens,
starting where you are, so letting go untwisted changes nothing. A garden this build
cannot join stands faint. Twisting shows the choice at the top larger and wears or
joins nothing until the release. The figures are the wheel's own icons, built ahead
while the wheel is closed (`Hand._prepare_icons`), so opening a dial builds none. In
flat mode hold Q or the left shoulder button, sweep with the mouse or right stick,
then move across to twist, and release (`tests/test_session_wheel.gd`,
`tests/test_flat.gd`, `tests/test_dial.gd`).

### Flat-roof ceilings

Flat stone decks carry exposed timber underneath: rafters span the shorter axis
at no more than 0.65 m spacing, with depth set by the span, and 24 cm boards run
across them. Both follow the deck's height function, including bowed decks;
round towers clip each strip to the wall's circle. The top deck, its standing
surface and furniture attachment remain the same.

Each roof draws its framing as one mesh surface, using the existing timber
shading and palette. Hidden top faces and internal segment ends are omitted.
The mesh disappears at the timber near-detail distance plus its bounding radius,
so an observer inside a large room does not lose the ceiling just because its
centre is far away. Joined ceilings yield equal-height overlaps to the older
building, as floors do. Higher joined rooms use the existing massing exclusions;
deck flags, boards and mortar crossing a joined building's centre line are cut
back to it, where its ceiling starts, including where bowed decks cross. Wall
panels hidden by their middle retain the portion above the lowest joining
ceiling at their drawn corners, so cutting the deck cannot expose a wall-corner
hole. Within overlapping domes, deck pieces are also cut where their undersides
would descend below the higher ceiling boards; the deck above those boards
keeps its overlap to seal the roof. Ceiling triangles are clipped at the same
ownership boundary, so a partial board or beam face stays instead of leaving
a triangular gap.
The budget conservatively charges each box segment as an existing timber member;
round roofs use their rectangular bound for that reservation.

`demo/roof-ceilings.json` views rectangular, bowed, round and merged ceilings from
inside and the merged deck from above; these cases also finish the stress scene.

A physical Quest 2 headless sample of a 6 × 4 m roof (30 measured builds after
five warmups) takes 11.60 ms median / 12.56 ms p95 for roof generation and mesh
construction, versus 0.42 / 0.49 ms with ceiling generation omitted. The added
ceiling has 1,812 triangles in one surface; the 59 deck tiles and 59 mortar
pieces are unchanged. These are construction times, not rendered frame times;
`perf/roof-ceilings/headless-quest-2.json` records the sample.

The paired physical XR fixture is recorded in `perf/roof-ceilings/render-quest-2.json`.
The interior holds the GPU budget in both runs. The dense scene exceeds it
before and after; the changed exterior also exceeds it. Ivy counts differ between
runs, so these totals do not isolate the ceiling's GPU cost or establish a
whole-scene budget pass.

The curved-join fix's physical Quest 2 stress run is recorded in
`perf/quest-2/stress/20261021T115148Z-cc6d453.json`. The overlapping bowed-deck
interior measures 9.94 ms GPU time against the then 12.5 ms budget, with zero late
frames; the existing ceiling interior measures 10.32 ms. The full benchmark
exits 1: 71 non-sweep phases exceed budget. This is not a whole-scene pass or a
paired attribution of those costs to the join change. Botq job 9186 carries the
remaining performance and stress-driver errors recorded in job 9165's artifacts.



### Skipping collapsed grass work

A grass clump beyond its individual distance limit is already collapsed to its root.
Its vertex shader now takes that path before computing water shading, bounce light,
wind and hand bending. Clumps still visible keep the same calculations, and the photo
layer still draws every clump in full.

On one physical Quest 2 at 72 Hz and GPU level 4, `demo/grass-collapse.json` holds
maximum quality in a dense hilly wall scene and measures grass on/off twice. In an
original/candidate/original sequence, grass's incremental GPU time was 6.67/7.09 ms,
5.29/5.36 ms, then 6.43/6.38 ms. The candidate saves 1.02–1.80 ms against those
controls. Whole-scene times vary between runs: the grass-hidden control moved from
25.51/25.55 ms to 24.38/24.27 ms and back to 25.51/25.49 ms, so that drift is not
credited to the shader. The records are in `perf/quest-2/grass-collapse/`.

This is a partial saving, not a frame-budget pass. The candidate's visible-grass
phases still take 29.67/29.63 ms against the then 12.5 ms budget. The full stress scene
and its validation remain unchanged.

### Dense-scene lighting remainder

`demo/dense-sun-shadows.json` repeats sunlight on/off/on three times in a dense
wall/hill view at full detail. It then compares quality steps 8 and 9, which differ
only in shadows, and separately toggles sunlight with shadows already disabled.
Run it with `CAIRNFOLD_SCENE=demo/dense-sun-shadows.json scripts/bench.sh OUT --android APK`.
These are diagnostic controls, not changes to the stress acceptance workload.

On one physical Quest 2 at 72 Hz, levels 4/4 and 1478/525 MHz, the bracketed GPU
costs on `21b182e4` were:

| Conditional comparison | Saving against before/after controls | GPU time with that work disabled |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Full detail, sunlight and shadows together | 5.16–5.39 ms | 25.25–25.38 ms |
| Lower detail, shadows alone | 1.36–1.64 ms | 22.40–22.48 ms |
| Lower detail, direct lighting with shadows already off | 3.16–3.61 ms | 18.97–19.21 ms |

The records are in `perf/quest-2/dense-sun-shadows/`. The fixture omits the earlier
pond and wildlife edits from the full stress tour. Its conditional differences
are not additive across comparisons or transferable to individual stress phases.
The entire time left with each control disabled remains unisolated; even the
lowest control exceeds the then 12.5 ms budget. This establishes costs to investigate, not a
quality-preserving optimization or a budget pass.

The unmodified stress run on the same revision, recorded in
`perf/quest-2/stress/20261021T122615Z-21b182e.json`, held 52 of 120 non-sweep phases;
68 failed, with `camera/two` at 38.67 ms (26.17 ms over the then 12.5 ms budget). The five sweep
phases are retained separately. All 69 previously failing phases were measured:
`hints/every` and `decorations/night` held, while the other 67 still failed;
`ruins/no-delete` also failed at 12.64 ms. The added held-object phases remain in
the count. Every full-stress excess is still unassigned: the diagnostic does not
explain those totals. The repeated sweep style-target misses and formatting error
persisted and remain a limitation on interpreting the sweep setup. The benchmark's
contamination and completion checks passed unchanged.

The stair stress fixture checks its own allowance directly. It must not leave the
garden's build limit enabled: later stress houses were otherwise rejected before
the foundation style rays ran. A regression follows the stair field with the
foundation house and its style ray, checking that the house exists and the ray
hits its face. Earlier foundation-sweep timings with style-target misses measured
an incomplete fixture, even though the benchmark's contamination checks passed.

`demo/stress-layer-costs.json` preserves the complete stress prefix through
`camera/two`, then brackets each diagnostic control twice at full detail. The
`shadows` control suppresses the main sun's shadows across quality steps and
restores each step's original setting; `masonry` hides the settled masonry node;
`camera_previews` pauses preview updates while retaining the displayed images.
These controls change only an explicitly requested diagnostic. Their differences
include interactions such as newly exposed geometry when an occluder is hidden.

On the physical Quest 2, the accumulated camera scene gave these bracketed
conditional differences (two on/off/on repetitions, milliseconds):

| Control disabled | Difference against on controls | Frame time with it disabled |
| --- | --- | --- |
| masonry | 14.89–16.10 | 20.84–20.94 |
| camera_previews | 5.97–7.08 | 30.05–30.90 |
| grass | 4.90–7.01 | 31.10–32.16 |
| sun | 4.75–5.45 | 30.39–30.91 |
| msaa | 1.14–2.57 | 35.49–37.07 |
| shadows | 1.01–2.45 | 35.61–36.65 |
| terrain | 0.67–2.25 | 35.83–37.45 |
| water | -0.96–0.58 | 37.55–38.01 |
| trees | -11.36–-9.40 | 46.54–48.27 |

The record is in `perf/quest-2/stress-layer-costs/`. All 1,460 labeled runtime
clock samples held levels 4/4 at 1478/525 MHz, and the contamination and completion
checks passed. Settled masonry has the largest measured net effect, followed by
camera previews, grass and sunlight. Shadow-only removal is smaller than removing
sunlight as a whole. Hiding trees makes the frame slower; exposing occluded work
is a hypothesis, not a separately isolated cause. Water has no consistent saving.

Each disabled-state total remains an explicit unassigned remainder. Even hiding
all settled masonry leaves 20.84–20.94 ms, 8.34–8.44 ms over the then 12.5 ms budget. These effects
include interactions and the camera previews' views, and cannot be summed into a
frame decomposition or transferred to every stress phase. They identify larger
costs to investigate; they do not justify hiding geometry, removing lighting or
lowering acceptance quality. No rendering optimization is claimed here.


### Carrying a trusted conure

Eight seconds of feeding from either of your hands earns a conure's trust for its visit.
With seeds or an empty hand, close the grip within 14 cm to cup it gently. Keep the
grip held to carry it; release it and it flies toward a perch, or away if none remains.
The bird settles into the cup and keeps its existing idle flits and eased gaze.
It never appears on the wheel or hotbar. Peers see the same plumage through the
shared held-item descriptor.

Touching its feathers to a canvas exchanges only the existing wet pigment and
water across a soft feathered oval. It adds no pigment, water or dry marks.
Smears use the normal recorded paint commands, including replay, undo and peers.

The Quest carry replay (`demo/conure-carry.json`) exercises feeding, both hands,
canvas contact and fourteen remote held birds through the shared renderer. At the
budget's fit step, its measured GPU times were 5.74 ms carrying one bird, 12.30 ms
smearing, 11.44 ms carrying two and 12.41 ms with fourteen remote birds beside them;
all passed `scripts/bench.sh`. The phases recorded 0, 22, 0 and 135 late frames.
Those 157 late frames remain unattributed; the GPU-budget pass is not evidence of
perfect frame pacing. The run is recorded in `perf/quest-2/conure-carry/`.

Those totals are mostly the garden around the bird. `demo/conure-cost.json` pairs each
view with the identical view, bird and cup hidden, alternating six pairs a phase, and
`scripts/paired.py RECORD` turns the bench record into the bird's own share. On a
Quest 2 one bird in view adds about 0.1 to 0.16 ms of GPU time, two draws and some
1.8k primitives; the carry phase's view never frames it, so it costs nothing there; at the
canvas its share is within the smear's noise; fourteen remote birds filling the view
add 1.74 ms, which takes that phase from 11.0 ms to over the then 12.5 ms budget in seven
of its twelve readings. The runs are recorded in `perf/quest-2/conure-cost/`.

Splitting the fourteen held birds on a Quest 2 put their cost in the vertex
animation: the birds drawn at 0.4 scale, the same primitives with a sliver of the fill,
cost what full-size ones did; without the cup they cost no less; static they added 0.23 ms
of 0.97 ms, and unlit with their animation 0.52 ms, so every vertex was paying for
the motion of its whole part. `Conure.pose` now works out each part's rigid turn once
a bird a frame, and the vertex shader applies one quaternion and offset per vertex,
keeping only the breathing scale, the wing's draw along the body in flight and the
feet tucking up. Paired on the same headset, the held birds went from 0.81 ± 0.03 ms
to 0.63 ± 0.02 ms and two carried birds from 0.13 to 0.07 ms, with every held reading
inside the budget before and after (worst 10.78 and 10.50 ms; that run's garden left
9.8 ms without them, against 11.0 ms in the first runs). The head's looking about
now draws its random snaps in double precision, so a given moment's head turn differs
from before while the motion is the same.

To fit the added birds, their small surfaces use fewer vertices and their ambient
lighting samples terrain height once per bird each frame. Feather variation uses
mesh colour, and canvas relief uses screen derivatives of its existing height
sample rather than eight neighbouring texture reads. Removing the supporting
palms did not improve the measured GPU total, so the cupped hold stays intact.

### A conure on your shoulder

Directive (verbatim): "I'd like it so that um once it Conure likes you it will follow you around." ... "let's try putting it out on the edge of your shoulder so you can to your side and see it there and grab it. Um, and then it, it goes away if you go underwater or if you flick it off. Like if you shoo it away"

Liking you is the trust above: eight seconds eating from your hands. A conure that trusts you,
perched or pecking within 12 m, now and then (`Birds.FOLLOWS`) flies to whichever of your
shoulders is free and nearer it, one bird to a shoulder, and rides there as you walk, teleport
and snap turn: it sits in the rig's space, eased only against head jitter. It stands out on the
shoulder's outer edge (`Torso.SHOULDERS`), facing as your body does. Your body (`Torso`) hangs
from the neck and turns only once your head has turned further from it than a neck turns, 70°,
so looking ahead the bird is out of view, and turning your head to it and looking down brings it
into view. It murmurs and grinds its beak, preens now and then, turns its head to you when you
look at it and otherwise looks where you look. Close a hand on it and it is carried like any
trusted conure. A hand brushed or flicked at it, within 30 cm and faster than 1.2 m/s (a reach to
take it is slower), or your head going under a pond's surface, sends it off to a perch a short
way off; it keeps away 20 to 45 s and then may come back while it still trusts you. A bird on
your shoulder has a perch, so it stays when every wall and tree is gone, and leaves the garden
only once sent off it. Peers see it standing on your avatar's shoulder; the avatar's body turns
the same way (see docs/multiplayer.md). It adds no draw: the bird was in the garden already, and
a peer's shoulder bird is one held conure, the cost measured above.
`demo/conure-shoulder.json` feeds one, lets it go and captures the view ahead and turned to it.


### Interior probe cost

The shared room bounds and opening spill keep the existing GPU lighting volumes and samples.
A matched Quest 2 pair on the bounded dense-building tour holds all 21 phases below the
11.11 ms GPU line: the median phase change is 0.00 ms. The largest increase is
plaster-village/aerial, 9.61 to 10.62 ms; timber-village/interior is 4.64 to 4.61 ms.
These are single-pair measurements, not a claim of zero cost. Total late frames across
labelled phases are 90 before and 51 after; the maximum reported phase p99 is 1045.51 ms
before and 94.16 ms after. Transient stalls remain, and this pair does not establish
their cause. The records are `perf/quest-2/interior-density/before.json` and `after.json`.
